The jnana path of liberation is the exalted one,
I argue that the karma path is all important.
If someone argues, karma is the important path,
I turn round and say that jnana is all important.
If one learned in Sanskrit comes to argue,
I speak of the exalted truths expounded in Tamil.
If pundits learned in Tamil similarly come,
I smatter a few slokas in Sanskrit.
Thus, confusing all, establishing nothing decisive,
Will this learning ever lead to mukti?

*	 Thāyumāṉavar, Siddhar Ghaṇam, v. 10.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 79

Oh! Thou, the Siddha Elite of Divine Light
That hath reached the Vedanta-Siddhanta accord 

high!
What Thāyumāṉavar derides here as ‘art’ is verily the 

“deceptive art (seppaḍi viddhai)” reflecting the very same 
thought bestowed by Bhagavan in this verse.

The jiva forgets, out of inadvertence, his real and 
Absolute Truth as the Non-dual, adjunctless Infinity that 
shines in the effulgence of Being. Unless the jiva asserts 
as the conceptual ‘I’ that is body-based, there is none who 
appears as the “other”. In trying to hood-wink the “others” 
(when in reality there is none ‘other’) he becomes the first 
victim of his deceptive art whether he succeeds or not in 
misleading the others as the very idea of ‘I’ has foretold 
his fall. Sincere and competent seekers, with due and deep 
reflection, must realize this truth and bearing this in mind 
forever, without a moment’s lapse, must practise sadhana in 
their lives, steering clear of such deceptive arts.

Kaṯ-ṯṟum aḍaṅgāril kal-lādārē uyndār
Paṯ-ṯṟu madap-pēyiṉ pāl-uyndār ‒ suṯ-ṯṟu-pala
Chindai-vāy nōy-uyndār chīr-tēḍi ōḍal-uyndār
Uyndadu oṉḏṟu-aṉḏṟeṉḏṟu uṇar.*
The unlettered are easier saved than those who are 
widely learned but are not subdued; for the unlettered 
are saved from the clutches of the wicked demon of 
the pride of attachment to religion; saved from the 
twin diseases of whirling mind and speech; saved 
from wandering all over, seeking with longing, fame 

*	 Sri Bhagavan, Supplementary Verses to the Verses on Reality, 
v. 36.



80 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

and wealth commensurate with their knowledge. 
They are saved from not one but many ills.
“The art of perfection or of Self-Realization (uruppaḍu 

viddhai)”, is the pursuit of art, where Arunachala, the 
resplendent light of Pure Awareness, surges forth as 
mentioned in the earlier verse; in other words the aparoksha 
anubhuti (the experience of being the Self) dawns.

Ūṉattai nīkkum uḍalē pōṯṯṟi*
“Lord who has a holy form which removes defects.”
What the word “body (uḍal)” in this verse means is the 

same as what is meant by ‘uru’ here.
While Bhagavan prayed to Arunachala “to destroy the 

dense darkness of ignorance (kīzhmaiyaip pāzh cey)” in 
an earlier verse (in a negative strain), here he prayerfully 
pleads, “grant me graciously the ‘sadvidya’ or the Supreme 
Knowledge of the Self” (uruppaḍu viddhai)” in a positive 
strain.
A.I.F.A :

Arunachala! Graciously reveal unto me the way so that I 
may become conversant with Brahma Vidya, or the science 
of the knowledge of the Self, extolled by all the sacred 
Texts as the bedrock of all knowledge, and delivered from 
the delusion of the world, may obtain the experience of the 
Self. The path that leads to the dawn of enlightenment or 
true knowledge, is the only way to jettison the erroneous 
conception of the world being real and the consequent 
deception it ensnares one into.

*	 Appar Thēvāram, 055-Tirukkayilāyam, v. 4.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 81

“What is”, is the Self or the Atman. Deliverance from 
the perception of ‘What is not’, namely, the empirical 
universe jostling with names and forms, is the realization 
of the Self. 

Another reading ensues when this verse is viewed in a 
different light:

“Thou, excelling in the art of trickery, like an adroit 
magician, pray, do not hoodwink me anymore.”

34.	 Serā yeṉiṉ-mey nīrā yuru-hik-kaṇ 
		  Ṇīrāṯ ṯṟazhi-vēṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
If Thou dost not commingle with me, my physical 

frame will melt away freely as water and the tears 
swelling up into rivers (and stricken with that intense 
longing), I, plunged in the ocean of worry and sorrow, 
will be drowned, swept away and be lost.
Commentary: 

The lady-love (devotee) deems it a paramount end and 
aim in life – nay the very fulfilment of birth as a woman 
like her to search for and select an ideal and perfect spouse 
of her soul, a soulmate – bridegroom – like Arunachala, 
befitting her in everyway, unite with Him in wedlock and 
live in unison for ever. That this is the content, is implied in 
“If Thou dost not Unite with me (sērāy eṉiṉ)”. If He does not 
unite thus, the principal purpose of human life – achieving 
the salvation of her soul – being lost out, she, though born, 
is yet unborn and her life is unlived. 



82 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Hence out of the enormity and intensity of her boundless 
love and longing for Arunachala, her body un-embraced by 
Him, melts away into water as if each pore has turned into an 
eye. The eyes that have not beholden His Form of effulgent 
brilliance (jyoti uru)* shed constant tears that swell into 
rivers. Pining sore and stricken with grief that has been the 
undoing of her physical frame, she, bemoaning her wasted 
life, says “my body will melt away in tears with unbearable 
grief, I’ll perish in tears swelling into rivers (mey nīrāy 
uruhik kaṇṇīrāl azhivēṉ)”.

Pūrāya māmēloṉḏṟu aṟiyā vaṇ-ṇam
Puṇ-ṇāḷar pōl-neñjam pulambi uḷḷē
Nīrāḷa-māy uru-hik kaṇ-ṇīr cōra
Neṭṭu-yirttu meym-maṟandōr nilai-yāy niṟpēṉ. †
Letting myself not investigate
And knowing further,
Like the wounded at heart
I wept in silence,
Shedding tears in profusion.
Sighing and sobbing
So did I stand transfixed.
This pen-picture drawn in the light of bridal imagery 

is of a lady pining away for her Lord and accordingly the 
import is explained. This applies equally to other such 
verses, that have come earlier to – and follow – this verse in 
this Text.

*	 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai, v. 32.
†	 Thāyumāṉavar, 14-Ākārabhuvaṉam – Chidambara Rahasyam, 
v. 24.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 83

A.I.F.A.:
“The heart melting away and the whole frame reduced 

to rivers of tears, I shall be drowned and lost in that flood 
of tears” is the meaning of “mey nīrāy uruhik kaṇṇīrāl 
azhivēṉ.”

Peria Puranam speaks of this sentiment as follows: 
“Torrents of floods of Bliss from the eyes overflowing and 
raining all over the body (kaṇṇil āṉanda veḷḷam meyyelām 
pozhiya)”.

“Arunachala, If Thou dost not unite with me, the pangs 
of separation and longing will make my frame melt away 
in tears and in that river flooded with tears lo! I shall be 
drowned and lost.”

Hence the plea is “Pray, guard me so that I do not 
end up in such a watery grave.” This is according to the 
amatory content of the poem. The heart of the lady-love 
due to prolonged separation from her beloved and ceaseless 
thinking of him apprehends his form in jagrat as in a dream 
and says so. The fruit of severe penance undertaken by chaste 
women like her is the cherished fortune of being wooed by 
their beloved on their own and embraced in conjugal bliss. 
Hence she says “If Thou dost not join me (sērāy eṉiṉ)” “the 
body melting away in tears, and losing its form be lost (nīrāy 
uruhi)” “and in the floods of tears so collected I’ll fall and be 
drowned (kaṇṇīrāl azhivēṉ)”.



84 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

35.	 Chai-yeṉat taḷḷiṟ sey-viṉai suḍu-malāl
		  Uy-vahai yē-durai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
If Thou dost not unite with me, but wert to reject me 

with disdain saying ‘tut’, the evil deeds (of bygone lives) 
that just await an opportunity, will catch up with and roast 
me alive as fire. Is there a way out for the redemption of 
me? Graciously bestow Thy saving Grace and show me 
the way out.
Commentary:

Viḷak-kaṯ-ṯṟam pārk-kum iruḷē-pōl koṇkaṉ
Muyak-kaṯ-ṯṟam pārk-kum pasappu.*
“Even as darkness lieth in wait for the light to be snuf-
fed out even so doth pallor lie in wait for my separation 
from the Lord” is worth pondering over here.
“Chai” is a term of derision. 
“The fruit of prarabdha – the assignment for expiation 

during the current birth – is the meaning of the term 
‘seyviṉai’. “It will burn me up (suḍum)” implies it being 
other than good, namely, evil or despicable deeds.

Rare in the world are men totally fault-free and 
virtuous. The ageless proverb “You pick faults at the cost of 
relationship”, underscores this truth. Following the footsteps 
of Maṇivāchakar when he sings,

Kuṉḏṟē aṉaiya kuṯ-ṯṟaṅgaḷ guṇamām eṉḏṟē nī-koṇḍāl
Eṉ-tāṉ keṭ-ṭadu iraṅgiḏāy eṇ-tōḷ muk-kaṇ emmāṉē.†

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 1186.
†	 Tiruvāchakam, Kuzhaitta Pattu, v. 3



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 85

What harm will ensue if Thou deemest hill-like 
flaws as virtues?

Pity me,
O eight-armed and triple-eyed Lord !
This verse too seeks from the Lord the forbearance of 

shortfalls.
But for the Divine intervention of the Grace of the 

Lord, the devout have no hope left for redemption, is the 
drift.

The lady love entreated Arunachala earlier: “Bestow 
on me with compassion Thy saving Grace by being united 
with me as one non-dually (aruḷ kūrndu eṉaic cērndu aruḷ).” 
Appalled, anguished and sorrowful at the prospect of the 
pitiable state she would be in, if He wert to turn down her 
plea to extend His saving Grace and protect her, she brings 
to His notice – to strike a sympathetic cord in His Heart 
– her plight with the impending peril of having to perish 
with no other go. This and the previous verse contain this 
poignant appeal of a helpless soul stricken with distress at 
the prospect of the failure to be espoused by the Divine.

36.	 Sollādu soli-nī sol-laṟa nil-leṉḏṟu 
		  Chum-mā virun-dāy Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
“Stay silent sans speech,” so didst Thou speak through 

the language of Silence indicating by Thy mystic pose 
or mudra (and in tune with that) Thou too lapsed into 
quiescence bereft of breath and thought. 



86 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Commentary: 
Bhagavan reminisces here the upadesa that Arunachala 

bestowed on him in response to his prayer in the earlier 
verse, “What is the way of redemption, please tell me, (uy 
vahai ēdurai)?”

This, indeed, is the upadesa that Arunachala, the Ruler 
and the Lord, dwelling within as the Awareness of awareness 
intuits and enlightens the ripe ones exceedingly competent 
and rich in grace, like Bhagavan. Lord Dakshinamurthy 
– wisdom embodied – teaching the Bliss of Silence had 
instructed His foremost disciples like Sanaka and others 
in the same fashion in days of yore. There is no better 
medium, more eminently suited, than silence, the divine 
or transcendent speech to know and let know the Absolute 
Truth, subtler than subtlety, immeasurable by the mind and 
indescribable by speech. Hence instructing “stay still sans 
speech (sollaṟa nilleṉḏṟu)”, in unspoken words, Arunachala, 
Himself the Form of Lord Dakshinamurthy, lapsed into 
stillness, the perennial Bliss of Self-Awareness.

The intent and purpose of the Lord remaining in the 
repose of quiescence is just to instruct the disciple to follow 
suit and abide like Him in the “Natural state” and to elevate 
him to that very state. Were the Guru to say verbally, “Be 
Still”, he would be transgressing his own upadesa and there 
will be total contradiction between his instruction and action. 
That a disciple endowed with discrimination, without being 
instructed in so many words, may stand instructed by mere 
observance of the guru’s deeds and follow suit, He lapsed 
into total and ecstatic silence.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 87

A Master, truly realized, by abiding spontaneously 
still in his Natural State, will inspire the knowledge 
of Sivajñāna by vanquishing the play of their ego-
consciousness, the sole obstacle to True Awareness, to all 
the devout who have sought, thronged and reached his 
immediacy with deep love in their heart.

37.	 Sōm-biyāy chum-mā sukha-muṇ ḍuṟaṅ-giḍil
		  Solvē ṟeṉ-gati Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Bereft of the least action of the triple organs (body, 

speech and mind) urged by an assertive ego, totally 
indifferent to matters external, simply being still and in 
total peace, revelling in the Bliss of Being the Self that 
springs up in the Heart as its inherent nature and abiding 
there in the state of “conscious sleep” (that being the 
Supreme Abode), if there be a state different than this, 
graciously inform me.
Commentary:

Sōmbar irup-padu suddha veḷiyilē
Sōmbar kiḍap-padu suddha veḷiyilē
Sōmbar uṇarvu surudi muḍindiḍam
Sōmbar kaṇḍār-ac surudik-kaṇ tūk-kamē.*
In space pure is tranquillity seated
In space pure It does repose,
Tranquillity begins where Vedas end;
There in self-realization they quietly abide.

*	 Tirumandiram, 1st Tantiram, 4-Upadēsam, v. 16.



88 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Thūṅgik kaṇḍār Siva-lōhamum tam-uḷē
Thūṅgik kaṇḍār Siva-yōhamum tam-uḷē
Thūṅgik kaṇḍār Siva-bhōgamum tam-uḷē
Thūṅgik kaṇḍār nilai solvadu evvāṟē.*
Stilling the mind they saw Siva’s World,
Stilling the mind they saw Siva’s Yoga,
Stilling the mind they saw Siva’s Bhoga,
How then describe the still minds of those who saw?

Soṉmā ṉada-daṉu-viṉ kaṉmā disiṟi-diṉḏṟic
Chummā amarn-dirukka ammā ahattil-āṉma 
Jõtiyē; nidāṉu bhūtiyē; irādu bhīdiyē;
Iṉba-vam bhōdiyē.†
If one abides in stillness in the total absence of any 
activity of speech, mind and body ah! me! What 
a wonder! The Self shines in the Heart in all its 
effulgence. Eternal is the experience of this Atman; 
total is freedom from fear; Infinite is the ocean of 
Bliss.
“The Bliss that shines in Being Still” is arrived at by 

supplying in two words “Shines (tulaṅgugiṉḏṟa)” and “Being 
(irukka)”. They, though not explicitly spelt out in the verse 
are implied. ‘uṇḍu’ means ‘feasting’ or ‘experiencing’. 

Fear arises out of the ‘second’ or the ‘other’. This fear 
triggered out of a ‘second’ in the empirical life occurs only 
in the states of wakefulness and dream called “Sakalam” 
where awareness is disparate and with parts. But in the state 
of simple sushupti or dreamless sleep called “Kevalam” 

*	 Tirumandiram, 1st Tantiram, 4-Upadēsam, v. 17.
†	 Sri Bhagavan, Ātma Vidyā Kīrttaṉam, v. 4.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 89

where awareness is whole and all by itself, none of them 
namely, the duality, fear or samsara is present. The state 
of wakeful sleep, sleeping without sleeping, or sleeping 
with awareness unspoilt, is the perfect state called ‘Turiya’, 
the Pure One. This state of ‘Turiya’ is true and laudable 
Awareness, the Grand sleep that knows no waking or is 
Eternal Wakefulness untouched by slippery sleep. This is 
all transcending, non-dual, state of refuge from fear, and the 
sovereign state of Emancipation or Kaivalya. The phrase “in 
the state of deep sleep with awareness (uṟaṅgiḍil)” implies 
this.

“Devoid of activity of the antahkaranas, remaining 
still and silent in the state of objectless Being (sōmbiyāy 
chummā sukham)”: The Bliss implied here is characterized 
by the jettisoning intoto of all endeavours including – most 
importantly – the sense of doership, the basis of all activities 
– and marked by the desirelessness of any special effort 
to attain the Self. This is Awareness that is objectless and 
immutable in nature present and shining everywhere and 
remains eternally as the Self.
A.I.F.A:

“If I, seeking the pleasures of the senses, simply eat, 
sleep and waste time in indolence (except for Thy Grace), 
what will be my fate? Graciously do deign to enlighten me.”



90 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

38.	 Sauri-yaṅ kāṭ-ṭiṉai chazhak-kaṯ-ṯṟa deṉ-ḏṟē 
		  Sali-yā dirun-dāy Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Arunachala, the might of jnana beyond compare! The 

moment Thou revealed Thy valour of Grace, the darkness 
of my nescience was driven out of existence. Then, Thou, 
resuming Thy state of repose, remained motionless.
Commentary: 

Prowess (sauriyam) is a derivative of Suran, one of the 
many names of the Sun God.

While Arunachala displays His prowess in freeing His 
devotees from the illusion of the vaulting ego, He appears 
to His devotees as if He is in motion. But once His job of 
the extirpation of the ego is accomplished, He comes to 
be realized by His devotees as “motionless” forever. Even 
though He executes His act of dispensing Grace with 
consummate skill and finesse, in reality, as if He has done 
nothing He “remained Still (irundāy)”.

Arunachala, who as a magnetic Mountain, never stirring 
from His state of reposeful motionlessness, with no resolve 
whatsoever on His part, merely by the power of His Presence, 
very much like the sun, wields His office of bestowal of 
Grace to perfection and wipes out totally the nescience of 
His devotees, is indeed the Lord of strange but wondrous 
and exceeding might, indescribable and beyond compare.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 91

39.	 Jñama-liyiṟ kēḍā nāṉeṉ ṉuṟu-diyā
		  Nāḍi-niṉ ṉuṟu-vēṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Worse than a cur am I in intelligence, with what 

strength other than the support and succour of the power 
of Thy Grace, can I seek and reach Thee?
Commentary: 

“Am I inferior to a cur? No, not at all; I shall by the 
resolute strength (of my stern determination to reach Thee 
positively somehow, or by all means) seek and obtain 
Thee”, can also be proffered as one import. It is not a whit 
surprising that the courage and confidence born of the fact 
of his having already been claimed and swept into His Grace 
by Arunachala, gives Bhagavan the liberty to flaunt such a 
bravado.

Since the Self can be realized only by one of solid 
dispassion and intense sraddha, Yoga Vasishtam emphasizes 
time and again the importance of spiritual endeavour with 
these two as the foremost components. Yet another school 
of learned elders pronounces emphatically that He can be 
obtained solely guided by the succour of the Divine. Though 
this view appears to strike a seemingly contradictory note 
from a cursory angle on the method of operation of Benign 
Grace of the Divine, they not only do not run counter to each 
other but that one view is very much the same as the other 
can easily be discerned by men given to deep reflection and 
earnest study.

In the heart where serene peace reigns supreme through 
formidable dispassion towards externals and which is 
rendered immaculately Pure by unalloyed satva (divine 



92 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

quality), the sheer power of the Atman springs forth as 
Arunachala Siva (God-centric). This is certain. But mere 
human strength that asserts as the ‘ego’ (ego-centric) due to 
the predominance of rajas (activity oriented mind) cannot 
accomplish it. “By the resolute strength of mine as ‘I’ (eṉ 
uṟudiyāl)” can be taken in the sense of “by the strength of 
enquiry – ‘Who am I’ aimed at realizing my true ‘Nature 
and Being’.” The primal thought ‘I’ is a ray of Arunachala, 
the Sun of Wisdom, the Self. A dog parted from the master 
and strayed far away retraces its way back led by the scent 
and ultimately reaches the refuge of its master’s feet again 
from where it got separated. In the same way searching and 
seeking within by the enquiry, Who am I? What is my nature 
and form? Whence am I? Which way did I come forth? and 
thus following the way the ray ‘I’ came out retracing it to 
its source within, and finally I shall positively regain my 
real Form – Thy Feet. This is an exposition on the path of 
enquiry, a very special and rare privilege and a gift of Grace 
that Arunachala bestowed on Bhagavan as his birthright.
A.I.F.A:

“Arunachala! Lowlier than even a cur, with what 
resolute strength will I acquire the skill to seek and search 
Thee within?”

His Grace divine does not well up unless the ego is 
rooted out wholesale. Hence “lowlier than a cur that I am 
(jñamaliyiṟ kēḍā)”. However mighty of mental strength 
informed men may be, He is difficult to be obtained. 
Hence “By what resolute strength (eṉ uṟudiyāl?)”. When 
an attempt was made to disrobe Panchali by the depraved 
Kauravas in the Royal Court, she, fully realizing her state of 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 93

total helplessness with none to provide succour and no self 
endeavour, threw up both her hands calling out for the Lord. 
Only then Lord Krishna came to her rescue and bestowed 
grace. 

But for the power of His Grace He is not attained. So 
the best way is to surrender the self unconditionally.

Another interpretation would be: Even if I am lowlier 
than a dog, by the strength of my devotion, I shall somehow 
reach Thee!

Niṉ-uḷḷattu aruḷ koṇḍu-iruḷ nīṅgu-dal
Eṉ-uḷḷattu uḷadu endai pirā-ṉirē.*
Sire mine! By the Grace in Thy Heart do I cherish 
ever in my heart the dispelling of darkness.

40.	 Jñā-ṉamil lā-duṉ āsai-yāl taḷar-vaṟa 
		  Jñāṉam terit-taruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Possession of a mere desire, though intense, to attain 

Thee unaccompanied by the acquisition of the requisite 
knowledge has only worn me out. Graciously rid me 
of this lassitude by bestowing on me the knowledge of 
enquiry.
Commentary: 

The term “weariness (taḷarvu)” being a suffix to both 
“desire (āsai)” and “devoid of knowledge (jñāṉam illādu)”, 
the content would be that worry and weariness has been 

*	 Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai, 096-Podu, v. 1.



94 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

brought on both by the desire for Thee and the lack of 
knowledge.

Jnana or Knowledge is, indeed, the sole, direct means 
or the road royale to know and attain the Lord, the very form 
of jnana. The absence of it is connoted by the term “not 
endowed with knowledge adequate to gain Thee (jñāṉam 
illādu)”. Until such knowledge becomes one’s possession, to 
know and obtain the Lord through experience, is well nigh 
impossible. In the absence of such jnana, the mere yearning 
unabated has landed him in languor and listlessness. Hence 
the statement “the unquenchable thirst to attain Thee” is 
the cause of “my weariness (uṉ āsaiyāl taḷarvu)”. If that 
fatigue has to be got over, “that jnana”, the tool of intuitive 
knowledge that will ferry him to His Feet revealing the 
knowledge of Him through experience, must be forged 
graciously by the Lord Himself. This entreaty is expressed in 
the line “that my weariness may disappear, graciously grant 
me the wisdom of enquiry (taḷarvaṟa jñāṉam terittaruḷ)”. 
Bhagavan follows suit with further pleadings in verse 60 
and 43 of this Text. “Arunachala! Having aroused the love 
for Thee in me who was without this yearning, please do 
not deceive me (nēsamil eṉakku uṉ āsaiyaik kāṭṭi nī mōsam 
seyādu aruḷ)” (verse 60) and “Oh Arunachala! Show Thou 
Thyself this truth that Thou art the Self of all (“tāṉē tāṉē 
tattuvam idaṉait tāṉē kāṭṭuvāy)” (verse 43).

Why is it that knowledge (jnanam) is spoken of as the 
direct, royal road? Cannot the Lord be beheld and attained 
by the route of Bhakti? 

The vision of the Lord that He bestows on the devout is 
as per their desires and in the forms they have conceptualized 
Him:



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 95

Eppeyar-iṭṭu ev-vuruvil ēttiṉumār pēr-uruvil
Apporu-ḷaik kāṇ-vazhiya dāyiṉu-mam ‒ meyp-poruḷiṉ
Uṇmaiyil-tan uṇmai-yiṉai ōrndo-ḍuṅgi oṉḏṟu-dalē 
Uṇmaiyil kāṇal uṇar.*
Under whatever name or form we worship Him, He 
leads us to the knowledge of the nameless, formless 
Absolute. Yet to see one’s true Self in the Absolute, 
to merge into It and be one with It, this is real 
knowledge of the Truth.
This may just be a way to the true vision of the Lord. That 

is certainly not the destination, His True Form. It is merely 
the mentally conceptualized form of the perceiver. That 
vision, not being permanent, only prolongs the weariness. 
On the contrary, the vision of the Lord as He is, in reality, 
gained through jnana, by the annihilation of the ego, a 
spurious entity of the perceiver, shines as his own Self, the 
substratum for the super imposition of the ego and never 
leaves him. That abides eternally in him and shines in all the 
Effulgence of Being. There is no room for weariness here 
for it is extirpated totally by the overwhelming power of the 
direct experience of the Self, the form of Pure jnana, that 
is ever attained. This truth is well asserted by the following 
two psalms of Saint Appar:

Jñāṉat-tāl tozhu-vār sila jñāṉigaḷ
Jñāṉat-tāl tozhu-vēṉ uṉai nāṉalēṉ
Jñāṉat-tāl tozhu-vārgaḷ tozhak-kaṇḍu
Jñāṉat-tāy uṉai nāṉun tozhuvaṉē.†

*	 Sri Bhagavan, Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu, 8.
†	 Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai, 091-Podu, v. 3.



96 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

O Siva! The Embodiment of jnana!
The Path of Knowledge ripe souls worship Thee through
The Path of Knowledge, gifted am I not to follow
The Path of Knowledge espying Thee worshipped 

by them
So shall I too worship Thee.

Muṉ-ṉai jñāṉa mudal-taṉi vit-tiṉaip
Piṉ-ṉai jñāṉap piṟaṅgu saḍai-yaṉai
Eṉ-ṉai jñāṉat tiruḷ-aṟuttu āṇḍavaṉ
Taṉ-ṉai jñāṉat taḷaiyiṭṭu vaippaṉē.*
Siva of lustrous matted locks!
The seed sole primal and ancient of jnana,
Shining as all jnana ever to come thereon,
My wisdom – veiling darkness dispelling He 

enslaved me
And I, chaining Him with wisdom shall seat ev’er	
	 in my Heart.
If that be so, what exactly is the gain of one wedded to 

the path of devotion? The very knowledge that dawns by the 
Grace of the Lord is the fruit, indeed, of devotion. If queried, 
is not the fruit of devotion, Supreme devotion (parabhakti) 
– to be tuned to His will and abiding in His service? Yes it 
is so. But the individuality of the ego as ‘I’ and the desire 
to attain the Lord in the dual plane as if He is ‘the other’ of 
one – a desire springing from the assumed individuality of 
the ego – and the listlessness that flows from such a desire 
are all rendered naught due to the loss of ego. What ensues 
is the unfoldment of Parabhakti as surrender of the restless 

*	 Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai, 091-Podhu, v. 2.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 97

ego is the sole mark of supreme Bhakti. And the reign of 
blissful Peace endures following the end of the aggressive 
ego. The Perfect Peace that prevails as one’s Form, by its 
very nature merges and abides in the Lord. Thus the way 
of knowledge and the way of love are closely interwoven 
and are not different from each other. For that which has 
gained identity and oneness with the other in terms of real 
experience, the disparity in name is due to nature of the path 
chosen by one to march on. This may also be due to the 
apparent differences of duality due to the association with 
adjuncts, purely in the perception of others. Hence by the 
experience of being the Self in the Sat Chit Ananda, the 
natural blossoming of pure, flawless, Supreme Love that 
ever abides spontaneously in one, the two are the same in 
content but differ only in name.
A.I.F.A.:

Arunachala! Thou Thyself graciously instruct me on 
the knowledge by which I can know and attain Thee. And 
rid me of the weariness caused by the failure of the desire 
unaccompanied by the knowledge of knowing Thee to 
attain its culmination. As ignorance or lack of knowledge 
is the sole cause of desire Bhagavan states, “not endowed 
with adequate knowledge to obtain Thee (Jñāṉamillādu uṉ 
āsaiyāl)”.

A deisre, be it even with the Lord, is but grief and 
hence he remarks “weariness due to unquenchable thirst to 
attain Thee.” Stirring of the mind triggers misery. But for 
the knowledge (of God) obtained in the stillness of mind 
relief from misery and the attainment of spiritual Bliss that 
is natural to one is not feasible. Hence the plea “graciously 



98 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

grant me the wisdom of enquiry, that my weariness may 
be totally erased (taḷarvaṟa jñāṉam terittaruḷ)”. Perfect and 
serene tranquillity of a quiescent or stilled mind is jnana 
for true jnana is devoid of the functions of knowledge and 
ignorance.

41.	 Jñimi-ṟupōl ṉī-yum malarn-dilai yeṉ-ḏṟē 
			   Nēr-niṉ ḏṟaṉai-yeṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Arunachala, the Sun of Wisdom! Even Thou, merely 

like a tiny bee, standeth still before me mouthing a lame 
excuse: ‘You have not yet bloomed’ (for me to gain entry, 
bore a hollow and soak in). What a trick of magic is this?
Commentary: 

Like a flower, having just then passed the stage of bud 
and in the process of blossoming from a half-blown to a 
full-blown flower, a young lady just blossomed into a young 
maiden, having crossed the intermediate stage, has now 
become cultured, highly refined and matured in intelligence. 
And on beholding her Lord the love for Him is rekindled 
in her heart. She, instead of addressing Him directly, out 
of modesty, resorts to the ingenious way of laying bare her 
emotions indirectly comparing herself to a lotus bud ready 
to open, and her beloved to the Lord of the lotus, namely, 
the Sun, so that he can divine her heart’s intent driven by 
hints thrown. “To blossom (malardal)” applies both to a half 
blown lotus and a young virgin. The word ‘eṉḏṟu’ stands for 
the Sun.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 99

The Sun, the lord of the lotus, has the power of making 
the lotus unfold, bloom forth and soak in just by his mere 
presence. Unless the lotus blossoms by itself, the bee cannot 
gain entry and suck in the honey. Therefore if the bee were 
to complain “you have not yet blossomed” it is fair and 
in the fitness of things. But if the Sun were to so exclaim 
helplessly like a bee, it would be a travesty of facts – and 
not being true to nature – would sound hollow, bordering 
on artful dodging. Hence she complains “Thou standeth 
still as if to mock me! How strange (malarndilai eṉḏṟē nēr 
niṉḏṟaṉai eṉ).”

The maturing or ripening of the disciple in the mere 
presence of his Guru – who is verily Siva manifest – and 
the ripening of the jiva in the presence of Siva purged 
of ‘mala’ or defilement and disentanglement from 
the knot of the ego will be effortless and spontaneous 
and the snapping of the knot of the ego will happen 
automatically on its own. (The alchemy of the glance of 
Grace of the Guru and Lord Siva transmutes the jiva 
into Pure Awareness casting the dross of the self out).

42.	 Tattu-vam teriyā dat-taṉai yuṯ-ṯṟāy
		  Tat-tuvam idu-veṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
“Unaware of even the Truth that ‘That Art Thou’, 

you have gained the Self – non-different from the state 
of Brahmanhood. That this has already been gained by 
you is the conclusive Truth. Do reassure me with certitude 
stating so.”



100 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Commentary: 
Of the many myriad of jivas born on earth, just one 

may achieve abidance in that state. The spiritual journey 
of such people on the virtuous path is characterized by 
distaste for a samsaric life out of the merit accruing over 
many an earlier birth, a burning passion for deliverance, 
renouncing attachments in their entirety, undergoing the 
traditional way of schooling by residing with the preceptor 
(gurukula vasam), rendering services of all types to him 
to acquire at first the four-fold sadhanas with no shortfall. 
Then they, by the grace of the guru, initiated into the 
mantra of the ‘Mahavakya’, follow the strict observance 
and adherence to sravana (hearing), manana (reflection) 
and nidhidhyasana (meditation), for prolonged spells of 
time, after great difficulty step into a state of Samadhi and 
finally reaching the state of final Beatitude, abide as That. 
However Bhagavan, due to some specific mission divinely 
ordained – out of supreme compassion to ferry thousands 
of fortunate devotees to the shore of Release – graced the 
world in the human frame. He never heard from any one – 
much less was he instructed on  – the concealed content of 
the ‘mahavakya’ – “That Art Thou”. And with no external 
prop or guidance such as from the sastras or through a Guru 
and even without any special endeavour on his part – except 
the grace of Arunachala – Bhagavan was a born kritakritya 
(one who has accomplished all there is to). He was one who 
was endowed with the innate wealth of the knowledge of the 
Truth – tattva – and abidance in the natural state of being 
one with the Supreme Awareness. Even as a youth, at the 
very first stroke and in a flash, he got firmly anchored in 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 101

the state of Transcendent quiescence – a state conclusively 
established as the final state of Beatitude. Bhagavan is 
one who became a Brahmajnani (who has gained the 
knowledge of Brahman) without even being aware of the 
word “Brahman”. Falling in line with the hallowed tradition 
of “sishtachara” – the empirical tradition of adherence to 
customary code of conduct by ideal disciples – Bhagavan 
desires to have an assurance affirming his experience from 
his Master and Guru, Arunachala, who is not the other of 
his Atman, an earnest desire akin to that of Sage Viswamitra 
to be proclaimed a Brahmarishi by none other than Sage 
Vasishta – a Brahmajnani. The episode of ‘Suka’ detailed 
in Yoga Vasistha, exemplifying this, is worth being reflected 
upon here. In the line, “blissfully ignorant of the great 
mahavakya TATTUVAM (tattuvam teriyādu)” the term 
“tattuvam” is employed to mean both TATTUVAM (THAT 
ART THOU – the Maha Vakya) and “tattuvam” – “The 
Truth” of “That Art Thou”. In “the Truth is this – (tattuvam 
iduveṉ)”, Bhagavan employs the term “idu (this)” for it 
has been his most intimate and non-mediated self-evident 
experience (aparoksha).

“Arunachala, Thou art beyond the reach and knowledge 
of all tattuvas (all the 24 principles) and hath gained 
exaltation beyond compare. (Thou, whose exaltation is so 
high), Is this all the Truth of Thee?” is another interpretation 
proffered. The tattuvas include, most importantly, the three 
forms of light that illumine the gross world like the sun, 
moon and fire, the five instruments of knowledge of the jiva 
and the four subtle instruments within (the anthakaranas). 
They are mere instruments of perception and knowledge 



102 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

and though are like the revealing light are incapable of 
perceiving (knowing) Arunachala. They are all but the 
perceived and are ‘prakrita (of ordained nature)’ due to 
their being material, insentient and created. But Arunachala 
is the sole aprakrita Purusha – Being beyond nature and 
matter. While Arunachala remains the sole Supreme Self 
or the Ultimate Seer, all else is drishyam (seen), asat (non-
being) and jada (insentient). And as such in their nature 
of having to perceive Him in the dual plane, they are the 
‘other’ of Him – the non-dual Sat Chit Ananda. Arunachala 
remains ‘unmoving’ as the Sole perceiving Eye and ever 
shines as Being-Awareness yet perceiving them all. They, 
merely being the perceived (asat), ceasing to exist, He 
remains unperceived by them. True Awareness is the Infinite 
Eye that sees no ‘other’, no duality, no space, time, object, 
subject and burns all other things to its own fiery brightness. 
This was explained by Bhagavan in the earlier verse (15) 
“Who can ever know Thee? The Eye of the eye art Thou 
and without eyes Thou seest, O Arunachala! (kaṇṇukkuk 
kaṇṇāyk kaṇṇiṉḏṟik kāṇuṉaik kāṇuva devarpār)”.

The translated verses – works of Grace – bestowed by 
Bhagavan such as

“Who is God? (dēvaṉārār maṉam)”
‒ Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu, Supp. 6

“By what light do you see (oḷiyuṉakkedu)”
‒ Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu, Supp. 7

and 
“I am eternal consciousness dissociated from the mind 

and senses (Manas-satchu-vadi)”
‒ Hastāmalakam, v. 7

throw more light on this truth and make it clearer.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 103

If the first line “tattuvam teriyādu tattaṉai uṯṯṟāy” is 
taken to mean that Arunachala is beyond being known 
directly, not only by instruments of perception but also by 
the inner organs, the following line should have been “what 
principle is that? (tattuvam aduveṉ?)”. But Bhagavan says 
“What principle is this? (tattuvam iduveṉ?)” to mean that 
Arunachala, the non-dual Sat Chit Ananda Brahman, as His 
very Self that is non different from Arunachala Brahman by 
His Grace eternally abides and shines as Being Awareness 
unceasingly as “aham aham” in his heart. 

That “Thy Truth defies being comprehended by any” 
can also be proferred as a meaning for “ignorant of tattuva 
(tattuvam teriyādu)” but it gets covered in the meaning 
offered here.

43.	 Tāṉē tāṉē tattu-vam ida-ṉait 
		  Tāṉē kāṭ-ṭuvāy Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
The Self Itself is He, the content of the tattuva. 

Thou, who art indeed the content of that truth (tattuva) 
– the Self Thyself – (by the Self effulgence of Thy Form, 
Being-Awareness), reveal unto me this truth as my direct 
perception (as non-mediated experience).
Commentary: 

The term ‘tan’ refers to the Self that is Brahman as the 
form of Pure and Supreme Awareness that, abiding in the 
hearts of all, shines in all its Self effulgence of ‘Being’, 
forever unceasingly and in all the states of existence that are 
mere mental creations.



104 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Remaining the witness of the jiva in all the three states 
amongst which the jiva keeps perpetually hopping from one 
to the other and appearing in some as the form of illusory 
knowledge, mentally imagined, and disappearing in the 
other (sushupti), He is the Jiva of the jivas that appear and 
disappear.

The reiteration of ‘tāṉē’ twice over is to emphasise 
with certitude the clear truth thus sealing the identity of the 
jiva with the Supreme. The principle (the tattuvam) is True 
Being or the Pure Awareness. That, beyond being doubted 
by any, is the ever and daily experienced Self with the 
utmost, intimate, immediacy of the heart by all, simply as 
the awareness of “Being” or “Existence”. Hence it is pointed 
to as ‘this’ (idaṉai) to indicate its utmost nearness.

“Thou Thyself reveal (tāṉē kāṭṭuvāy)”: The jiva in the 
form of ego, not being the content of “That” (the Self-God), 
like the cool moon fading away out of existence in broad 
daylight, losing its borrowed ‘lustre’ is dissolved in the Divine 
white light of Supreme Awareness and absorbed within Him 
as His own Form, the Atman – the Sat Chit Ananda – and 
what shines ever is Arunachala, the sole, whole, Non-dual 
and Impartite, rich Content, all by Himself. This revelation 
is what Bhagavan prayerfully beseeches for, in this verse.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 105

44.	 Tirumbi aham-daṉai diṉa-mahak kaṇ-kāṇ
		  Ṭeri-yumeṉ ḏṟaṉai-yeṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou Graciously instructed me, “Turn the mind 

heartward, away from the objects and matters of the world 
with a stern dispassion. Pursue the enquiry ‘Who am I’ 
with inward perception and attention single-pointed. And 
ceaselessly scrutinize the self by the self and perceive. 
(The Self as the form of the Self-itself is the truth or 
tattuva – The Self will be known to the Self) It will be 
revealed.” What is this? (What am I to make of this?) 
Commentary: 

“To turn inward (aham thirumbal)” is to sunder the 
objectified or denotative knowledge of the external world. 
Here, the objectified knowledge to be sundered includes the 
misconceptions within as well. When the Self is seen by the 
inner Eye, veiling – the cause of non-perception of the Reality 
– is rent asunder and the perception of ‘That’ (the tattuva) 
or the Knowledge of the Self or Enlightenment or natural 
abidance in Sat, dawns. The object of enquiry indicated as 
the “self by the self” (taṉṉai) here denotes indeed the jiva, 
the form of the ego and not the Self, the form of Aham or ‘I’. 
How is it? The pain and misery of birth that flow from the 
nescience and the need for a means to secure its removal in 
the shape of vichara is only for the jiva bound and perplexed 
by illusion and not to the Atman, Beginningless, Eternal, 
Immaculate, Ever-Awake and Ever-Free. 

That Self or Atman, when the ego, the innate corruption 
of the Self that stands in the way of Atma Sakshatkara, is 



106 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

rendered extinct as the result of Atmavichara, remains and 
shines as the very form of Self of the jiva and is known and 
experienced as the ramification of serene peace – the Self. 
Hence never – and in no way – can It become an object 
of enquiry as a sadhana. Samsara, the grievous cycle of 
repeated embodiment and death, the effect of inadvertence, 
the primal veiling power – which itself is death – is non-
existent in the absolute sense. 

Quest or vichara, keen, methodical, ceaseless and 
persistent, is a sine-qua-non for one, until the ego-knot 
that lies at the root of all this is totally extirpated. 

Hence the exhortation “Turn back, and day in and 
day out, see the ‘I’ (diṉam ahak kaṇkāṇ)” was bestowed 
graciously by Arunachala.

The mind, reined in from rushing out after external 
objects and made to abide at its source in the Heart is, 
“inward turned (or aha mukham)”.
A.I.F.A.:

Bhagavan reveals through this verse the instruction or 
upadesa of the ‘Tattva’ (the Truth) that Arunachala bestowed 
on him.

45.	 Dhī-ramil ahat-til tēḍi-yun taṉai-yāṉ 
		  Tirumba vuṯ-ṯṟēṉ-aruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
I searched within the Heart – the limitless Expanse of 

Awareness – Thee, verily my Self, whom I had lost and 
have regained Thee by Thy Grace.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 107

Commentary: 
The word “thīram” stands for “shore” or “boundary”. 

The ‘Heart (aham)’ that is mentioned here is the Supreme, 
Impartite, Transcendent, Expanse of Grace of the Unbroken 
Infinite intelligence and not the fleshy organ of the mortal 
frame. This is the reason for the usage “In the Heart, the 
limitless Expanse (thīramil ahattil)”. The Lord, who is of 
the form of the Transcendent expanse, is Self-established by 
His might of the Light of Awareness, the Space of Expansive 
Grace; in other words the Lord liveth in His light. He has 
to be sought, known and realized only in the Heart where 
He resides – as Heart. Hence the statement “Seeking in 
the Heart (ahattil tēḍi)” for the Self reveals Itself direct to 
whosoever turns towards It.

Since Arunachala remains ever as the Self, shining 
as Being and Awareness, never has anyone really ‘lost’ 
Arunachala and hence there is no question of ‘regaining’ 
Him either, in the absolute sense. He is assumed to have 
been ‘regained’, because of the non-perception of the Reality 
due to ‘Pramada’ (which is forgetfulness and inattention to 
our nature due to the frisking ego’s mischief), the origin of 
which is beyond reckoning in the time scale, whereby He is 
presumed as ‘lost’. This Bhagavan expresses in “I was made 
to repossess the precious Thee (tirumba uṯṯṟēṉ)”.

If that state is now non-existent but comes later into 
existence, It cannot be our natural state; hence It will not 
abide but go away. But It is something real, It exists even 
‘now’ no less than ‘then’.

The mind is created and ordained by the Lord with 
an inherent proclivity to rush outward through the senses. 



108 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Only when, by the Grace of the Lord, the mind is turned 
heartward and acquires the power to seek and attain the 
Lord who shines in the Heart (aham) as I (aham) He can be 
known and realized as He really is. This is the significance 
of the word ‘Grace (aruḷ)’ here.

The term “dhīram” taken in the sense of “strength” 
speaks of a different import. “Searching for Thee, the 
indweller of my Heart, lacking the requisite fortitude and 
‘courage’ unable to search for and attain Thee, I turned 
outward as before with my attempt thwarted. Therefore 
graciously bestow Thy Grace”. In the phrase “in the heart 
devoid of courage (dhīramil ahattil)”, the want of fortitude, 
as the reason for the inability to attain Him is spoken of as 
a special quality of the heart, euphemistically. The reason 
offered here provides the raison d’etre for supporting this 
version. 

The word “strength or fortitude (dhīram)” connotes the 
prowess of Grace, the power of the Atman, the Self-God. Yet 
another interpretation emerges as well: “I searched for Thee 
in a heart bereft of fortitude (and lest my endeavour should 
become futile) I regained Thee purely by Thy benign Grace 
I was enveloped in.” Here the “strength (dhīram)” stands for 
the strength of a sharp intellect – the denotative knowledge 
of the jiva – accompanied by the sense of doership.
A.I.F.A.:

Tēḍik kaṇḍu koṇḍēṉ; - Tiru
	 Māloḍu Nāṉ-muhaṉum
Tēḍit tēḍoṇāt Dēvaṉai eṉ-ṉuḷḷē
	 Tēḍik kaṇḍu koṇḍēṉ.*

*	 Appar Thēvāram, 4th Tirumuṟai, Tiru Aṅgamālai, v. 12



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 109

The Lord whom even Brahma and Vishnu could not 
search and obtain, seek search within and obtain did I.

46.	 Tup-paṟi villā ip-piṟap peṉ-payan
		  Op-piḍa vā-yēṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Of what avail is this birth of mine, bereft of the 

knowledge of quest – the wisdom of vichara? Since it 
is fruitless, should I as much as dare open my mouth, 
deeming it as an entity to stand comparison with any – 
even with beings of so low a specie as a cur?
Commentary: 

Vichara jnana (tuppu aṟivu) is the subtle enquiry carried 
on in secrecy, unknown to others. It is both an experience 
and the might of excellence. It refers to the solid and firm 
knowledge of knowing the real truth of oneself through 
experience by the subtle process of tracing closely within, 
the way one came out. In the first place, it consists in 
compacting or gathering all the other thoughts of the mind 
into the ‘I’ thought, the support and foundation of them all, 
like the thread in which disparate gems are strung.

Then with stern dispassion the quest or Atma Vichara 
“Who am I” must be pursued by the mind heartward-turned 
detecting and (investigating) the way the ego (a mere 
reflection) as ‘I’ surfaced, tracing it to its logical end. Then, 
there in the Heart, the source of the ‘I’, its original Truth, 
one’s inherent Form, is directly perceived as the first hand 
experience. This is pratyaksha, direct experience, solid or 



110 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

firm knowledge. The stamp and seal of that knowledge is 
the shining of the mind in its source, the Heart, as ultimate 
and serene peace in Supreme Silence.

A dog, blindfolded and abandoned in a strange place, by 
searching and sniffing its way, guided by its olfactory sense 
returns back to the original location from where it got parted. 
In the same way, swerving carelessly from one’s nature and 
form of pure Sat Chit Ananda due to inadvertence and bound 
and whirled round in the imagined fictitious appearance of 
‘I, the ego’ the jiva reversing the course by turning back 
inward and retracing the way he came out, must through 
the tool of jnana vichara, seek and reach the source of this 
‘ego’, namely, the Heart where one’s natural Form shines as 
the Eternal and Ever Free one. Thus does Bhagavan explain 
the path of ‘vichara’. The verse “Am I worse than a dog? 
(jñamaliyiṟ kēḍā)” needs to be perused and reflected upon 
once again in this light, to one’s advantage. As mentioned 
here, while even a dog possesses the power of being led 
by the scent, I, lacking the detective knowledge akin to the 
olfactory instinct of a dog, am, indeed, of a lowlier birth 
than that of a dog. This is the drift of the phrase “lacking 
discriminative wisdom to pursue the subtle enquiry, of what 
avail is this birth? (tuppaṟivu illā ippiṟappeṉ payaṉ?)”.

The aparoksha jnana, the non-mediated experience 
of the Self, the ultimate end and goal of human life, is 
beyond attainment mainly due to this want of investigative 
knowledge. Bhagavan bemoans “of what avail is this birth 
(ippiṟappeṉ payaṉ).” One whose birth is marked by the failure 
of achieving the supreme aim is one still-born, though born, 
since he has lost out on its purpose and therefore is lowlier 
than the lowliest. Hence the berating, “Do I need a mouth 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 111

to compare it (oppiḍa vāyēṉ).” “oppiḍal” is “to compare”. If 
the ego mind is not annihilated the knowledge of the direct 
experience cannot dawn. Saint Thāyumāṉavar emphasizes 
the same truth:

“Of what avail is it to learn and gain siddhis, subsidence 
of anger great, if subsidence of mind is not learnt? Where 
then is the need for a mouth? (ciṉam aḍaṅgak kaṯṯṟālum 
siddhiyelām peṯṯṟālum maṉam aḍaṅgak kallārkku vāyēṉ 
parāparamē).”

The same expression “oppiḍa vāyēṉ” can be given a 
different interpretation too. “I have not surrendered my 
heart unto Thy Divine Feet. I have not surrendered the 
self – “Atma Samarpanam” (oppuvikka - to offer). It also 
means “by the ceaseless dhyana occasioned by the loss of 
the ego, I have not (spontaneously) thought of Thee on my 
own in my heart bereft of either the dawn of remembering 
or the night of forgetting.”

When the heart is surrendered at the Feet of the Lord, the 
Sovereign Ruler, He is ever beheld in the heart in the form 
of the Self. Even in all the empirical states of existence of 
the external world (this is solely from the perception of the 
onlookers) everything in its entirety, including the perceiver, 
that fragmented entity of the triad, shines as the very form of 
Transcendent Awareness – the Self God – Arunachala. Self- 
knowledge is the realization that one is not the body alone 
but the whole universe. One is the universe which one thinks 
that one only sees. Such a perception as this is generated 
by the “subtle enquiry (tuppu aṟivu)”. “As a means leading 
to the development of this ‘tuppu aṟivu’, let me surrender 
myself to Thee, the nectar of Love.” Here “oppiḍal” is in the 



112 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

sense of “surrender”: and “vaidal” is “vaiththal” and means 
“chanced to be blessed with”.

Uṉṉi-ḍattil oppu-vitta uḷḷat-tāl ep-pozhudum
Uṉṉaik-kaṇḍu ellā-mum uṉṉuru-vāy ‒ aṉṉi-yamil
Aṉbu-seyum aṉṉōṉ Aruṇāchalā velgum
Iṉbu-ruvām uṉṉil āzhndē.*
He who surrenders his mind unto Thee and seeing 
Thee always beholds the universe as Thy Image, he 
who at all times, glorifies Thee and loves Thee as 
none other than the Self, he is the master without a 
rival, being one with Thee, O Arunachala, and lost 
in Thy Bliss.
Surrendering the self in all its dimensions – in totality – 

rid of the sense of doership, the mischievous play of 
jivabodha, to the Lord and Master, and to be like a dry 
leaf wafted and blown away by the wind, treading the 
way the Divine chooses to lead on, is the perfect and ideal 
sadhana worthy of being achieved by a sadhaka. This is the 
instruction imparted by this verse.

Further, if the phrase, “oppiḍa vāyēṉ”, is split up 
differently “(oppiḍa + āyēṉ)” ‘oppiḍal’ – ‘filling up a void 
and making the surface even’, it can also mean “of what 
avail is the life without the power of enquiry? Come – and 
by Thy Grace – fill up the void and make my adhyatmic life 
smooth and even, by intense reflection and adoption of a 
strong, sound and suitable strategy.” The term ‘āydal’ refers 
to the work that needs to be done without procrastination, 
but with deep reflection and initiative.

*	 Sri Bhagavan, Arunachala Pancharatnam, v. 5.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 113

47.	 Tūy-maṉa mozhi-yar tōyu-muṉ mey-yaham
		  Tōyavē yaru-ḷeṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Arunachala, my liege and Lord! Bestow Thy Grace 

so that in Thy Form, where in merge those souls ripened 
in grace with mind purified, perfected, purged and 
annihilated, I may also merge.
Commentary: 

The mind becoming pure and purged (maṉam tūydal, 
ozhidal) signifies the mind becoming pure and satvic, that 
is unalloyed with the other qualities with rajas and tamas 
totally annihilated.

What is known as the “extinction of the form of mind”, is 
the mind becoming crystal clear, distilled into purity, purged 
of all dross and defilement, leading it to its real form and 
landing it in the state of the experience of the knowledge of 
the Self. The inherent nature of the mind is unsullied purity 
or pure satva. The other two qualities, namely, rajas 
(activity-oriented nature) and tamas (indolence), causing 
avarana (non-perception of Reality due to veiling caused 
by tamas), and vikshepa (misperception of Reality or 
the projection of something else as Reality caused by 
rajas), come in between, mix with satva and besmear it 
with impurity. (avarana and vikshepa are twin aspects 
of the primal nescience). Total transformation of these 
two qualities that intervene and attach themselves in 
between can be accomplished by special endeavours 
undertaken, banking on the power of the Grace Divine. 
None can merge, soak and sink in the Self, the Real Form, 



114 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

but for this total purity or pure satva – the extinction of 
the form of mind. This content is verbalized in “Those 
ripe jivas purged of their minds and merged as one 
with Thee, (tūymaṉa mozhiyar tōyum uṉ mey)”. Since 
I am one who is not already equipped with this rich ripeness 
of maturity, by the might of Thy Grace, ripen me (either 
by look, thought or touch, as in verse 61) and “make me 
merge and mingle in Thee (aham tōyavē aruḷ).” Thus does 
Bhagavan entreat Arunachala. The implied emphasis on 
“only those (ozhiyarē)” would provide the meaning “let 
even me the poor one, not so richly qualified, sink in Thee.” 
If the line is taken to mean “Those ripe souls with mind 
and speech matured with purity (tūy maṉa mozhiyar)”, 
it refers to the trikarana suddhar, those who are Pure of 
body, speech and thought. Though only speech and mind 
are mentioned, the third is implied. The purity of body may 
not be such an indispensable necessity as far as sadhana is 
concerned as that of the other two for the minds of those 
illustrious and eminent souls have become purity itself due 
to meditation like the flow of oil. Hence the need to spell it 
out in particular did not arise.

Gaṅgai yāḍil-eṉ kāviri yāḍil-eṉ
Koṅgu taṇ-kumarit-tuṟai yāḍil-eṉ
Oṅgu mā-kaḍal ōda nīrāḍil-eṉ
Eṅgum īsaṉ eṉāda-varkku illaiyē.*
Of what avail is bathing in Ganges, Kaveri or Kumari, 
in the ghat of Cape in waters cool and fragrant or in 
the ocean great of massive tides? Fruitless are such 

*	 Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai, 099-Podhu, v. 2.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 115

efforts if Civan is not perceived and thought of as 
All-pervading.
Let Thy Real Being attained by them shine and be 

enshrined in my Heart is the import. “Aham” stands for the 
‘Heart’.
A.I.F.A:

Arunachala! Bestow Thy grace that I too may become-
one with Thy Real form with which those pure, both in 
thought and speech, commingle.

Purity of mind lies in desirelessness. Purity of speech 
is Truth not in conflict with non-injury.

“Those ripe souls with mind and speech pure (tūy maṉa 
mozhiyar)”, stands for sages who yearned and endeavoured 
for and attained that purity and truth. ‘Truth’ here stands for 
the Transcendent Truth.

Tūy-mai yeṉbadu avā-viṉmai maṯ-ṯṟadu
Vāy-mai vēṇḍa varum.*
Purity is naught but freedom from desire; this freedom 
is achieved by yearning after perfect truthfulness. 
Purity includes external purity as well. ‘tōydal’ is To 

‘merge’: Like a figurine made of nectar risen from the 
ocean of nectar, the jiva, sprung from the condensed mass 
of Bliss, the True Being (Iswara), again dissolving in that 
True Being is ‘tōydal’. Jiva denotes ahankara. Thy True 
Form (uṉ meiaham): Thy Form that is verily my Heart. The 
expression “My Arunachala (eṉ Arunachala)” is out of the 
liberty he enjoys with Him.

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, 364.



116 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

48.	 Dey-vameṉ ḏṟuṉ-ṉaic chā-ravē yeṉ-ṉai
		  Chēra vozhit-tāy Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
When I, reposing faith and regarding Thee as my 

Guardian God surrendered unto Thee, (contrary to my 
intentions), Thou hast made me extinct, completely.
Commentary: 

In the light of “As I surrendered unto Thee” (chēra), 
‘God’ (deivam)” is interpreted as Guardian God. “As I 
have taken refuge at Thy Feet as God, I have become Thy 
charge and ipso facto it behoves Thee to protect me as my 
Guardian God.” “Surrendering to Thee (chārdal)” is to think 
unceasingly without a moment’s lull. The blessing such a 
one enjoys is proclaimed by Tirukkuṟaḷ.

Malar-misai ēhiṉāṉ māṇaḍi chērndār
Nila-misai nīḍu-vāzh vār.*
Behold the man who taketh refuge in the sacred 
Feet of Him who walked on flowers; his days will 
be many upon the earth.
“Destroyed in toto (chēra ozhittāy)” is the same as 

“shaving off my head clean (moṭṭai aḍittal)” of verse 85. 
This is to render naught the knowledge of the ego, the 
individuality that, out of delusion regards the body as ‘I’ 
along with its multitude of illusory ramifications. This 
is again alluded to later on as “by throwing mud on my 
livelihood Thou hast ruined me (eṉ vāyil maṇṇiṉaiyaṭṭi eṉ 
pizhaippu ozhittadu)”.

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, 3.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 117

What was all along a knowledge that he was indirectly 
aware of as to the nature of Arunachala as one who uproots 
the ego of those who think of Him in their hearts, now has 
been conclusively confirmed as an irrefutably established 
Truth by his aparoksha anubhava, the non-mediated 
experience, in the utmost immediacy of the Heart. This fact 
has been explicitly made clear in this verse.

Even those who worship Arunachala in an attitude 
of duality, through the annihilation of their ego by His 
Grace will ultimately experience the Non-Dual Jnana, is 
the idea.

Ōdariya duvi-tamē at-tuvida jñāṉat-tai
Uṇḍu-paṇu jñāṉam-āhum.*
The inexplicable Dvaitic knowledge
Is the knowledge that
Leads to Advaitic knowledge.

A.I.F.A.:
Those who, seeking intensely take refuge in 

Arunachala regarding Him as “Arunachala indeed is my 
God (deyvameṉ)” abide in Him as Self (That) ripened 
by the matchless power of ceaseless rememberance and 
comtemplation. And by the sheer strength of such abidance 
they achieve the death of the phantom ego – false God – 
called self-loss. Bhagavan proclaims this Truth based on the 
proof of his own experience.

*	 Thāyumāṉavar, Eṅgum Niṟainda Poruḷ, v. 3.



118 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

49.	 Tēḍā duṯṯṟa-nal tiru-varuḷ ṇidi-yahat 
		  Tiyak-kam tīrt-taruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Oh, The benign treasure-trove of divine Grace that has 

come to me unsought on It’s own! Graciously transform 
the penury of my mental delusion.
Commentary: 

All the requisite sadhanas and tapas that ought to have 
been performed to entitle one to be a worthy recipient 
of Arunachala’s Grace had already been performed by 
Bhagavan in previous births and since he has not embarked 
on any new endeavour in the present birth, he says “The 
wealth of divine Grace that he gained unsought (tēḍātuṯṯṟa 
tiruvaruḷ).” What revered elders of renown glorify as 
“causeless compassion”, is founded on this axiom. He is 
verily the antidote that transforms the mental delusion – the 
prime cause of all the afflictions of transmigration; hence He 
is “ever benign and auspicious holy Grace (nal tiruvaruḷ)”. 
As there is no other wealth that is as rich and opulent with 
excellence to devotees as the Grace of Arunachala, Bhagavan 
describes it as “the wealth of Divine Grace (tiruvaruḷ nidhi).” 
Arunachala is spoken of as wealth (nidhi) itself and not as 
the Lord of wealth (nidhipati) for that Grace – personified 
as Wealth – indeed is His very Form and a possession that is 
not the other of Him. While the divine Grace is compared to 
the treasure trove, the mental bewilderment that is removed 
by it, though, has not been spoken of as impoverishment, 
has to be inferred so. The celebrated Nālaḍiyār declares 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 119

Nuṇ-uṇarvu iṉmai vaṟumai ahdu-uḍaimai 
Paṇ-ṇap paṇaitta perum selvam. (v. 251)
Poverty is the absence of Subtle Awareness, 
Possession of it is wealth, great and rich. 
What is empirically called in the dual plane as ‘grace’ 

is nothing but the form of Arunachala, Pure Awareness. 
It must be known that ‘That’ indeed has the potency of 
removing the delusion in the hearts of the devotees.

Verse 18 is worthy of being studied in tandem with this.
A.I.F.A.:

The terms “auspicious or holy or divine (thiru) and 
“wealth (nidhi)” can be taken as two ways of addressing 
Arunachala. ‘Thiru’ stands for the benign Deliverance or 
Release. As it is the very form of Infinite Bliss of Beatitude, 
it is called ‘auspicious (nal thiru)’. As He is the wealth of 
Grace unsought, He Himself is “wealth of Grace Divine (aruḷ 
nidhi) – the wealth of causeless compassion. Maṇivāchakar 
too addresses the Lord in a similar vein 

Aruḷ-nidhi tara-varum āṉanda malaiyē*
Mountain of Bliss, treasure of grace Who com’st to 
yield.

*	 Tiruvāchakam, 20-Tiruppaḷḷi Ezhucchi, v. 2.



120 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

50.	 Dai-riya mō-ḍumuṉ mey-yaham nāḍayāṉ 
		  Taṭ-ṭazhin dē-ṉaruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Venturing forth to enquire and know Thy real form in the 

heart, by human endeavour (that effort becoming fruitless 
and wasted), my mental strength undone, vexed, I am lost. 
Pray, forbear the fault of my arrogant ego and extend Thy 
Grace so that I may behold Thy Real Form.
Commentary: 

Courage (dairiyam) is the assertion prodded by an 
arrogant ego – the jivabodha. The piled up knowledge of 
the mounting ego is useful in knowing things alien to itself 
as the “other” of it by objectifying them but is useless in 
knowing the truth of It’s source by abiding in the Heart 
as ‘That’ and by subsiding into it. It must be known that 
learned and knowledgeable elders ever emphasize that with 
the subsidence of the assertive nature of the vaulting ego, 
depending ever on and led by the power of the divine Grace, 
one must know and attain the Feet of the Lord. Just like a 
ship caught in the eye of the storm in the middle of the ocean 
tossed about by the waves and winds of a tempest, unable to 
reach the shore, I, caught in the tempest of bondage in the 
wretched ocean of birth, buffeted all over by the tumultuous 
winds of vasanas from beginningless time, repeatedly 
floundering, have become heart-broken, unable to reach the 
shore of deliverance – Thy Feet – the True Being. So much 
of content is packed in a single simple word “taṭṭazhindēṉ”. 
Heartened and encouraged by Thy Grace, seeking in my 
heart, Thee, the form of my Real Self (and by the grandeur 
of that power), the ego I, the state of jivahood – that veiled 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 121

that Truth – ruined and rendered naught, I attained Thee, 
Thy Form of Sivahood. “My gaining the same is the gift 
and grandeur of Thy Grace” is another interpretation. In 
the rendition ‘I-(yan)’ is the ego mind ‘I’, the jivahood. “To 
be ruined (taṭṭazhidal)” stands for the Supreme Awareness 
without even a trace of false duality – the form of Siva – the 
Substratum – flashing and shining in the Heart as Self as the 
plenitude of Bliss with the super imposition, imagined and 
projected on it veiling that substratum, made extinct.

51.	 Toṭ-ṭaruḷ kai-mey kaṭ-ṭiḍā yeṉi-liyāṉ
		  Naṭ-ṭamā vē-ṉaruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
If Thou dost not touch me with Thy hand of Grace (so 

that the assertiveness of the individuality of the jivabodha 
as ‘I’ surceasing, I can unite with Thy non-dual Form) and 
enfold me not tight – if ‘this’ does not commingle with 
‘That’ as one – the purpose of my birth unachieved, I will 
be lost. Graciously protect me from such a doom.
Commentary:

“Bestow Thy touch of Holy Grace (toṭṭaruḷ kai)” 
is probably an entreaty for sparsa diksha (initiation by 
touch). Aspiring for Union with Siva Swarupa by mere 
human endeavour alone by a jiva by accomplishing solid 
anchorage in ‘nishtai’ is an impossibility. This is due to the 
fact that the jiva is rendered totally impotent by the power 
of the propensities (latencies) that lead the jiva in the path 
that is diametrically opposite to inherence in the Heart. 
Hence Bhagavan beseeches Arunachala to come forward 



122 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

and accomplish it for him by absorbing him into Himself 
in “If Thou dost embrace me not (mey kaṭṭiḍāy eṉil)”. 
“Embracing (kaṭṭiḍāl)” stands for the non-dual and solid or 
firm commingling: ‘solid’ in the sense of being dissolved 
to the last iota in the form of ‘That’ or Siva Swarupa, as 
luminous Awareness of Pure Being.

Saint Thāyumāṉavar extols with great zeal the necessity 
and the grandeur of ‘getting bound’ within that enfoldment 
of Grace as follows:

Iha-parat-tum viṭ-ṭup piriyāda mēlāṉa attuvidak
Kaṭ-ṭuk-kuḷ āvadu eṉḏṟō kāṇ
Oh! When will that day dawn when I become bound 
by the Supreme bond – Non-dual – that never parts 
from me either here or hereafter.
In the context of bridal imagery in which this Text is set, 

the import of the first line may be construed as: “If Thou 
dost not embrace me and enjoy the bliss of Union”, wherein 
‘embracing’ is an expression of euphemism. Human birth 
achieves the acme of fulfilment, when the lady-love united 
in wedlock to an ideal husband possessed in full, of form 
(exquisite beauty), wealth, spotless lineage and virtue, 
enjoys the bliss of conjugal union. The sole goal worthy of 
achievement by a jiva is attaining Siva, the bridegroom who 
is verily the fathomless ocean of nectarine virtues and being 
submerged in the waves of Bliss of Siva.

piṟandum piṟavādāril
Amongst the learned, there are none who failed to 
achieve the goal of human birth



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 123

Hence Bhagavan beseeches Arunachala for His Grace 
benign, so that such a fate of wasting a human birth may 
not befall him. An echo of what he voices poignantly here 
as “this human birth of mine becoming wasted, I’ll be lost 
(naṭṭamāvēṉ)”, has reverberated in verse 46 “Of what avail 
is this birth, if it lacks the discriminative wisdom to pursue 
Self enquiry? (tuppaṟivillā ippiṟap peṉpayaṉ)?”

52.	 Tōḍa-mil ṉī-ahat tōḍoṉ-ḏṟi eṉḏṟum-san
		  Dōḍa-moṉ ḏṟiḍa-varuḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou, undefiled by any, pray, do bestow Thy Grace 

on me, so that – rid of the pangs of separation from Thee, 
from which hitherto I had been suffering – I may ever 
be immersed in the eternal Bliss of non-dual union with 
Thee.
Commentary: 

Arunachala is addressed as “Thou, the ever undefiled, by 
any (toḍamil nī),” for the absence of the original defilement 
of the ego in Him negates totally all other defilements 
that follow in its wake. Therefore He is the shoreless 
ocean of virtues. The grandeur of Self-Supreme is that It 
is the flawless state of ‘Being’. Bhagavan by addressing 
Arunachala thus, invites and prayerfully submits to Him to 
rectify this fundamental flaw in him specifically.

Here “defilement” stands for the flaw of the ‘ego’ and 
all its entourage. The word ‘defilement’ (tōḍam) is a generic 
term standing for all the defilements – the ego and all in its 
train.



124 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

“Becoming non-dually one with me (ondrudal)” is to 
lose the difference of “the other” and becoming one with 
non-difference. The separation or parting from the Lord 
called ‘viyoga’, ‘vibhakti’ is an imagined illusion of the ego-
mind born of nescience. When that illusion drops off by the 
firm knowledge that dawns by the descent of Grace, the Self 
commingles with Brahman and finds that it has ever been 
so. Never was it apart from Brahman. Having commingled 
and abided in Self as Being once, is to be united forever, 
immersed in the still, immutable, uncloying Bliss Supreme 
and never again is there a lapse back into the night of 
forgetting and becoming the jiva ever.

Hence Bhagavan implores “united ever as one with 
Thee, ever in Thy Bliss may I remain, bestow Thy Grace 
(ahattōḍu oṉḏṟi eṉḏṟum sandōḍam oṉḏṟiḍa aruḷ).”

Thāyumāṉavar declares this as follows:
Oruk-kālē kalanda-varuk(ku) ekkālun-tāṉ
Ulavāda pēriṉbam ulavā-niṟkum.
For those merged once ceaseless and Supreme Bliss 
		  will ever abide.
“Thou, uniting with my Heart, graciously grant me the 

eternal Bliss”, is the prayer.
The term “uniting (oṉḏṟi)” taken as “oṉḏṟiḍa” will mean 

“bless me to become non-dually one with Thee”, and just 
as ‘oṉḏṟi’ will mean “bless me and become non-dually one 
with me.”



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 125

53.	 Nahaik-kiḍa milai-niṉ ṉāḍiya veṉai-yaruḷ
		  Nahai-yiṭṭup pār-nī Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
This is no opportune time to mock derisively at me 

who, with a heart, tattered and gone threadbare due to 
my inelegance and simplicity, have sought succour and 
reached Thee as the sole Refuge. Bestow Thy Glance on 
me with the smile of Thy Grace Divine.
Commentary: 

Appar’s Thēvāram in a similar strain can be compared 
and relished. 

“eṉṉai nī ihazha vēṇḍām (deride me not).”
Verse 81 which runs as “Do not be a mirror held before 

a noseless man ridiculing him. Rather, be like an endearing 
mother, lift me up, embrace me and bestow Thy Grace 
(mūkkilaṉ muṉkāṭṭu muhuramāgādu eṉait tūkki aṇaindu 
aruḷ)” can be viewed in passing for the flow of an identical 
trend of thought.

Here the import of the term as “This is not an occasion 
to laugh (nahaikkiḍamilai)” may also be read as ‘nahai’ 
meaning ‘jewel’ and “This is no time to jeer at me for lacking 
in beauty since I am not bedecked with ornaments,” and for 
“Look at me by bestowing on me Thy smile of Holy Grace 
(aruḷ nahaiyiṭṭup pār)”, “adorn me with the ornament of Thy 
Grace and thence look at me.”

The heart ruled by love and affection will ever be 
blind to the faults of one it loves and will gloss over the 
shortcomings but will perceive with great pride the positive 
virtues in them. Great men matured in wisdom granting 



126 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

shelter and succour provide asylum to those reduced to the 
state of dire penury, stricken with distress and do not mock 
at them with derision.

Inner purity alone is true beauty. Real beauty lies within, 
in the blossoming of the heart caused by the benign Grace of 
the Lord. Beauty of the countenance, a mere external sheen, 
is not the real beauty for men of wisdom.

Kuñji azhagum koḍun-tāṉaik kōṭ-ṭazhagum 
Mañjaḷ azhagum azhagalla - neñjattu 
Nallam-yām eṉṉum naḍuvu nilaimai-yāl 
Kalvi azhagē azhagu.*
Neither the beauty of the tresses,
Nor of the attire one is draped in,
Nor the colour of the skin
With the sheen of golden yellow
Is any beauty at all but
Learning that engenders equanimity
One abides in, is Beauty True.

54.	 Nā-ṇilai nā-ḍiḍa nāṉā yoṉ-ḏṟinī 
		  Tāṇuvā niṉ-ḏṟaṉai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
To be united with Thee as one (ravaged by longing 

and intensely thinking of Thee and even foregoing my 
modesty, a lady, now) coming forward and seeking Thee 
on my own, Thou, the Supreme among men, who had 
earlier, on Thy own come and claimed me as Thine own, 

*	 Nālaḍiyār, 131.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 127

without the least shame standeth motionless as a pillar 
now. (Is this all the grandeur and beauty of Thy Grace and 
manliness?)
Commentary: 

The content within the bracket is to be seen in the light 
of bridal imagery alone. 

‘oṉḏṟi’ is to commingle with or merge into. The line 
“nāṉāy oṉḏṟinī tāṇuvā niṉḏṟaṉai” may also mean “Thou 
(without standing as one apart from me) abiding as part 
and parcel of my being as my own Atman standeth rock-
like” wherein the seeker has merged into the sought. The 
word ‘nāṉāy’ (on my own; as myself) as in (nāṉāy oṉḏṟi: 
Thou abiding as me) and in (nāṉāy nāḍiḍa; seeking Thee 
on my own) functions as a word common to both. “Seeking 
Thee on my own Thou standeth as a pillar (nāṉāy nāḍiḍa 
.. nī tāṇuvā niṉḏṟaṉai)” spells out the truth that the jiva’s 
nature, with its perpetual drift outwards is to flit forever 
while Siva’s nature is to remain “motionless and serene” 
simply “to be and to shine.” The idea is that Arunachala, 
at the culmination of steadfast Atma vichara and as the 
fruit of vichara reveals Himself as the resplendent Self 
that is non-different from the tranquil Siva and thus 
manifesting the real truth of His unmoving and rocklike 
nature He abides as such and shines.

Arunachala shining in luminous Awareness as the 
Self is the act of consummation of True Grace. 

What Bhagavan bespeaks as the act of Arunachala 
in verse 77 is also in tune with this namely, “māṉam 
koṇḍu uṟubavar māṉattai azhittu abhimāṉam illādu oḷir 
Aruṇāchalā” “Thou, by the might of Thy Grace, dost 



128 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

destroy the conceit of attachment to the ego of the devotees 
who surrender to Thee with love. And though appearing to 
be indifferent and devoid of the concern displayed earlier, 
Thou shinest in their hearts as their own Self.”

That Arunachala standeth ‘unashamedly as a pillar’ is 
explained thus: An attitude of difference of being the ‘other’ 
and alien, gives rise to a sense of shame. This differential 
attitude springs from the ego-mind that vaults itself as ‘I’. 
Arunachala, being the Sole, Non-dual Bhuma by His very 
nature (Bhuma: where nothing else exists, nothing else is 
seen and nothing else is heard), is totally bereft of the ego, 
its fragmented perception leading to a sense of shame. And 
He shines as the Self.

“Stone pillar (sthanu)” here denotes the Whole Sole, 
Primary, rich Expanse of Transcendence – the Condensed 
mass of jnana – parabrahmam.

Nāṉāṉa taṉmai-yeṉḏṟu nāḍāmal nāḍa-iṉba
Vāṉāgi niṉḏ-ṟaṉai-nī vāzhi parā-paramē. *
I sought Thee not in my real nature
But seeking Thee in terms of my egoism
And so Thou stood before me
As the firmament of Bliss
Blessed be Thou
Oh, Para Paramē!

*	 Thāyumāṉavar, 43-Parāparakkaṇṇi,v. 225.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 129

55.	 Niṉ-ṉeri yerit-teṉai nīṟāk kiḍu-muṉ
		  Niṉ-ṉaruḷ mazhai-pozhi Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Let Thy rain of Grace be showered on me ere the fire 

of jnana, which is Thy very Form, burns me as ashes of 
Thy Form – the Self (so that I may endure it with my 
heart cooled).
Commenatry: 

“nīṟu”, in general stands for vibhuti, the sacred ash. 
In truth it is a synonym for Swarupa – the grandeur of the 
formless Self. It stands for a state of Being and Becoming. 
The conflagration of jnana (niṉṉeri) must be understood 
in the sense of Rahu’s head. (Rahu is one of the nine 
planets of the solar galaxy and has just a head and nothing 
else to speak of as a ‘body’). Likewise Arunachala is Fire 
Himself. “Thy Fire (niṉṉeri)” is similar to saying “Fire 
of fire”.

Unless the heart has been already cooled sufficiently 
enough by the Grace of the Lord – unless the Grace has 
already ripened the soul sufficiently – the flames of 
conflagration of jnana of the Swarupa – the Self – difficult 
to endure, being fiery and fierce, will burn up the jiva. Hence 
the reverential supplication to cool down the heart. In the 
framework of bridal imagery, it may mean “Before the fire 
of the pangs of separation scorch and burn me up to ashes, 
graciously shower Thy rain of Grace so that my life may 
flourish.”



130 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

A.I.F.A.:
Arunachala, let Thy rain of Grace descend on me ere 

Thy fire of jnana burns me up reducing me to ashes.
“The fire of Thy Form (niṉṉeri)”: Since the Lord is the 

form of jnana, the fire is interpreted as the fire of jnana. 
In order to be relieved of the afflictions of transmigration 
occasioned by nescience, if he were to be burnt up first by 
the fire of jnana, he possesses not the power to endure it; 
hence he pleads for the rain of cool grace.

“Burning up” is to make one the ‘form of Self’. “Me 
(eṉṉai)” stands for the ego; since it is the form of life as 
well. “Reducing to ashes (nīṟākkudal)”: to render it extinct 
totally. ‘If the descent of the rain of Grace precedes it, I can 
have the strength to endure the flames’ is the content.

56.	 Nī-nā ṉaṟap-puli nidaṅ-kaḷi maya-mā 
		  Niṉ-ḏṟiḍu nilai-yaruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Graciously enfold me in Thy firm embrace so that the 

attitude of duality such as ‘Thou’ and ‘me’ may cease and 
depart totally from my heart and I may abide forever in 
the state of Bliss-filled Self.
Commentary: 

Nī vēṟu-eṉādu-irukka nāṉ vēṟu-eṉādu-irukka 
Nērāha vāzhva-daṟku aruḷ-kūra*
Bestow Thy Grace I may live as such
Bereft of the idea Thou, the One and I, the other.

*	 Tiruppugazh, 223.	



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 131

Iru-viṉai mu(m)-malamum aṟa iṟavi-yoḍu piṟavi-yaṟa
Ēka bhōga-māy nīyu nāṉu-māy
Iṟugum-vahai parama-sukham adaṉai-yaruḷ 

Iḍaimarudhil
Ēka nāyakā lōkha nāyakā imaiyavar perumāḷē.*
Lord seated in Idai Marudhoor,
Lord Unique and Sole,
Lord of the Celestials
Bestow that Infinitude of Bliss
The sole joy of Thou and I
Dissolved as one in Thee
Rid of the two deeds, three slags†
Death along with birth.
“The attitudinal difference of Thou and me sundered 

(nī nāṉaṟap puli)” depicts the ‘advaita bhava’, the non-dual 
attitude and the state of abidance as Bliss. This – and the 
prayer for the attainment of the grand Bliss of Release in 
the line “ever in the blissful experience of the Self (nidam 
kaḷi mayamā)” – are revealed respectively in the above 
mentioned Psalms from Thiruppugazh. “The attitudinal 
difference as Thou and me sundered (nī nāṉaṟappuli)”: 
Graciously embrace me so that my ego-mind – the hurdle in 
the way of my attaining Thee –be made non-existent. This 
fragmented individuality of the ‘ego’, ever plaguing one 
with despair and despondency of vibhakti and viyoga will 
not perish but for the Union with the Grace of Arunachala, 
the mass of jnana, the Brahman, the Sole and Non-dual 

*	 Tiruppugazh, 858.
†	 Two deeds: good and bad deeds. Three slags: delusion, desire 
and anger.



132 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Being. But for the destruction of the differential attitude 
which is but the death of the ego – the experience of the 
Bliss of the One beyond name and form – cannot be.

The comparison that Bhagavan draws to this state is that 
of “the bee drunk with honey (kaḷimayamā)”. It refers to 
the state of inebriation or intoxication caused by constant 
revelling in the Bliss of the Self.

Adu-eṉḏṟāl edu-eṉavoṉ ḏṟaḍukkuñ caṅgai
Ādaliṉāl adu-eṉalum aṟavē viṭṭu
Madu-uṇḍa vaṇḍeṉa-vuñ caṉakaṉ ādi
Maṉṉavarkaḷ Sukar-mudalōr vāzhndār.*
If you say just “That,”
Doubt mounts as “What?”
So giving up saying “That,”
Did Janaka the king
And sages like Suka
Live indeed like bees
Drunk with honey.
It is worth recalling here verse 23 that runs thus: “Let 

Thy Grace rain on me so that I may drink the essence of the 
fruit in hand, namely, the experience of the Self, which is 
but the Supreme nectar of the essence of Thy real Self and 
become inebriated in that ocean of rapturous Bliss of non-
dual union,”

“kaiyiṉil kaṉi uṉ meyrasam koṇḍu uvahai veṟi koḷavaruḷ”
In truth, uniting with Arunachala just for a while, once, 

is union with Him forever and that Bliss Infinite continues 

*	 Thāyumāṉavar, 14-Ākārabhuvaṉam – Chidampara Rahasyam, 
v. 23.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 133

ever eternally with neither a decrease nor any change. Hence 
Bhagavan bespeaks of “ever in the bliss (nidam kaḷimayamā 
niṉḍṟiḍu nilai)”. Verse 52 speaks of this as well, “that I may 
be immersed in eternal Bliss in non-dual union with Thee 
(tōḍamil nī ahattōḍu oṉḏṟi eṉḏṟum sandōḍamoṉḏṟiḍa aruḷ)”.
A.I.F.A:

Arunachala! Graciously commingle with me so that I 
may, with my ego undone, abide forever homogenously as 
the form of Supreme Plenitude, Bliss of Thee.

Since ahankara is the sole reason for the misery of 
transmigration, to be rid of that pain, Bhagavan states “with 
the perishing of ‘I’ (nāṉaṟa);” and since the ego, reinforced 
and entrenched strongly with a solid firmness over 
innumerable births will not perish but for the grace of the 
Lord, he says “Thou embracing me (pulli)”. What remains 
after the demise of I, is the Lord as Self (Atman). He is the 
personification of the Supreme Bliss that is homogeneous. 
That state of abidance as Bliss – Eternal Home – is described 
as “ever in Bliss (nidam kaḷimayamā)”.

57.	 Nuṇ-ṇuru vuṉai-yāṉ viṇ-ṇuru naṇ-ṇiḍa
		  Eṇ-ṇalai yiṟu-meṉ ḏṟaruṇāchalā

Substance: 
When will that day dawn, when the waves of thoughts 

and the billows of misconceptions, the sole hurdle to my 
uniting with Thee, who shines in my heart as the most 
subtle form of space of consciousness, cease?
