134 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Commentary: 
“Thy form of the sky of consciousness, ether-like” 

flows from the words “nuṇṇuruvāṉa viṇṇuru”. Addressing 
Arunachala as the one of “subtle form” straight away, 
denotes by contrast, the physical, gross, form of ‘I’, clinging 
on to a body with attachment. While the term “the Form 
of Space (viṇṇuru)” would itself suffice and the addition 
“the subtle form (nuṇṇuru)” may appear superfluous, yet it 
is employed with the specific intention of bringing out the 
contrast between the space of the heart that is mentioned here 
and the mere physical or gross space. The space of the heart 
remains as the ‘Eye’ of the space of the mind which, inturn, 
remains the eye of the physical space. The ‘Heart Space’ 
is otherwise known as “daharakasa” (daharm denotes 
subtlety and minuteness) and ‘chittrambalam’ (the space of 
Awareness). Verse 6 of Arunachala Padikam exclaims and 
extols Arunachala’s Grace as “Thou the limitless Expanse 
of Grace, Thou art subtler than ether (viṇṇiṉu nuṇṇaruḷ 
veḷiyē)”.

“To unite (naṇṇudal)” and “as the form of Bliss 
(kaḷimayamāha)” mean “uniting with Thee, non-dually, 
with the shedding of the difference of Thou and I.”

“Thought waves (eṇṇalai)”: Waves after waves 
of thoughts in endless succession under the sway of 
predilections buffet the mind unceasingly like the turbulent 
billows and they are the impediment for the jiva to attain and 
become Siva. The cessation of all movements of the mind, 
in toto through the attrition of vasanas alone can lead to that 
highest Bliss. Bhagavan, therefore, implores Arunachala for 
the showering of His grace.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 135

A.I.F.A.:
“Oh, the Form of Subtlety! Oh, the firmament of 

Awareness! Thou deemest not in Thy Divine Heart that I 
be united with Thee. Oh, When will that defect of not being 
thought of by Thee be removed? (When will Thou think of 
me?)” is another reading.

58.	 Nū-laṟi vaṟi-yāp pē-daiya ṉeṉ-ḏṟaṉ 
		  Mā-laṟi vaṟut-taruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Pray, Graciously remove the perverse knowledge of 

the besotted mind of mine, who am an ignoramus bereft 
of any learning and reveal the True Awareness of Thine 
in my heart.
Commentary: 

The declaration “an ignoramus with no knowledge 
(nūlaṟivaṟiyāp pēdaiyaṉ)” does not mean that he regarded 
knowledge acquired by learning or even scriptural knowledge 
as an indispensible necessity or a special requirement for a 
sadhak as a means to awaken the true awareness in one. (yet 
such a prayerful entreaty from a heart melting away with a 
langurous love augmented by the onrush of yearning is for 
the sacred gift (prasad) of His grace). How is it so?

At the source of the ego, after it has been done away 
with, the transcendent form of Arunachala shines on His 
own as “Aham, Aham”, in a highly subtle form. But this 
Form of Arunachala, the Supreme Being, until the ego 
is done away with by His Grace, cannot be experienced 
directly and most intimately in the immediacy of the heart 



136 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

as the non-mediated experience, however subtle and clear 
may be the knowledge garnered from the scriptural Texts 
radiant with verbal wisdom. Yet, through that knowledge, 
He, to a certain extent, may be grasped intellectually and 
known indirectly. But Bhagavan, in sheer self-denigration, 
declares himself to be an ignoramus bereft of even that. That 
he berates himself in all such humility is precisely to inspire 
compassion in and move Him to thaw and melt down – and 
shower His grace on him. The Grace of Arunachala in truth 
is, indeed, causeless. Though the most supremely pure form 
of desireless and selfless Bhakthi, as an offering to the Lord, 
in a way, may be considered a cause of it, knowledge gained 
from mere book lore is fruitless to one from the ultimate 
point of view.

“The perverse knowledge (mālaṟivu)”: The fragmented 
knowledge of the jivabodha is but the illusory projection 
of the mind – a figment of imagination woven with its 
inseparable strands of seer, seen and the spectacle. Hence it 
is called “mālaṟivu”, empty ignorance, for it is conceptual 
and ego-born, and that being the root from which spring 
all known calamities, the entreaty is for sundering the 
same. This jivabodha goes by the names the awareness of 
objects (suṭṭaṟivu), awareness of the mind and awareness 
of the ego. The aesthetic skills and subtle dexterity in 
multifarious arts and the knowledge of occult sciences like 
clairvoyance, clairaudience and telepathy that astound and 
stun the people of the world, in true perception, fall under 
the category termed by the sobriquet “mālaṟivu”. Sundering 
the ‘mālaṟivu’ encompasses the phantasmagoria of all the 
effects of such knowledge as well. ‘mālaṟivu’ may also 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 137

be interpreted to mean the ‘veiling’ or ‘āvaraṇa’, the non-
perception of Reality, due to ‘concealment’, one of the twin 
aspects of nescience and vikshepa, the misperception of the 
unreal as the real, the other aspect of nescience.

59.	 Nek-kunek kuru-hiyāṉ puk-kiḍa uṉaip-puha
		  Nak-kaṉā niṉ-ḏṟaṉai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Softened and softened by the yearning for Thy Grace, 

body and mind melting away ever more and more with love 
and languor and on surrendering to Thee as my sole refuge, 
Thou dost stand tall in all grandeur, sky-draped – with 
the accoutrement of Awareness, one with the Transcendent 
Expanse. What a wonder is this!
Commentary: 

“dost stand naked (nakkaṉā):” Thou shinest with the 
very quarters as Thy attire, as the Form of Pure jnana, the 
Impartite Atman, Formless.

Juxtaposed this, verse 54 can be read which runs 
thus: “When I have come to be united with Thee, Thou 
unashamedly standeth rocklike (nāṉilai nāḍiḍa nāṉāy 
oṉḏṟinī tāṇuvā niṉḏṟaṉai)”

The ego is the mere reflection of Awareness due to 
mixing up with jada or insentience. When that reflection of 
insentience is rendered naught, the ego, like the hailstone 
in water, dissolves with love as the Self in Siva Swarupa. 
That State, indeed, is bestowed by Arunachala on one 
who becomes fit enough to receive His Grace by having 
surrendered to Him his ego-self and remains as he is in 



138 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

reality. In other words, he is bestowed with the Supreme 
Knowledge of the Self as a direct experience or aparoksha 
anubhuti.

This truth is enshrined and ingested in verse 4 of Uḷḷadu 
Nāṟpadu. “If we have a form, the world and God likewise 
have form. If we have no form, by whom and how can form 
(of world and God) be seen? Without the eye, can there be 
sight or spectacle? The Self, the real eye, is Infinite.”

Uruvan-tāṉ āyiṉ ulahu-param aṯ-ṯṟām
Uruvan-tāṉ aṉḏṟēl uvaṯ-ṯṟiṉ - uruvat-taik
Kaṇ-ṇuṟu-dal yāva-ṉevaṉ kaṇ-ṇalāl kāṭchi-yuṇḍō
Kaṇ-ṇadutāṉ anta-milāk kaṇ.

60.	 Nēsa-mil eṉak-kuṉ āsai-yaik kāṭ-ṭinī 
		  Mōsam seiyā-daruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
I was one who inherently did not harbour even a 

vestige of desire for Thee. But Thou Thyself at first on 
Thy own bestowed Thy Grace on me and inspired in me 
a longing for Thee, the Form of the Bliss of Beatitude. 
But now (by withdrawing that Grace halfway through, 
rendering my love for Thee barren and fruitless) pray, do 
not deceive me but graciously bring my love for Thee to 
fruition and final consummation.
Commentary: 

Though the unassailable love and longing he bears for 
Arunachala is immense, still he was plagued by a sense of 
its inadequacy in his very bones. Hence Bhagavan, thinking 
in tune with all the other mahatmas rich in grace who 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 139

bemoan weeping in wonder, “Neither did I seek, search and 
run after Thee, Nor did I within my heart melt with devotion 
saying where doth Lord Siva abide and who hath perceived 
Him” (viyaṟn dāṅgalaṟit tēḍiṯṟilēṉ, Sivaṉ evviḍattāṉ evar 
kaṇḍaṉar eṉḏṟu ōḍiṯṟilēṉ kiḍanduḷḷuruhēṉ)* says ‘nēsamil’ 
(not having even a whit of longing for Thee). The incidence 
of the conclusive experience with the admixture of non-
duality with Arunachala was one of Bliss beyond name and 
form. But the love and longing that wells up in the hearts 
of the devout as a step and pathway to this experience is 
inspired, indeed, by His Grace alone, true to the dictum “By 
His grace alone, bowing before His feet” (avaṉ aruḷālē avaṉ 
tāḷ vaṇaṅgi)†. Hence he breaks forth in this hymn “kindling 
in me a longing for Thee (uṉ āsaiyaik kāṭṭi)”. But astounded 
with fear and a heart much aggrieved by the misgiving that 
if the devout love that, by Divine Grace and good luck was 
his, were to be thwarted midway, preventing its ripening in 
the due process and becoming fruitful and he being left high 
and dry, being neither here nor there, pleads “Forsake me 
not (mōsam seiyādē)”. This echoes the desperate anguish of 
Appar when he pours forth as follows:

Tuṟak-kap paḍāda uḍalait tuṟandu-vem dūduvarōḍu
Iṟap-paṉ iṟandāl iru-visumbu ēṟuvaṉ ēṟi-vandu
Piṟap-paṉ piṟan-dāl piṟai-aṇivār saḍai piñjjakaṉ-pēr
Maṟappaṉ-kolō eṉḏṟu-eṉ uḷḷam kiḍandu maṟuhiḍumē.‡

*	 Māṇikkavāchakar, Tiruvāchakam: 6-‘Nīttal Viṇṇappam’ 
(Forsake Me Not), v. 149.
† 	 Māṇikkavāchakar, Tiruvāchakam, Sivapuranam.
‡ 	 Appar Thēvāram, 4th Tirumuṟai, 113-Podhu, v. 7.



140 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

My heart is seized with bewildering fear that when 
I shake off the mortal frame to claim which come 
the fearful myrmidons of the Lord of Death, I may 
ascend to Heaven but only to be reborn. And if I 
be reborn I may remember not the holy Name of 
That Lord with coils of matted hair adorned with the 
Ganga stream and Crescent moon.
Since the initial pull towards Him was His benign 

handiwork there is none else now who can be his Saviour. 
Hence the plea “bestow Thy benevolent Grace (arul).” It 
is significant that a similar line of humble supplication is 
discernible in Arunachala Navamanimalai (The Necklet of 
Nine Gems, v. 4) too, “Bless me with Thy cool and refreshing 
glance of Compassion without recourse to any deception 
(taṇṇāraḷi ceṟi kaṇṇāḍorukiṟi paṇṇādu).”

Thāyumāṉavar too prays in a similar vein in the couplet: 
Māl-kāṭṭi cintai mayaṅ-gāmal niṉḏṟu suhak 
Kāl-kāṭṭi vāṅgādē kaṇḍāy parā-paramē.*
Mind mine may remain
Maya Thine by undeluded
Bliss It’s source Thou revealest!
Take it not back Oh Lord Supreme!
He seeks the fond care and concern from Arunachala 

similar to that of one who, prompted by deep desire plants 
a sapling and protects it from the grazing cattle and from 
wilting and withering away in summer by watering it and 
fondling it forever with caressing looks.

*	 Thāyumāṉavar, 43-Paraparakkanni, v. 68.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 141

61.	 Nain-dazhi kaṉi-yāl ṉala-ṉilai padat-til 
		  Nāḍi-yuṭ koḷ-nalam Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
No earthly purpose is served by consuming an over-

ripe and rotten fruit. (Incidentally in this couplet the pun 
on the word ‘kaṉi’ is delectable. ‘kaṉi’ being an abridged 
form of kaṉṉi, meaning a virgin, the word stands for both 
a fruit and a virgin). [Hence, I a virgin, though like a 
fruit, simply remain taciturn fruitlessly] Seek and claim 
me, here and now – the time appropriate – and graciously 
enjoy with relish the positive wellness and gratify Thyself.
Commentary: 

“But for this, the wealth of youth coupled with beauty, 
if not claimed at the proper time, though visible but unseen, 
may fade away, ageing with the passage of time with beauty 
ravaged and charms withered away. And I may waste away 
and die an old maid like the fruit unconsumed when it is just 
ripe becomes spoilt and unfit to be enjoyed.”

The lady-love, coy, learned, refined, highly cultured and 
sophisticated, never for a moment forsakes her modesty – 
the precious jewel that a lady of virtue esteems highly and 
holds dear – expresses her heart’s intent towards her beloved 
with great clarity, finesse and candour. This couplet, rich in 
esoteric content, will be the delight of the discerning ones.

The line “a fruit over-ripe and rotten unfit to be eaten 
(naindazhi kaṉiyā nalaṉilai)” also lends itself for yet 
another reading. An aged virgin with youth faded away is 
unacceptable – is a figure of speech – an embellishment 
suggestive of a hidden content. He pleads with a heart 
kneaded, softened and melting away with overwhelming 



142 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

devotion to graciously claim and consume him in the 
blessed state ere the mind is obliterated and made naught, 
after ascertaining the ripeness and flavour of the essence of 
devotion he is soaked in.

62.	 Non-diḍā duṉ-ḍṟaṉai tan-deṉaik koṇ-ḍilai
		  Andaka nī-yeṉak karuṇāchalā

Substance: 
Without courting any pain on my part Thou hath 

given Thyself unto me and hath Thou not claimed me 
in exchange for it? As this sounds the death-knell of me, 
Thou art verily the Lord of Death unto me.
Commentary: 

“Hath Thou not claimed me? (koṇḍilai?)”. This elicits the 
reply in affirmation “Yes Thou didst” (based on the principle 
of grammar that two negatives make one positive). “To take 
away or claim (koḷḷuthal)” in the sense of claiming the life 
means “to kill one (kolludal)” as is found in the adage “Ere 
the cruel Lord of Death, Yama who claims or takes away 
(the life) and therefore kills, do engage in deeds of virtue 
with a tender heart filled with mercy (kollum koḍumkūṯṟam 
kolvāṉ kuṟukutaṉmuṉ uḷḷam kaṉindu aṟamceytu uykavē)”*. 
Incidentally, Bhagavan also addresses Arunachala in Padikam 
(The Decad on Arunachala) as “one who spirits away lives 
is this mighty Arunachala (uyirkkoli Aruṇamāgiriyē)”. 
“Thou killed me though painlessly (nōvāmalē koṉḏṟāy)” 
– suggests that this was a painless exchange. This is an 

*	 Sivapprakāsa Swāmikaḷ: Paṉuval Thiraṭṭu, v. 30.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 143

enjoyable expression that reveals dexterous felicity in the 
wielding of words. The word ‘antham’ stands for ‘end’ and 
the one who causes the end is ‘anthakan’ which is another 
name for Yama. A jiva cannot attain Siva-hood except by the 
loss of the ego-self. The delusion of the specious assumption 
of what is not the Self as Self plunges one into the grievous 
cycle of births and deaths. It is worthwhile to realise that the 
death of the ego-self, the individuality that triggers off this 
misconception, is the gaining of the Self, the Bliss of the 
Beatitude beyond name and form, the sole panacea for the 
torment of transmigration.

When the word ‘andhaka’ is considered in the light of 
its other meaning “a blind person” and taking the phrase 
“giving Thyself in exchange (nondiḍādu tandu)” as it is 
in the text, a different idea emerges. “That I may not be 
drowned in the sea of sorrow caused by the trammels of 
transmigration and that I may not, to attain Thee, suffer from 
the observance of rigorous austerities and hard penance, 
Thou bestowed in a trice the Impartite Atman, the ocean 
of nectarine Bliss Supreme, saturated with Grace on me as 
my rightful recompense. But in exchange, Thou claimed me 
who, devoid of any worth either here or hereafter, is but the 
circumscribed ego – the product of delusion and the cause 
of all ills. Hence Thou art verily a blind one, meaning one 
who is indiscreet and unwise.” How? Tirukkuṟaḷ provides 
the answer in the following couplet:

Pēdai-mai eṉba-doṉḏṟu yādu-eṉiṉ ēdam-koṇḍu
Ūdi-yam pōha viḍal”*

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 831.



144 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Dost thou want to know what folly is? It is throwing 
away which is profitable and holding fast to that 
which is hurtful.
The composer of Tiruvāchakam also addresses Lord 

Siva in a similar vein marvelling at the strange nature of 
Siva:

Tantadu uṉtaṉ-ṉaik koṇ-ḍadu eṉ taṉ-ṉai
Saṅkarā ārkolō caturar
Andam-oṉḏṟu illā āṉandam peṯ-ṯṟēṉ
Yādu-nī peṯ-ṯṟadu-oṉḏṟu eṉ-pāl*
What Thou hast given is Thee; What Thou hast 

gained is me. 
O Sankara! Who is the knowing one? 
I have obtained the rapturous bliss that knows no end
Yet now, what one thing hast Thou gained from me?

63.	 Nōk-kiyē karudi-mey tāk-kiyē pak-kuvam
		  Ākki-nī yāṇḍaruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
(Marking me as Thy target out of Thy grace so that 

I may experience the descent of Grace (Sakti nipātham), 
ripening me by Thy gracious look, by (gracious) thought 
and by (gracious) touch, render me a slave fit for Thy 
Holy Feet and be pleased to rule over me graciously.
Commentary: 

The three variants of initiation highly celebrated are 
mentioned together. They are chakshu dīksha – initiation by 

*	 Māṇikkavāchakar, Tiruvāchakam, 22-Kōyil Tiruppadikam, v. 10.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 145

gracious look, bhava dīksha – initiation by gracious thought 
and hasta dīksha – initiation by gracious touch called “aruḷ 
vaḍivāl tākkal” – to touch by the form of grace and hasta 
masthaka saiyogam (to touch the head of the disciple by 
the hand of the Gracious Form). [“Gracious Form” refers to 
the Divine frame of the Guru which the Lord embodies out 
of His benign and profound grace to bestow Grace on His 
devotees. Hence the limbs and lineaments, all of the Guru 
are but Grace, Pure Awareness]. ‘Kaivalya Navaneetam’, a 
famous spiritual treatise spells out these efficacious methods 
comparing them to the way fish, tortoise and birds hatch their 
eggs respectively. While fish are said to hatch their eggs by 
mere look, tortoise by mere thought and birds by brooding 
(physical contact), Bhagavan imploring Arunachala says 
“As I lead the list of those least qualified and competence 
not being my forte, merely one method of initiation will not 
suffice to enable me attain Union with Thee; hence Thou 
must make me ripen and empower me with competence 
by adopting all the three in Unison” and beseeches Him 
“Blessing me with Thy gracious look, thought and bestowing 
Thy physical touch of grace on me, fortify me with fitness 
and competence (nōkkiyē karudi mey tākkiyē pakkuvam 
ākki)”. It would do well to stand instructed that the 
thought and all the limbs of the Perceptor Supreme – 
like eyes, hands and feet – are all Grace personified, the 
form of Pure Chaitanya that is Pure Awareness. Probably 
this is what is implied by the esoteric content of the couplet 
by Tiruvalluva Nayanar that goes thus: 

Uṟutōṟu uyir-taḷirp-pat tīṇḍalāl pēdaikku 
Amizhdiṉ iyaṉḏṟaṉa tōḷ.



146 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Of ambrosia are the arms of my artless damsel 
formed; for their very touch reviveth my dying 
limb.* 
“āṇḍaruḷal (Graciously govern over me)”: Refers to the 

act of extirpating totally the fictitious fraud of the roguish 
selfhood or individuality of the goblin ego and uniting it 
with Siva Swarupa that forever naturally and spontaneously 
simply abides as the form of Self and shines as the Awareness 
of Pure Being. It is an idiom and tradition in the language 
of duality to refer to Siva Swarupa out of reverence as the 
“Feet twain of Siva”.
A.I.F.A.:

Arunachala, ripen me, the incompetent one by thinking 
of me like a tortoise, by looking at me like a fish and by 
touching me like a bird and graciously govern me.

The three types of initiation by bhava (thought), chakshu 
(eyes) and sparsa (touch) are mentioned respectively. They 
may occur either in unison or separately. Since initiation by 
thought is of significant consequence, when the other two 
ways of initiation happen independently, they will invariably 
be accompanied by thought in a subtle way.

“Ripening (pakkuvamādal)” is like the egg, breaking 
away to become the young one, the jiva with the ego 
sundered becomes the form and nature of the Self. “To 
govern (āḷal)” is to render the ego void. The revelation of 
the eternally experienced Self, obstructed all along by the 
vile ego, with the removal of that obstacle shines now in its 

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 1106.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 147

natural effulgence. Since this is not one that is gained anew 
“to govern (āḷal)” is simply to bestow Grace.

The illumined Teacher abiding in his natural unbroken 
state of Self – his real nature – free from dualistic perception 
before his disciple like Lord Dakshinamoorthy is in truth 
the real initiation more eloquent than any other is the view 
with which Bhagavan concurred; yet with due reverence 
to the traditions upheld by the Sastras, he bespeaks so in 
this verse. Bhagavan who did not know or have any other 
external Guru except the Lord obtained not any of these 
initiations from Him. However, he was “instructed without 
being instructed” by the Lord – the Self-God – abiding as 
Awareness in his heart and was by His Grace established in 
“nishtai or the state of Self-absorption” – the blessed state 
of Bliss. This Text of Hymns was composed by him only 
years later. 

The question “what then is the need to hail and hymn 
even after the attainment of Samadhi?” may well arise. 
It was not to achieve something which in his opinion still 
remained unachieved by him. It was out of boundless 
compassion towards all beings who too, worshipping His 
Feet seeking His Grace as the Sole refuge, may, like him 
be Delivered. This is made clear in the Preface as well.

Kaivalya Navaneetham corroborates this:
Nallavaṉē kēḷ ulagupakāra jñāṉigaḷ vivahāram,
Alladu vēṟoru peṟumpēṟum ilai adaṉāl piṇiyumilai
Oh, the good one! Hear me further, the activities of 
the sage are solely for the uplift of the world. He 
does not stand to lose or gain anything.



148 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

The Form of Transcendence, Arunachala, as Guru, the 
embodiment of Grace ripens the devotees who worship Him 
with competence and claims them.

64.	 Paṯṯṟi-māl viḍan-talai uṯ-ṯṟiṟu muṉam-aruḷ 
		  Paṯ-ṯṟiḍa aruḷ-puri Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Ere the deadly venom infused by the maya serpent 

mounts to my head culminating in death, bestow Thy grace 
that I, partaking the nectar of Thy grace may survive.
Commentary: 

In line with the interpretation of the phrases handled “māl 
viḍam” “as the deadly venom of maya” and “iṟumuṉam” 
“before death overtakes me”, “aruḷ paṯṟiḍa” is explained as 
“to survive, partaking the ambrosia of Thy Grace”.

‘aruḷ paṯṯṟiḍal’ (to hold on to Thy Grace) is elucidated 
as follows: When death doth approach – and exit from the 
world is imminent – instead of perishing in languor due 
to inadvertent forgetfulness of Arunachala, let the life 
ebb out of the body with the inner awareness of the heart 
holding on firmly to Arunachala as the Atman within. 
This is facilitated only by the prior and solid inherence 
in the experience of the natural State called firm jnana. 
When the life-breath is about to depart with creeping loss of 
consciousness due to the fatal poison mounting to the head, 
there is absolutely nothing that can be accomplished by any. 
Shutting the stable after the horse has been stolen is of no 
avail. Hence men of wisdom anticipate and erect dams well 
ahead of the onrush of floods. While the faculties like the 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 149

instruments and the organs of action and perception are 
intact and retain the power of consciousness, ere they go 
wayward losing their moorings, one should be redeemed 
by holding on firmly and with all might to Arunachala 
as the sole refuge. In this verse while the trivial samsara 
appearing in nescience due to the veiling power of ‘maya’ 
is personified as ‘deadly venom, (māl viḍam), its antidote, 
namely, the redeeming Grace has not been stated as the 
“nectar of Grace (aruḷ amudam).” Hence this is a mixed 
metaphor wherein the figure of speech is applied only in 
part.
A.I.F.A.:

Arunachala, ere I die, overtaken by the poison of 
delusion that has gripped me mounts to my head bestow Thy 
Grace that I may, securing the nectar of Thy Grace, consume 
the same and live. “The delusion (maruḷ)” that is spoken of 
here is “the ego”. Since the ego with its susceptibility to 
mistake as ‘Real’ or “True content” the illusory spectacle 
of the world appearing in the void like a mirage in a desert, 
is the cause of one falling into the birth-begetting ocean of 
samsara, it is called “delusion (maruḷ)”.

Poruḷ allavaṯ-ṯṟaip poruḷ-eṉḏṟu uṇarum
Maruḷāṉā māṇāp piṟappu.*
The befuddlement of taking the chaff for the corn, 
the unreal for the Real, plunges one into ceaseless 
births.
Those afflicted with delusion are as certain to lose their 

natural state of living in the Self – the Bliss of Peace – as 

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 351.



150 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

those betaken with poison to perish. Only when the jiva, 
instead of abiding in the Heart, his source, arises in the form 
of ego, alienating himself from the Self and that ego mounts 
to the head, the universe with its moving and unmoving 
forms appears and whirls him round and round in it, robbing 
him of peace. Similarly the poisonous venom causes ruin 
only after it mounts to the head. The phrase “mounting to 
head (talai uṯṯṟu)” is employed to provide both the imports. 
Since delusion has been compared to poison, the delusion 
that has become deeply entrenched is spoken of as “having 
galloped to the head.” Whatever has to be experienced is 
to be experienced while one is awake and not asleep for 
sleep negates the possibility of such experience. The truth 
of the Self, one’s reality, must be known before one perishes 
if salvation is to be achieved. Knowing the Self after death 
is an impossibility. Hence the usage “before it causes death 
(iru muṉam)”. “That I may take the nectar of Thy Grace 
and live (aruḷ paṯṯṟiḍa)” stands for the attainment of the 
experience of the Self. As the Lord is but the Self and 
Grace, Divine Grace is but the experience of being the Self. 
As delusion was termed as ‘poison’, its antidote ‘Grace’ is 
personified as ‘nectar’. While the antidote to the poison of 
delusion is ‘True Awareness’, why was Grace called ‘nectar’ 
and not the ‘True Awareness?’ The experience of being the 
Self called ‘Grace’ is but ‘True Awareness’ indeed. The 
jivas before the descent of grace, mistake themselves for 
the insentient body and its origin and subsidence for birth 
and death and superimposing all of these on the Self wallow 
in the maladies of transmigration. After taking refuge in 
His Grace, rid of that confusion, they, realising themselves 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 151

as the indestructible Self wipe off all their miseries. As 
abidance as the immortal Self happens by His Grace, Grace 
is called ‘nectar’. “That I may not perish with the poison 
of ego mounting to my head, bestow Thy Grace that I may 
drink the nectar of Self Experience – the Bliss of Serene 
Peace” – is the content.

When the venomous poison of the snake, namely, the 
desire and thirst triggered by the five senses grips and 
mounts to the head, the jivas are rendered impotent unable 
to accomplish anything unless they are already bestowed 
with grace. Hence he prays to the Lord to rid his mind of 
delusion so that he too, like them may not come to nothing.

65.	 Pārt-taruḷ māl-aṟap pārt-tilai yeṉiṉ-aruḷ
		  Pār-uṉak kār-solvar Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Pray, do redeem me by the sidelong glance of Thy 

Grace and rid me of my mental delusion which is maya 
embodied. If Thou Thyself failest to do so, who else, in 
this world, interceding on my behalf, can acquaint Thee 
with my predicament?
Commentary: 

Nescience, mental delusion, maya and illusion are 
all synonyms. The dense darkness of nescience or maya 
vanishes out of existence in toto and in no time by the 
gracious glance of Arunachala – the Sun of Pure Jnana. This 
explains the usage “pārttaruḷ mālaṟa” (Do look and bestow 
Thy grace that the illusory knowledge of my deluded mind 
be destroyed). A mother, whose endearing solicitude for her 



152 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

cherished child is spontaneous and natural, ranks first and 
foremost, outdoing others among those who care for the 
child. When she faileth to do so even those who draw her 
attention to her neglect deem it a superfluity. “Arunachala, 
the rarest of the rare in the world, with love far greater than 
that of a mother who, with great alacrity and solicitude, 
feeds her infant, Thou art the real mother of beings on 
whom Thou bestowest the infinite fullness of Thy loving 
Grace. Wert Thou not to bless me with Thy grace, there is 
none in the world who would beseech Thee on my behalf 
espousing my cause.” Hence the expression “If Thou dost 
not bless me with Thy look of grace, who else is there in 
the world to intercede on my behalf? (pārttaruḷ pārttilai eṉiṉ 
aruḷ pār uṉakku ār solvar?).” As bestowal of grace is but 
His very nature, the faith of Bhagavan that Arunachala’s 
bestowal of solicitous grace on him will be spontaneous 
and instantaneous is boundless. This act of Arunachala, like 
the unremitting love of a mother for her child, is innate and 
natural and is not the result of mediation by another on his 
behalf, is the line of thought.
A.I.F.A.:

“Arunachala! If Thou dost not bestow Thy glance so as 
to intercept and sunder my madness for Thee which Thou 
inspired in me by Thy Glance of Grace in my Heart, who 
else is there in this wide world to speak to Thee on my 
behalf? As there is none Thou Thyself knowing that bestow 
Thy Grace on me.”

That the unquenchable longing for the Lord springs not 
but for His Grace he says, “Do behold and bestow grace so 
that the delusion is sundered (pārttaruḷ mālaṟa).” As long 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 153

as two entities (he and the Lord) were there the desire for 
and to delight the ‘other’ remained. Hence the passionate 
desire and longing for the Lord sprang up in him, and the 
overwhelming upsurge of that longing made him become 
Him with self-loss. Henceforth there was no ‘other’ and 
there was no need to delight the ‘other’ and no room for the 
longing either. Hence with the intent, “with the self rendered 
naught, let me remain as Thou” he says “destroy the delusion 
– the self (mālaṟa)”. Revealing the certain confidence that 
the Lord, on His own, will bestow His look of Grace on 
Him that he cherishes in his heart he states, “If Thou dost 
not thus bestow Thy look (pārttilaiyeṉiṉ).” And in such an 
event as there is none to espouse his cause, he, out of pity 
for the state of ruination that would be his lot says, “Who 
will explain my plight to Thee (pār uṉakkār solvar).”

It is true indeed that Para Bhakti or Supreme Bhakti is 
to remain in sadbhava (in the mere awareness of Being) 
transcending all ‘bhavas’ or stances. But that is achievable 
only by the strength of the bhava. Hence “destroy my 
delusion (mālaṟa)” may also be interpreted as “Bless me 
that I may be filled with passionate longing.” The desire that 
seizes one at times and releases at other times is the cause of 
mental agitation. But unshakeable firmness of desire begets 
serene peace and calls for no mental movement. Hence 
while the former is misery, the latter is bliss. Bhagavan 
through this verse prays to Arunachala that between the twin 
looks of Grace that He wields towards Him, just as by one of 
which He caused in him the disease of desire, the gateway 
to misery, He must now, by the other as the antidote to that 
disease graciously bestow on him the Bliss of Perfect Peace. 
This reminds us of the couplet of Thiruvalluvar



154 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Iru-nōkku ivaḷuṇ-kaṇ uḷḷadu oru-nōkku 
Nōy-nōkku oṉḏṟu-an-nōy marundu.*
A double witchery have glances of her liquid eye;
One glance is glance that brings me pain; the other 
heals again.
Further if “mālaṟa pārttaruḷ” is taken as a separate 

sentence, in itself, one meaning would be “Behold me with 
Thy gracious Glance so that the delusion of my ego be made 
extinct.”

The yearning for grace is to feel the passionate longing 
for the Lord wanting in intensity and to pray for augmenting 
it more and more till there is no room for it to be contained. 
Then, it by itself subsides and gets extinguished. Hence 
“to destroy the delusion (mālaṟa)” in both the sense of “for 
the longing to increase” and “for the longing to be made 
extinct” the same identity of thought runs through and there 
is no contradiction between the two.

Behold (pār): The Lord is but Grace. He is addressed 
as ‘Grace’ which is His Name. Hence “Oh Grace, look at 
me,” can also be stated. In the earlier verse he prayed for 
His Grace to transform the poison of his delusion. Here to 
ensure such bestowal, he appeals to His sympathy drawing 
attention to his impoverished state in the event of His failure 
to remove his delusion as “there is none on earth who out of 
pity for him would speak to the Lord on his behalf.”

“If thou dost not bestow Thy glance and render naught 
my delusion, lo and behold, is this all the grandeur of the 
nature of Thy Grace? Who can draw Thy attention to it?” 
can be proffered as another reading.

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 1091.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 155

66.	 Pittu-viṭ ṭuṉai-nēr pittaṉ āk-kiṉai-aruḷ
		  Pittam teḷi-marun daruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou, freeing me of the madness for the excessive 

enjoyment of the sense objects of the world of maya that 
constitutes the travails of transmigration, made me yearn 
madly for the attainment of Thy Pure Atman (sans world, 
sans objects, but the Bliss Beyond, transcending name 
and form). Thou, who caused this madness seize me must, 
indeed, graciously bestow on me a cure also that frees me 
of this madness and also makes me aware of the Truth.
Commentary: 

“Madness (pittu)” stands for the delusion of the world, 
an intemperate passion for the objects of the ephemeral 
world. The cause of this ‘madness’ is the perverse 
perception of mistaking the mere appearance for the Truth 
or the shadow for the substance. A madman (pittan) is one 
seized with insane passion. Excepting a jnani – a jnana 
siddha (ever anchored in jnana – the only siddhi in truth), 
by virtue of having obtained jnana – who abides ever in 
the state of tranquil quiescence with the mind annihilated 
– all the rest, in truth, are insane in one way or the other. 
People who wallow in the quagmire of mental delusion and 
yearn to be freed of it with the proverbial prudence of using 
a thorn to dig out the other injurious one embedded (in the 
foot), holding on to the divine madness for Arunachala, 
Awareness embodied, seek to get rid of their mad craze 
for the world. Bhagavan in the line “freeing me from the 
passion for sense objects made me mad for union with Thee 



156 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

(“pittu viṭṭu uṉainēr pittaṉ ākkiṉai)” reveals that Arunachala 
out of His infinite Compassion for him transformed him into 
a mad one like Himself. Like the long pole that turns the 
corpse in the fire of the funeral pyre is itself consumed 
by the same fire after it accomplishes its work, the divine 
madness for Arunachala, after destroying its enemy, the 
madness for the objects of the world, itself quietens and 
resolves as Self. The state of ‘uṉmāta’ (acute madness or 
intoxication) that is experienced prior to the realization of 
jnana or Supreme Devotion (both of which shine as one in 
their essence for, in both, the individual disappears in the 
Self, the luminous Awareness, Pure Being which is both 
Grace and God) is what is mentioned here. Bhagavan here 
pleads to Arunachala, “Thou who caused the madness for 
Thee to seize me, must Thyself graciously bestow on me 
the antidote for that madness too, through your Swarupa 
Sakshatkara – the Realization of the Union of the Self with 
Thee” the truth of which is vouchsafed by Tiruvalluvar too 
in one of his couplets that runs thus:

Piṇikku marundu piṟamaṉ aṇiyizhai
Taṉnōykkut tāṉē marundu.*
The cure of all disease doth always lie in some other 
thing than that which causeth it: but the pang this 
damsel causeth, she alone can heal. 
Bhagavan voices the same idea in verse 8 of Padikam 

(The decad on Arunachala) too in the following lines 
“paittiyam paṯṟip payaṉaṟum eṉakkuṉ padamuṟum 
arumarundu aruḷvāy”, “Seized by the madness of love 

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 1102.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 157

for Thee, I have not gained the fruit of that madness, the 
Transcendental Experience. Grant me that rare cure-all, the 
elixir of Eternal Bliss, that I may attain the state of Union 
with Thee.” Bhagavan brings to the fore, with great clarity, 
in the light of his experience that the Real Truth of our 
nature, that never gets separated from us but forever from 
eternity remains and shines as the Non-Dual, Impartite, Sat-
Chit (Being, Awareness) form of Arunachala can be realised 
by transforming and transcending the delusion that has 
cropped up in between. Hence what Bhagavan calls here 
as the “cure for madness (pittam teḷi marundu)” is the True 
Awareness that is His gift of Grace. Once that Awareness 
dawns the desire to gain Arunachala anew that sprang up 
earlier, not only gets sundered but the very thought of having 
gained Him afresh will not arise either. If it does, having 
been separated from Arunachala prior to gaining Him would 
become true. But there never was a parting from Arunachala 
as was mentioned earlier.

Bhadda-ṉāṉ eṉṉu-maṭṭē bhanda-mutti chin-taṉai-kaḷ
Bhaddaṉ ār-eṉḏṟu-taṉṉaip pārk-kuṅ-gāl - cidda-māy
Nitta-muttaṉ ṯāṉiṟka niṟkādēṟ bhanda-chindai 
Mukti-chindai muṉ-ṉiṟkumō. *
So long as the thought ‘I am bound’ remains, thought 
of either bondage or release (mukti) will be present. 
When one enquires of oneself ‘who is the one so 
bound’ the Self, Eternal, Ever-free, reveals Itself 
and remains. The thought of bondage vanishes and 
along with it disappears the thought of Release too.

*	 Sri Bhagavan, Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu, v. 39. 



158 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

The apparent notions of “separation” and “reunion” 
are mere fictitious projections of the ego-mind which itself 
is an illusory entity. This truth, in its entirety can well be 
perceived from Verse 86 of Arunachala Akshara Mana 
Malai where Bhagavan says: “mōham tavirttuuṉ mōhamā 
vaittumeṉ mōham tīrāy” 

“Eradicating all my hitherto delusive attachments to the 
ephemeral world, Thou, by creating an insatiable longing 
for Thee hast bound me securely to Thee. Why then hast 
Thou not removed even that, Arunachala?”

Sekkizhar, the author of Periya Puranam, lauds those 
who have ascended to that elevated state as “Men of proud 
distinction of not desiring even mukti (Freedom)” (‘vīḍum 
vēṇḍā viṟaliṉ viḷaṅgiṉār’).

It is of further interest to note that the contents of the 
verses 73 and 84 reflect the fruit of this prayer and are in 
perfect consonance with this verse. They are: “poḍiyāl 
mayakkieṉ bhōdattaip paṟittu uṉ bhōdattaik kāṭṭiṉai” (73) 

“Like a deft magician stupefying with a magic powder 
the minds of people, Thou, stupefying me with the wonder 
powder of Thy holy Grace, took away my jivabodham and 
revealed Sivabodham, the experience of Siva to me.”

“maimayal nīttu aruḷ maiyiṉāl uṉadu uṇmai 
vacamākkiṉai” (84)

“Removing the dense darkness of my mind’s delusion 
by the magic power of Thy grace, Thou hast made it into 
Supreme Form of Thee, the Content of Truth.”

A.I.F.A.:
This verse can be read with profit in conjunction with 

verse 40.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 159

Jñāṉam illā-duṉ āsai-yāṯ ṯṟaḷar-vaṟa 
	 Jñāṉam terit-taruḷ Aruṇāchalā
“Despite the inadequacy of my knowledge, the insatiable 

longing to attain Thee has wearied my soul. Wean me from 
this worry by enlightening me with the knowledge of Self-
enquiry that will make me gain Thee.”

The content of that verse is stated here differently 
couched in the flavour of Bhakti.

67.	 Bīdiyil uṉaic-chār bīdiyil eṉaic-chēr 
		  Bīdi-uṉ taṉakkēṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Why art Thou seized with fear to become one with me 

when I, free from fear, am emboldened to unite with Thee 
who art fearless?
Commentary: 

The prayerful supplication to Arunachala here is to 
unite with him without fear. Presuming that the slight delay 
on the part of Arunachala in embracing him the moment 
he thought of uniting with Him, is due to His developing 
trepidation, he superimposes ‘fear’ on Arunachala. Since 
there is no occasion or need for it he reassures Him in a 
conciliatory note “Fearless art Thou. I, unafraid, seek to join 
Thee. But Thou developing fear to join me is by all means 
improbable.” Probably this is in a lighter tone of banter.

The cause of all fear is the appearance of duality as ‘the 
other’ – the work of maya. The Non-Dual Truth of Arunachala 
– the condensed form of Pure jnana – the Self-sovereignty 
is the reason for His freedom from fear. Bhagavan’s 



160 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

fearlessness springs from his faith in and devotion, total and 
supreme, to Arunachala – the embodiment of Grace – and 
who is the haven of refuge for all the jivas. Appar too, in his 
Thēvāram, eulogises Siva as “bhīdiyil uṉai-dhīraṉ tīttiraḷaṉ... 
vīraṉ vīzhi mizhalai vihirdaṉē” meaning “Thou – free of 
fear – the valiant hero the mass of fire… the brave and 
wonderful hero of Tiruvīzhimizhalai”. Transposing the 
words different interpretations can be provided as follows:

“chār bhīdiyil – fearless to join Thee” and
“cher bhīdi – fear to join”
While the above reading renders the meaning “I, who 

am fearless in uniting with Thee”, another, when the words 
are taken as “bhīdiyil uṉaicār eṉai” would emerge as under: 
“Though I sought to join Thee shrinking in shock and fear of 
the world reeling with dualities and multiplicities”. And the 
other import suggested is: “Though I was seized with fear 
regarding the world of pluralities, I have no fear concerning 
you.” The Seer who knows his nature as Pure and True 
Awareness, vast as the sky and bright as the sun, has no fear.

68.	 Pul-laṟi vē-durai nal-laṟi vē-durai 
		  Pul-liḍa vē-yaruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Pray, enlighten me as to what is it that is termed as 

‘lowly’ and ‘false’ awareness? Pray what is that which 
is termed as ‘beneficial’ and ‘true’ awareness? Pray do 
bestow Thy Grace and help me acquire that true awareness 
bereft of the wrong one.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 161

Commentary: 
‘pullaṟivu’ is a little or limited knowledge, the 

knowledge of the ego (jivahood), objectified knowledge or 
denotative knowledge (knowledge of men and matters of 
the world, differentiated knowledge or knowledge based on 
triputi (triad) like knower, known and knowledge). This is 
but mentally conceived, deceptive and deluded and not real. 
This meretricious knowledge, like the dream-stuff encircles 
by guile, whirls round the jivas who are unaware of the truth, 
in the cycle of samsara unceasingly – an expansive sport 
of maya. This deceptive knowledge – the cause of endless 
torment of transmigration – spells doom for all living beings.

Since “nallaṟivu” (beneficial knowledge) – real 
awareness (Swarupa jnanam – the knowledge of the Self; 
tattuva jnanam – the knowledge of Reality of essence of 
things), Gnosis of God (Siva jnana or Brahma jnana) is 
bereft of duality which is imagined by the mind It blossoms 
as one’s real nature, shining in tranquil Peace solely as Being 
and Awareness and is termed as kaivalya jnana (knowledge 
of Liberation) and Kevala jnana (Pure knowledge). In the 
parlance of Vedantins this goes by the name ‘Atma-jnana’ 
and in the conclusively established perception of the 
Absolute it is called Total Awareness (Sarvajnatva – Infinite 
knowledge) or Omniscience.

The glory of this knowledge is sung in Ātma Vidyā 
Kīrttaṉam (song on Self-Knlowledge) which underscores 
the indispensability of this knowledge. 

Taṉṉai aṟidal-iṉḏṟip piṉṉai edaṟikil-eṉ
Taṉṉai aṟin-diḍil-piṉ eṉṉai uḷa-daṟiya?



162 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Of what avail is knowing things other than the Self? 
And the Self being known what other thing is there 	
	 to know?
The imagined mental awareness that is conceived by 

the unreal and circumscribed ego, of objects and matters 
as the ‘other’, objectified, or denotative, fragmented and 
differentiated is called ‘pullaṟivu’ – lowly and unreal 
knowledge. And this ‘pullaṟivu’ so gained is the cause of 
bondage. ‘Nallaṟivu’ or True or Real awareness is Infinite 
Awareness of the Self bereft of the denotative knowledge 
accumulated by the vaunting ego. This, affording solid 
and benign succour to the jivas, ferries them across the 
grievous ocean of transmigration to the shore of mukti, the 
form of Beatific Bliss. This is authenticated by Bhagavan 
in verse 13 of Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu (Forty Verses on Reality), 
“jñāṉamām tāṉē mey nāṉāvā jñāṉam ajñāṉamām.” “The 
Self that is Awareness, that ALONE is true. The knowledge 
that is conscious of the plurality of appearances is ignorance 
and unreal.” ‘Pulludal’ is to merge in unison. To merge 
in unison with ‘nallaṟivu’ means even as a hailstone 
becomes one with water as water, the ego gets dissolved 
as the form of non-dual Self at the Feet of Arunachala, 
the Ocean of unbroken Bliss of Awareness, the very Form 
of Immaculate Purity, unflawed by any defilement. Here 
attention may be drawn to verse 8, 21 and 22 of Forty verses 
on Reality.

Meyp-poruḷiṉ uṇmaiyil-taṉ uṇmai-yiṉai ōrndo-ḍuṅgi 
Oṉḏṟu-dalē uṇmaiyil kāṇal uṇar. (8)
Talai-vaṟ kāṇal-evaṉ ūṇādal kāṇ. (21)
And 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 163

Madi-yiṉai uḷḷē maḍakki ‒ padi-yil 
padit-tiḍu-dal aṉḏṟip Padi-yai madi-yāl 
madit-tiḍu-dal eṅṅgaṉ madi.  (22)
To see one’s true Self in the Absolute, to merge unto 
It and abide as one with It, is true knowledge or 
darsan. (8)
Who is the Seer of God? How to see Him? To see 
Him is to be Consumed by Him. (21)
Other than turning the mind within, lodging it firmly 
and uniting it with the Lord, can anyone measure 
the Lord by the mind? (22)
The perception of Arunachala – the Brahman – by 

the inner organ is indeed ‘knowing’ Brahman. Knowing 
Brahman is to become Brahman. And becoming Brahman is 
to abide as Brahman.*

Verses 34 and 35 of Forty Verses on Reality contain the 
upadesa of Bhagavan as follows:

Eṉḏ-ṟum evark-kum iyal-bāy uḷa-poru-ḷai
oṉḏ-ṟum uḷat-tuḷ uṇarndu-nilai (34)
“Abide in the Heart with an inward-turned and 
subsiding mind, know by experience and gain 
perfect identity with that Reality that shines always 
in the Heart as one’s own nature.”

chit-tamāy uḷporu-ḷait tērn-dirut-tal siddhi (35)
Knowing and Being in Atman, “That which is”, that 
forever and everywhere shines as the attained reality 
is truly the perfect siddhi (attainment) indeed.

*	 The actual words once bestowed by Bhagavan are: “To see is 
to know; to know is to become, and to become is to be.”



164 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

uḷla poruḷ – True Reality, forever resides in the Heart. 
Realizing it, abide as That.

uḷḷadu – the true reality
In short, abiding as God is true darsan or knowledge 

of God. Brahma nishtai – abidance as Brahman is Brahma 
jnana (Knowledge of Brahman) is the quintessence of this 
teaching.

69.	 Bhū-maṇa mā-maṉam pūraṇa maṇaṅ-koḷap 
		  Pūraṇa maṇam-aruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Bestow grace on me so that my mind, now impregnated 

with impressions (vasanas) of the world may, rid of them, 
gain the vasana (fragrance) of Brahman that is worldless 
and grant me the knowledge of union of Atman and 
Brahman.
Commentary: 

Vasanas or impressions are of three kinds. They are 
imprints caused in the mind by experiences of a) the world 
b) the body and c) the Sastras. Here ‘bhūmaṇam’ (smells or 
vasanas of the world) includes the other two as well. The 
phrase ‘pūraṇa maṉam’ (Brahma Vasana or fragrance of 
Brahman) occurring twice in the couplet stands exclusively 
for the experience of Brahman. ‘pūraṇam’ is the Whole 
or Infinite or all-embracing Brahman, ‘maṉam’ is union 
in wedlock or to mingle non-dually. Here it means the 
knowledge of the union of Brahman and Atman.

In the essence of their being, the Lord and the jiva are 
in substance one. Stita prajnai is the steady state of Being-



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 165

Awareness that is made solid and firm by the experience of 
the Atman which is but the elimination of the ego and which 
shines as Siva in the Heart, the living shrine. Since the 
experience of Brahman cannot dawn but for the knowledge 
of the union of Brahman and Atman, Bhagavan beseeches 
for ‘pūraṇa maṇam koḷap pūraṇa maṇam aruḷ’ “Grant me 
that I may gain the fragrance of Brahman (the objectless 
Awareness) by uniting me in wedlock with the Perfect 
Whole – Brahman.”

The segment ‘aksharamanam” contained in the title 
of this work (maṇam – marriage; akshara – indestructible, 
eternal – meaning Union with the content of akshara, 
Arunachala Brahman or Eternity), provides precisely the 
same meaning as the phrase “pūraṇa maṇam (wedlock with 
the Whole)” of this verse. 

“Aruṇāchala varaṟku (the bridegroom Arunachala)” in 
the Invocatory verse, “bhūmaṇa māmaṉam” in this verse 
and “mālai aḷittu (offering the garland)” in the last verse are 
posited at the commencement, middle and conclusion of the 
Text respectively. These three verses, sui generis stand apart 
in respect of their special significance, providing the internal 
evidence for the text gaining its title Akshara Mana Malai.

(‘akshara’ in the title, ‘maṇa’ in this verse in the middle 
and ‘mālai’ in the concluding verse).



166 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

70.	 Peyar-niṉait tiḍavē piḍit-tizhut taṉai-yuṉ
		  Perumai-yār aṟivār Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
The moment Thy name ‘Arunachala’ flashed in my 

mind, Thou, that very moment, binding me with the 
cord of Thy grace, drew me to Thee. Who can gauge the 
grandeur of Thy grace?
Commentary: 

Bhagavan, with the intent to announce that “to think 
of His name is to think of His Real Form” employs the 
words “that very instant I thought of His name (‘peyar 
niṉaittiḍavē’).” In fact the two are non-different. (The 
name Arunachala, the mountain of magnetic pull, is 
identical with the Transcendent Awareness that He is). 
How, moved by divine compassion in response to his earlier 
prayers, Arunachala condescended to shower His grace on 
him by drawing Him to Himself like a magnet attracting 
iron filings, is revealed by the phrase “caught and drew 
me” (‘piḍittu izhuttaṉai’). It was a thought firmly inhered 
in, surcharged by a concentrated mind, ripened and matured 
over numerous births. The sublimity and the grandeur of 
Arunachala, the comprehension of which is beyond the 
ken of most, was known to Bhagavan as a matter of direct 
experience. That he could give expression to his experience 
in such words of wonder bespeaks of the perfect ease, 
receptivity and spontaneity with which he yielded himself 
to the play of Grace – profound and instantly forthcoming. 
Since Bhagavan records in Padikam, “Though never had I 
the faintest idea of thinking of Thee, drawing me with the 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 167

cord of Thy grace (iṟai uṉai niṉaiyum eṇṇamē naṇṇā eṉai 
uṉadu aruḷ kayiṯṟāl īrttu)”, it is clear that the benign grace 
was the reason, true and prime, for drawing him to Him. 
Until then the Lord was waiting for just an excuse. This is 
clear when he says “the moment I thought of Thee, Thou 
drew me (niṉaittiḍavē piḍittizhuttaṉai)”.

The experience of having been caught and drawn made 
him comprehend clearly the glory of His grace which is 
well nigh impossible for anyone to know except for His 
grace. Hence he wonders “Who can gauge Thy glory?” 
(uṉ perumaiyai yār aṟivār?). The prowess and greatness 
of Grace of Arunachala can be comprehended only by 
the supremely devout, those awakened souls revelling 
in the highest Bliss of ‘Self-relish’ in the state of total 
Quiescence in a dialogue with Grace – the Lord – 
in personal intimacy, in the vast Expanse of Pure 
Awareness — a prodigious occurrence beyond the ken 
of others — is the idea conveyed. Bhagavan, based on 
the might of the grace of Arunachala, in general, says 
“Thou dost root out and annihilate the ego of those who 
meditate on Thee in their hearts (‘Aruṇāchalameṉa ahamē 
niṉaippavar ahattai vēr aṟuppāy Aruṇāchalā’*) and based 
specially on the evidence of his own personal experience 
says “I had but thought of Thee as Arunachala. Ah, me! 
I was caught in the trap of Thy grace! Can the net of Thy 
grace ever fail (‘aruṇai eṉḏṟu eṇṇayāṉ aruṭkaṇṇi paṭṭēṉuṉ 
aruḷvalai tappumō?’†)

*	 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai, v.1.
†	 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai, v. 102.



168 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

This, read along with the preceding and succeeding 
verses, illustrates explicitly the rare and enormous 
fortune that the devotees stand to gain by the smarana 
(remembrance) of Arunachala.

71.	 Pēyt-taṉam viḍa-viḍāp pēyāp piḍit-teṉaip
		  Pēya-ṉāk kiṉai-yeṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
In order to release me from the ego-ghost, totally vile 

and mean, that never releases its clutches due to its nature 
of egoity, Thou, the mighty ghost of Brahman, beyond 
being exorcised (turning those possessed by Thee into 
Thine own form by consuming them), hast possessed me 
and changed me also into a ghost as well. Oh! What a 
wonder is this!
Commentary: 

The attitude of “I am the body” (dehoaham) which is 
the fragmented and circumscribed chit and jada, is termed 
“a ghost” and the attitude of ‘the Impartite, non-dual, Pure 
Awareness, Brahman I am’ (Brahmoaham) is the ghost, 
relentless and unexorcizable. The former is declared as 
“the formless ghost of the ego” (uruvaṯṟa pēyahandai*) 
having no form of its own in Verse 25 of the Forty Verses 
on Reality. When the mighty giant of a ghost possesses one, 
the numerous smaller ghosts that had held on to that person 
flee, unable to face it and hold their ground. Those ripe in 
devotion and jnana, unlike others without the inadvertence 

*	 Sri Bhagavan, Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu, v. 25.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 169

of their real nature, ever asleep and unconcerned to the 
matters of the world, live like mad men (Pittar) and ghosts 
(pēyar). Hence Bhagavan declares “thou hast turned me into 
a ghost” (pēyaṉākkiṉai).

Bhagavan, hailing and commending with fondness 
within, the greatness of Arunachala’s grace, pre-eminent, 
and special in turning him into one of His own tribe, without 
the distinction of being his equal or superior, wonders at the 
sublimity of His grace as “what a wonder is this?” (en?).
A.I.F.A.:

In order that the smaller ghost of egoity that has seized 
may release me, Thou, the mammoth ghost hast possessed 
me to quit me no more and hast changed me into one of 
Thine own tribe. What is the mystery of this act of Thine? 
Tell me, O Arunachala!

When a mammoth ghost takes hold of one, the smaller 
one that has possessed earlier lets go of its hold. This is made 
explicit in the phrase “that the ghost of ego may release me 
(pēyttaṉam viḍa).” That even if he were to shake himself 
loose of that ghost, that Brahman ghost never quits him is 
expressed in the phrase “Unrelenting ghost (viḍāppēy).” 
And “Thou made me into Thine own Form (Brahman)” is 
stated in the words “has turned me into a ghost of Thine own 
kind (pēyaṉākkiṉai).” He exclaims “What a wonder is this?” 
in “eṉ”? The ego, by itself though mighty, in the presence 
of the Lord becomes threadbare and relinquishes its hold. 
Since the Lord is the Self of all, He becomes the relentless 
Ghost. Earlier he says “Graciously enfold me in Thy firm 
embrace so that the attitude of duality such as ‘Thou’ and 
‘me’ may cease and depart totally from my heart and I may 
abide forever in the state of Bliss-filled Self.”



170 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Nī-nā ṉaṟap-puli nidaṅ-kaḷi maya-mā 
	 Niṉ-ḏṟiḍu nilai-yaruḷ Aruṇāchalā
The Lord is often referred to as “mahatbhutaha (the one 

who became the all)”

72.	 Paiṅ-koḍi yā-nāṉ paṯ-ṯṟiṉḍṟi vāḍāmal
		  Paṯ-ṯṟukkō ḍāyk-kā Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
That I, a young damsel with no subtle or firm 

knowledge, may not be distressed like a tender creeper 
withering away for want of a support to entwine on, 
graciously guard me with solicitude.
Commentary: 

The line “like a tender creeper withering away for want 
of a support (paiṅkoḍiyā nāṉ paṯṟiṉḏṟi vāḍāmal)” indicates 
that Bhagavan regarded himself as a young maiden pining 
away for want of a companion.

A supporting staff guards the tender creeper from 
wilting in heat and withering in wind. Bhagavan pleads to 
Arunachala, the Preceptor of knowledge (Jnana Guru) who 
claims sincere and simple folks by lighting up the flame of 
knowledge in them to consider him, bandied about by the 
power of maya and deluded and distressed by the afflictions 
of samsara, as His spouse and to protect and guard him with 
no reservation but with due liberty in the same way a post 
supports a tender creeper. Verse 79 that voices a similar plea 
with poignance is worth paying attention to:



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 171

“Guard me lest I flounder, storm-tossed like a ship 
without a helmsman. Be Thou the helmsman of my life and 
save me.”

Mīkāmaṉ illā-mal mākāṯ ṯṟalai-kalam 
	 Āhāmal kāt-taruḷ Aruṇāchalā.*

A.I.F.A.:
The striking similarity of Maṇivāchakar’s feelings 

compels our attention.
Kombar illāk koḍipōla alamandaṉaṉ kōmaḷamē,
Vembugiṉḏṟēṉai viḍudikaṇḍāy
Like climbing plant with no supporting bow,  
I wavering hung!
Lo, Tender one, me-trembling, Thou’st forsaken.

73.	 Poḍi-yāl mayak-kiyeṉ bhōdat-taip paṟit-tuṉ
		  Bhōdat-taik kāṭ-ṭiṉai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
As if stupefying one with the deft use of a magic 

powder, Thou, befuddling me, robbing me of my ‘jiva 
bodha’, laid bare Thy ‘Siva bodha’, the state of the 
experience of being Siva.
Commentary: 

Siva bodha and jiva bodha differ in the quality of Truth 
(satyam) they possess. While Siva bodha is the truth, the 
latter is one imagined in it. Hence both do not stand revealed 
*	 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai, v. 79.



172 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

at the same time, true to the old adage that says when a dog 
is seen, the stone out of which the form of a dog is carved 
will not be seen and vice versa.

Poṉ-ṉai maṟait-tadu poṉ-ṉaṇi pūḍaṇam
Poṉ-ṉiṉ maṟain-dadu poṉ-ṉaṇi pūḍaṇam
Taṉ-ṉai maṟait-tadu taṉ-kara ṇaṅgaḷān
Taṉ-ṉiṉ maṟain-dadu taṉ-kara ṇaṅgaḷē.
Marat-tai maṟait-tadu māmada yāṉai
Marat-tiṉ maṟain-dadu māmada yāṉai
Parat-tai maṟait-tadu pārmudaṟ bhūtam
Parat-tiṉ maṟain-dadu pārmudaṟ bhūtamē.*
It is the ornament that veils the gold
It is the gold that gets veiled in the ornament
It is the inner organs that veil the Self
It is the organs that get veiled in the Self.
And
Think of the wood in the wood elephant,
Image of the elephant fades away,
Think of the elephant
Image of the wood pales away;
Think of the elements five
Thought of Param fades away
Think of Param
Thought of the elements fades away.
When one of the two prevails, the other concealed, gets 

dissolved in the other and, becomes invisible. Therefore 
unless the imagined jivabodha becomes extinct, Sivabodha, 

*	 Tirumūlar’s Tirumandiram, 8th Tirumuṟai , 8-Parāvattai, 
v. 20-1.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 173

the substantive truth of jivabodha, does not shine. In order 
that the effulgence of Sivabodha may shine in him, Bhagavan 
claims that Arunachala, by the magic power of His grace, 
eliminated his jivabodha “robbing me of my jivabodham 
(en bodhattaip paṟittu)”. “This jivabodha that cannot be 
rendered existenceless by any known means, remained in 
me in the form of ego, the mental awareness entrenched 
deep in the firm conviction that ‘what if anybody instructs 
me on anything? Can my mind be deluded?’ Such a strong 
jivabodha of mine was mesmerised out of existence as if 
by the use of a magic powder.” Bhagavan, wondering at the 
novelty of the skilful, sanctified and flawless art of Divine 
Grace remarks: “Thou, creating a spell by the use of the 
magic powder (poḍiyāl mayakki)”. What he alludes to later 
in verse 89 as “who else, other than Thee, stupefying me 
and by stealth, unknown even to those close to me stole my 
mind away (yārum aṟiyādu eṉ madiyiṉai maruṭṭi evarkoḷai 
koṇḍadu)” refers to the same truth. ‘Powder (poḍi)’ 
means both a magic powder and the sacred ash (vibhūti) 
fortified by the chanting of appropriate mantras (sacred 
incantations). The manifold arts, the eight-fold siddhis, 
divine visions, the spectacle of the cosmic form witnessed 
either within or externally, all fall under the fold of ‘triputi’ 
(triad) like the seer, seen and the spectacle. Further, even 
the perceived objects are differentiated as ‘this’, ‘that’ and 
‘the one in between these two (udu).’ All these fragmented 
or compartmentalised knowledge, which are perceived 
and known, imagined on the unbroken non-dual Sat Chit 
Ananda – the Self – out of the inadvertent forgetting of one’s 
inherent unity by the circumscribed ego arising out of the 



174 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Self, is termed jivabodha – the differentiated knowledge. 
What is commonly referred to as “nescience” (ajnana) by 
the learned ones in the Sastras is this. But how? It is simply 
because “Truth is knowledge and knowledge is Truth.” The 
Supreme Truth is ‘what is’, i.e. ‘Being’. Awareness of ‘Being’ 
or ‘Truth’ is knowledge. Hence both are one and the same. 
Nescience or absence of knowledge and therefore absence of 
‘Being’ is what is untrue, false and void. Therefore all other 
objects excepting the two mentioned above are all “being-
less”. The things that we think as existing do not exist. The 
ego’s relative knowledge is but ignorance. 

Sivabodha is synonymous with Brahma jnana and Tattva 
Jnana (knowledge of the essential principle or axiological 
Truth). Knowledge (without the fragmented knowledge), 
indeed, remains with the elimination of the ego mind – a 
fraudulent appearance.

What shines with the annihilation of the mischievous 
and fiendish ego – a conceptual I, born in nescience – is 
the Transcendent Expanse of Pure Awareness devoid of any 
cognition. That supreme space bereft of any attribute is the 
formless Sivam shining as Self in the Self. That indeed is 
innate Pure jnana or Prajnana. Tiruvaḷḷuvar christens it as 
“Infinite Knowledge” or “Perfect Intelligence (Vālaṟivu)”. 
This state called ‘Bhuma’ is totally bereft of any differentiated 
appearance. (Bhuma is perfection where one does not see 
any other, hears nothing. It is as it is). Verse 13 of the ‘Forty 
verses on Reality’ highlights this Truth. “Atman that shines 
as Pure form of Chaitanya or Awareness alone is the Truth. 
The knowledge that knows the plurality of appearances is 
ajnana (jñāṉamām tāṉēmey nāṉāvā jñāṉam ajñāṉamām).” 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 175

The content of verse 84 of Akshara Mana Malai is in 
conformity with the content of this verse, and these two 
verses mutually bear out each other.

“Removing the dense darkness of my mind’s delusion 
by the magic power of Thy grace, Thou hast made it into the 
Supreme Form of Thee, the content of Truth. (maimayal ṉīttu 
aruḷ maiyiṉāl uṉadu uṇmai vasam ākkiṉai Aruṇāchalā)”.

The “jivabodha (eṉ bodham)” of this verse is the “dense 
darkness of nescience (mai mayal)” of the verse 84 and 
“Sivabodha (uṉ bodham)” of this couplet is the “form of 
Pure Knowledge (uṉadu uṇmai)” of verse 84.
A.I.F.A.:

Arunachala! Thou, casting a spell and stupefying me 
with Thy mesmerising Powder, hast robbed me of my bodha 
and in exchange revealed Thy bodha.

Arunachala effected the transformation of his state all 
on a sudden and in a trice like the unbelievable trick of a 
deft magician. Bhagavan describes this act as “befuddling 
the mind by the spell of a magic powder (poḍiyāl mayakki).” 
The powder has a reference to the sacred ash too. Bhagavan 
stayed immersed for long in the ocean of Supreme Bliss 
with the self sundered, an experience gifted to him by the 
Lord out of His munificent grace and causeless compassion. 
When he was brought to its surface for once, he, reminiscing 
the wondrous Grace of the Lord, bespoke Thus.

We may say that Bhagavan here metaphorically refers to 
Arunachala Himself as “the mesmerising Powder (chokkup 
poḍi)”.



176 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

74.	 Pōkkum varavu-mil podu-veḷi yiṉil-aruḷ
		  Pōrāṭ ṭaṅkāṭ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
(For those who have known and gained the Self, no 

more return from the Self back to samsara again) Free 
of ‘going’ and ‘coming’ in the Supreme Expanse of the 
Heart, graciously display, for my benefit, the battle play 
of the might of Divine Grace destroying the demoniac 
legion of the predilections like the ego of those who think 
of Thee.
Commentary: 

The state characterised by the absence of “coming and 
going” is Bhuma (Perfection, Absolute, Infinite) that is of 
the nature of the limitless expanse (poduveḷi) of the chit, 
since spaces, physical and mental, are subject to ‘coming 
and going’ (rising and setting). By “the all-pervading 
Supreme expanse bereft of coming and going (pōkkum 
varavumil poduveḷi)” is meant the extremely subtle space 
– and subtler than all the subtle spaces – the all-pervading 
Space of Awareness, Brahman, called Daharākāsā. This 
provides the raison d’être for the use of the term, “the 
all-pervading space (poduveḷi)” for, while the word ‘veḷi’ 
itself means ether or space – and though will satisfy the 
purpose – the use of the term ‘poduveḷi’ may appear 
superfluous, yet its use meets the occasion well, indeed. 
“The battle of Grace (aruḷ pōrāṭṭam)” stands for the 
battle between Sivabodha (the mass of divine Grace) and 
jivabodha (the ego, the mass of nescience). It is a war that 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 177

is being waged from eternity and it is Sivabodha that ever 
emerges the ultimate victor. (This must be truly understood 
as the war between the Devas and the demons, the divine 
and the devils). But it is forever the Divine Grace that shines 
wreathed in laurels of victory in the arena of this battle. 
Hence the battle is termed ‘the battle of Grace’. (Here by the 
term ‘battle ground’ is meant the locale of the Heart Lotus 
where the warfare of Grace is being fought. Is this not verily 
the Dharma Kshetra (the sacred field of righteousness of the 
Gita)? Here what is spoken of in ‘Kandhar Alaṅkāram’ by 
Sage Arunagirinatha is worth paying attention to wherein he 
says “The One free of coming and going keeps impacting 
me again and again (pōkkum varavum ... illādadoṉḏṟu vandu 
vandu tākkum”*). Bhagavan, desiring to see this spectacle, 
sweet and delightful to behold, like the one climbing atop a 
hill to have an hindrance-free, and clear view of the war of 
elephants on the plains below, was already firmly poised on 
the peak of the Hill of Arunachala, the Self, the condensed 
mass of Pure Non-dual jnana and already abiding in the 
state of quiescence – the farthest boundary of jnana – and 
beseeches Arunachala to display the spectacle. Probably, 
this is what is called Sakshi Vritti (witness mode) by 
knowledgeable elders.

*	 Kandhar Alaṅkāram, v. 73.



178 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

75.	 Bhau-dika mā-muḍal paṯ-ṯṟaṯṯṟu nāḷum-uṉ 
		  Bavisu-kaṇ ḍuṟa-varuḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Bestow Thy grace that I may, rid of my attachments to 

the gross or physical frame, behold ever the spectacle of 
Thy exalted and ubiquitous form beyond compare.
Commentary: 

Gross or physical (bhautikam) means association with 
the five gross elements called Pañca bhūtam. Attachment 
to the physical frame referred to by the words ‘uḍal paṯṟu’ 
in the verse means attachment born of the conception 
either as “I am the body” or “the body is mine” and other 
attachments, both internal and external, in the nature of I and 
mine. The singular “attachment” (instead of attachments) is 
used for both due to the commonality of their genre. The 
word “aṟal (to become non-existent)” means ‘to render it 
totally extinct’ either by contemplation or by the pursuit 
of enquiry. Though the gross body alone is meant by the 
word ‘bhautikamām uḍal’, the other two bodies, namely, the 
subtle and causal are also mentioned by the rule of “upa 
lakshana” (or synecdoche), whereby a part is mentioned 
for the whole. ‘bavisu’ connotes “the unbroken splendour 
of Thy Form – Being, Awareness, Bliss”. ‘Your splendour’ 
as in ‘uṉ bavisu’, is like referring to ‘Rāhu’s head’ for head 
is all that Rāhu possesses. Arunachala’s splendour is His 
Form – Satchitananda. ‘kaṇḍuṟal’ means “to behold and to 
unite” or “to know and to become”. “uḍal paṯṟaṯṟu nāḷum 
uṉ bavisu kaṇḍuṟa, (rid of the attachment to the body, to 
be unceasingly conscious of Thy ubiquitous Presence)” 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 179

means the following: Except for the sundering of the ego 
which manifests itself as attachment to the filthy physical 
frame, mingling non-dually as the Self in the Impartite 
Satchitananda – Arunachala – that is, experiencing Him as 
Quiescence in the citadel of the Heart in unified Awareness, 
cannot happen. Further if one were to think of Him as, 
indeed, one should, with thoughts totally surceasing, he, like 
the tenth man of the popular parable, though unaware of the 
truth – out of delusion born of nescience that binds – of his 
axiological or quintessential oneness or unity (ekibhāva) that 
eternally by his inherent nature he enjoys with Arunachala, 
he gets dissolved in Arunachala. Bhagavan hymns the same 
in Ashtakam “uṉṉuru vuṉalaṟa uṉṉiḍa munnīr uṟucaruk 
karaiyuru eṉavuru vōyum (To dwell without thought upon 
Thy (boundless) nature is to lose one’s separate identity like 
a doll of sugar in an ocean)”. In other words, in the ego-free 
integral vision, seen through the Eye of our true Being the 
truth that unifies Arunachala with us is just one – Being-
Awareness.

When one becomes aware of Thy Form with an 
unthinking mind, like a sugar doll sunk in an ocean of 
milk, his ego mind – the body based – even if he wills 
it or not, will gradually dissolve away unknown to him 
to become one with Arunachala the Form of Awareness-
Bliss. Then, by the plentitude of the grace of Arunachala, 
His splendour will flash all on a sudden in his heart as the 
form of the Self. Considering a segment of this verse “That 
I may unceasingly behold and unite with the splendour of 
Thy ubiquitous Presence (nāḷum uṉ bavisu kaṇḍuṟa varuḷ)” 
with the line of verse 8 that goes “That I may, ever beholding 



180 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Thy Form, be stilled in Thee, unveil Thy beatific Form, (viḍā 
duṉaikkaṇ ḍaḍaṅgiḍa vuṉṉazhahaik kāṭ)” and reading them 
together, will be fruitful and rewarding.

The attachment to the physical frame – the work of 
maya – and the vision of Siva swarupa, the form of 
attributeless Awareness, like light and darkness, at no time 
can remain together. Hence the extinction of attachment to 
the physical frame is a must for those who desire to behold 
His grandeur and unite with Him. Until then, to aspire for 
the non-dual experience of the Self, with the attachment to 
the body unsundered can at best be a mere pipe dream. Verse 
12 of the Supplementary verses to Forty verses on Reality 
highlights this truth:

Izhi-vuḍal yāṉ-eṉṉal ihan-diḍuha eṉḏṟum
Ozhivil iṉbān-taṉṉai ōrha ‒ azhiyum
Uḍa-lōmbal-ōḍu taṉai ōra-vuṉal yāṟu
Kaḍak-kak karāp-puṇai koṇḍaṯṯṟu.
Abandon the thought of mistaking this detestable 
and vile body as Atman. Know the Atman, ever 
the form of ceaseless bliss. Endeavouring to know 
the Atman without giving up the care for the safe 
upkeep of the body is akin to attempting to cross 
over a river with a crocodile as the float.

Kūval āmai kurai-kaḍal āmai-yaik
Kūvalōḍu okkumō kaḍal eṉḏṟal-pōl
Pāva kārigaḷ pārp-pari deṉbarāl
Dēva dēvaṉ Sivaṉ-perun taṉ-maiyē.*

*	 Appar Thēvāram, 100-Podhu, v. 5.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 181

Just like the tortoise that lives in the well queries a 
tortoise that lives in the ocean ‘will the roaring ocean 
be as great as the well?’, sinners cannot comprehend 
the greatness of Sivan who is superior to the other 
minor Gods.
So does Appar laud Lord Siva in a dulcet hymn of 

Thēvāram driving home the same truth. This lyric terms the 
despicable attachment to the body of filth and flesh, namely, 
the ego (for it identifies itself with the body) – a sin. People 
blinded by this delude themselves into assuming the world 
of appearances to be real and get bound in it only to be 
assailed by grief and misery. They are branded as ‘sinners’.

But in the hearts of devotees devoid of I and mine, 
Arunachala, the Form of Siva, Being-Awareness, bereft of 
any adjunct, ever pulsates as ‘I-I’ as Self in the most subtle 
form. But the form is well beyond the perception of these 
sinners.

Men of discernment will clearly perceive the beauty and 
elegance with which Appar instructs the same truth that this 
verse contains.
A.I.F.A:

“Arunachala, bestow Thy grace that I, rid of my ego that 
holds on with attachment to the body made up of the five 
elements as ‘I-I’, may forever revel in the grandeur of Thy 
Form and Grace.”

The five elements are earth, water, fire, air and ether. The 
attachment to the body includes also mental attachments. In 
Tirukkuṟaḷ called the “Tamil Veda” Vaḷḷuvar of great renown 
pronounces in a verse:



182 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Yāṉ eṉadu eṉṉum cerukku aṟuppāṉ vāṉōrkku 
Uyarnda ulaham puhum.*
The feelings of ‘I and mine’ are naught but vanity 
and pride; he who crusheth them entereth a higher 
world than the world of Gods.
The “world higher than that of Gods” is the same as 

“pōkkum varavumil poduveḷi” – the all-pervasive supreme 
expanse of chit – which is indeed the grandeur of the Lord. 
Revelling in the splendour of chit is possible only by the 
ascension to that space of chit which, in turn, is accomplished 
only by renouncing both types of attachments. Hence he 
says “rid of my attachments let me behold Thy ubiquitous 
grandeur of Thy Form (paṯṯṟaṯṯṟu nāḷum uṉ bavisu kaṇḍuṟa 
varuḷ)”. Since the annihilation of the ego ‘I’ is followed close 
at its heels by the elimination of ‘mine’, Bhagavan mentions 
the “exit of I” as the sole means to renounce attachment. 
When the body-bound ego dies, the jiva, once wedded to 
matter, shines now as mere Awareness which abides as 
Being and hence Bliss eternal of the Self. Awareness is the 
dwelling place of the Self-God which is but Arunachala, 
the all pervasive Space. The Self thus revels forever in 
the grandeur of the Lord. Since that ‘poduveḷi’ is free of 
coming and going Bhagavan states “Beholding forever” 
(nāḷum kaṇḍu) which means “to perceive unceasingly” that 
‘poduveḷi’. This is the same as “seeing without seeing”.

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 346.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 183

76.	 Malai-marun diḍa-nī malait-tiḍa vō-varuḷ
		  Malai-marun dā-yoḷir Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou art the physician who cures the disease of 

birth. Even after being administered the anti-dote to 
delusion – the cause of birth – by Thee will I still be 
subject to delusion any longer? Oh Arunachala! Thou 
remaineth and shineth as the Self-born Hill, the mass of 
jnana, the cure-all medicine of grace!
Commentary: 

The word ‘malai’ in the phrase “malai marundiḍa’ 
denotes delusion or befuddlement (malaippu). Malai 
(Mountain) and marundu (medicine) together stand for “the 
cure for delusion”, like the medicine for any other disease. 
The idea is that the Hill is the medicine that transforms the 
delusion. Taking it as “nī iḍa, (nāṉ) malaittiḍavō” – (the 
word ‘nāṉ’ supplied to make the sense complete) – gives 
the meaning “Will I be seized with delusion any more after 
Thou hast administered the medicine?”

Yet another meaning can be gleaned if the words “malai 
marundu” (The medicine of the Mount) are taken to mean 
Devi Uma the universal Mother, the Power of Divine 
Grace. It would then yield the meaning “Thou, who hast 
Devi Uma as Thy left half (while Thou abideth in my heart 
and shineth forever as my guide and saviour) will I ever be 
deluded again?” (The words within brackets above are used 
to make the sense complete). Since it is the medicine that 
transforms the delusion it is called “the medicine of grace 
and jnana that revives one from death assuring immortality 



184 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

(aruḷ marundu)”. It is the panacea or “cure-all” medicine, 
the elixir of wisdom.

Another plausible reading also emerges when ‘delusion’ 
is taken to mean hesitation and attributed to Arunachala. It 
would then read as follows: “Arunachala, Thou shinest as 
the cure-all medicine of jnana assuring immortality before 
the devout (to remove the disease of birth, caused by the 
inadvertence of their real nature, the instant they think 
of overcoming it). Why dost Thou hesitate and standeth 
transfixed and administer not that medicine that effects a 
cure for my delusion? Pray bestow that medicine on me 
with no hesitation or procrastination. Why art Thou not 
emboldened to act post-haste and with alacrity to administer 
it but delay inordinately with Thy own mind perplexed?” 
An idea identical to the one expressed here of Arunachala 
shining as the “Mountain medicine” occurs in verse 8 of 
Arunachala Padikam.

Pait-tiya marun-dāp pār-oḷir Aruṇa
Parup-pada vurup-peṟu paraṉē.
Arunachala, Thou, as the cure for the insanity of 
worldliness shinest in the form of a Hill of the hue 
of burnished gold.
Arunachala Puranam lauds the Hill as “The Hill – the 

potent medicine that cuts asunder the cycle of births and 
deaths and its one name is ‘malai marundu’* (Mountain of 
medicine).”

“maraṇabhava maṟukka nalla marundāhum adaṟku 
malai marundeṉḏṟōr pēr”

*	 The presiding deity of Thirukkazhuk Kundram also goes by 
the name “Malai Marundu”.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 185

77.	 Māṉam-koṇ ḍuṟu-bavar māṉat-tai yazhit-tabi
		  Māṉam illā-doḷir Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Arunachala! By the prowess of Thy grace, Thou 

dost destroy the minds of those, who, with the conceit 
of the ego, reach Thee out of love. Though from the 
cursory perception of the imagined attitude of duality, 
Thou appeareth to be indifferent without the solicitude 
displayed earlier towards them, Thou, later on, shineth in 
their hearts as the Self, still and motionless.
Commentary: 

The Text commences by proclaiming “Arunachala, 
Thou dost uproot and annihilate the ego of those who 
meditate on Thee in their hearts.” But what He does later 
is quite different. Without blessing them even with a glance 
out of the corner of His eyes, He destroys their individuality 
without showing any concern.

This seeming indifference and lack of concern is due to 
the differential attitude born of mental delusion that views 
Arunachala who is the very form of the Self or Atman, as 
alien and the ‘other’ of him. Such an ego mind, verily the 
form of vibhakti and viyoga [(harbouring under the notions 
of having been separated from the Lord (vibhakti) or being 
in disunion with the Self (viyogam)] desires and seeks the 
remedies of bhakti and yogam. This is the reason for the 
sorry state it finds itself in. The apparent lack of solicitude 
towards such devotees later on is because they, by the conceit 
of the ego with the dehatma bhava and thus deluded into 
adopting differentiated attitude fall in line with the ways of 



186 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

the world and desire to exhibit the insignia of love. But then 
the Lord, the Ruler of all, by the profuse expanse of His 
ever-present grace, wipes out the differentiated attitude of 
delusion from their minds, bears them in Him and rules over 
them so that they can flourish as the form of blissful Self in 
Self. And the devotees too no longer entertain a desire for 
them as before. The following verse of Guhai Namasivaya 
is but proof of this truth.

Nallaruṇai nādar namaioṟuttal neñjamē
Kollukaikkō pollāk guṇampōkkak - kalliṉ
Mazhukkuvāṉ kūṟaikku māṟupaṭṭō vaṇṇāṉ
Azhukku vāṅgaikkē avaṉ*
O my good heart! Know that the good Lord of 
Arunachala chastises us not to kill us but to rid 
us of the vicious impurities and dross even as the 
washerman rubs the clothes on the stone to remove 
the stains and not to make them threadbare.
As long as true love, as the form of Self, abides in the 

heart, the outward symbol indicating it on its own, out of
