uncontrollable necessity wells itself up as Tiruvaḷḷuvar 
emphasizes in Tirukkuṟaḷ.

Aṉbiṟkum uṇḍō aḍaik-kuntāzh ārvalar 
Puṉkaṇīr pūsal tarum.†
Where is the bar that can close the gates of love? 
The gentle tear-drops that form themselves in the 
eyes of lovers are sure to proclaim its presence.

*	 Aruṇagirimālai, Guhai Namasivayar, v. 21.
†	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 71.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 187

They are not however desirable and men of 
discrimination, specially, do steer clear of such unwanted 
displays.

‘Abhimanam’ here denotes love in the temporal sense 
in the plane of duality. True love is what spontaneously 
blossoms and prospers non-dually as the form of Self in the 
heart and never is revealed by outward marks or symbols. 
Hence for an external perception it may appear to be non-
existent. A verse in Tirukkuṟaḷ also more or less reflects this 
content. 

Ceṟā-ac ciṟu-sollum ceṯ-ṯṟārpōl ṉōkkum
Uṟā-arpōṉḏṟu uṯ-ṯṟār kuṟippu.*
The half-hearted approach and the offended look 
are the marks of those who pretend to spurn but who 
really love us in their hearts.

78.	 Miñ-jiḍil keñ-jiḍum koñja vaṟivaṉi-yāṉ
		  Vañ-jiyā daruḷ-eṉai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Mean-minded am I to cringe and cower before others 

who overpower me with greater might. Do not, pray, 
deceive me, holding this against me but bestow Thy grace.
Commentary: 

Paṇiyu-mām eṉḏṟum perumai siṟumai 
Aṇiyu-mām taṉ-ṉai viyandu.†

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 1097.
†	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 978.



188 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Greatness is ever unpretending and modest; but 
littleness vaunteth its merits before all the world.
In line with the essence of this couplet, is “I, bereft of the 

virtue of behaving uniformly towards all with spontaneous 
humility, am of a lowly mind of bullying the less fortunate 
inferiors. Lacking strength, due to this mean nature of 
mine I, the chicken-hearted, cringe and beg abjectly when 
overpowered with greater prowess.” This he expresses in 
the line “miñjiḍil keñjiḍum koñja vaṟivaṉiyāṉ”. “Wert 
Thou, with the exceeding glory of the power of maya, 
holding the flaw against me, to delude my mind further 
making the comprehension of Thy Truth beyond me, and 
thus deceive me, I , the indigent one, have neither the 
strength to endure it nor the power to change it.” Hence he 
supplicates “without recourse to any vile stratagem, bestow 
Thy grace”, in “vañjiyādu aruḷ”. The very same plea is 
voiced in verse 32, where the terms “sūdu ceydu cōdiyādu 
(Test me not with Thy guiles)”. Though this verse contains 
only the part “Of the lowly nature of the abject cowering 
in the face of overpowering might (miñjiḍil keñjiḍum)” the 
other “of the nature of bullying the inferiors” is, by the rule 
of inferential logic (arttāpatti), derived as a consequence. 
“Mean-mindedness” called “koñja aṟivu” in the verse refers 
to “pullaṟivu” of verse 68 meaning, base, deluded or wrong 
knowledge. 

“I, faint-hearted and of a cringing nature, when 
overpowered (“miñjiḍil keñjiḍum koñja vaṟivaṉiyāṉ),” has 
a deeper connotation. “Unlike the great men of wisdom who, 
bereft of the inadvertence, with spontaneous equanimity at 
all times, viewing and meeting adversity and prosperity in 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 189

their stride, pray and worship Thee forever in all humility. 
But, I, the lowly one, rush to worship Thee cringing and 
crying for help only when assailed by miseries”. That grief 
and misery will be the outcome of the cringing nature when 
overpowered (miñjiḍil keñjiḍum) is to be inferred, though 
left unsaid. An example of this can be cited from a verse in 
Puṟanāṉūṟu which goes as follows: 

Uṯṟuzhi udaviyum, uṟuporuḷ koḍuttum,
Piṯṟainilai muṉiyādu, kaṯṟal naṉḏṟē! (183)
It is good to learn either by rendering succour in 
times of dire emergencies or by proffering money 
so that one does not languish in anger for the earlier 
negligence later on. 
‘Not doing so lands one in grief’ is inferred, though left 

unsaid.

79.	 Mīkāmaṉ illā-mal mākāṯ ṯṟalai-kalam 
		  Āhā-mal kāt-taruḷ Aruṇāchalā.

Substance: 
Like a ship tossed about and tormented by a typhoon 

bereft of a helmsman to steer it to safety, I, the poor one, 
caught in mahamaya, may not be dismayed and distressed, 
pray, graciously be the pilot of my life and save me.
Commentary: 

This verse dwells upon Arunachala’s munificence in His 
bestowal of saving grace to devotees caught in the ocean 
of samsara. Lest these devout souls deprived of knowledge 
due to the vaunting ego be buffeted by the fierce force of 



190 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

maya, dashed against and tossed, twisted, wrecked and be 
blown to smithereens, He, acting as the helmsman of the 
ship of their lives, helps them ford the ocean successfully 
and steers them to safety and to the port of peace and mukti.

He, en route, like the pilot, using the mariners’ compass 
of ‘upadesa vichara’ (instructions of enquiry) divines the 
directions with certitude, halts the vessel at requisite ports 
of call, dropping the anchor of the Power of Divine Grace 
and ferries them safely ashore.

The ‘mīkāmaṉ’ is the helmsman and ‘mākāṯṟu’ stands for 
high winds of fierce velocity. ‘alai kalam’ is a ship battered 
by gusts of winds with terrible force. This is an echo of the 
plea made to Arunachala earlier ‘to be the support of his 
life by bearing the burden providing solid strength to his 
fluttering heart just as the supporting pole of a tender creeper 
reduced to wilting and wavering by the wind (paiṅkoḍiyā 
nāṉ paṯṯṟiṉḏṟi vāḍāmal paṯṯṟuk kōḍāykkā Aruṇāchalā)’. In a 
similar vein here Bhagavan supplicates Arunachala to lead 
him in the path of deliverance (mukti) without weariness 
or slip ever by being his Guru like the helmsman so that 
he, with a deluded mind, in the ocean of samsara, may not 
drown like a vessel tossed about by a hurricane.
A.I.F.A.:

This verse is in the form of a metaphor. ‘Guard and save 
me that I may not suffer bereft of the power of discrimination 
caught in the ocean of transmigration, coming under the 
sway of vasanas, reinforced by numerous births and with a 
mind perpetually oscillating.’ Here the ‘vessel’ represents the 
mind and the ‘wind’ that buffets the ship stands for vasanas. 
The ‘captain’ of the ship who, divining the favourable course 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 191

sails it safely to the shore is the discriminative knowledge 
which is privy to the secret, drives the mind inward and 
shores it in its origin, the heart. ‘Ocean’ stands not only for 
unceasing birth but the phantasmagoria of the world with its 
names and forms. ‘Guide me to ascend to my heart so that I 
may be enthroned there,’ is the gist.

80.	 Muḍi-yaḍi kāṇā muḍi-viḍut taṉai-nēr 
		  Muḍi-viḍak kaḍa-ṉilai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Pray, do graciously untie the intricately entangled 

knot of the ego, badly tousled up, an imbroglio, whose 
origin and end are beyond the perception of me, Thy 
beloved devotee, with the solicitous affection of a mother 
that wells up in Thee out of Thy grace. The undoing of 
the knot is beyond the meagre knowledge of this indigent 
one.
Commentary: 

Like light and darkness, chit (awareness) and jada 
(insentience) are diametrically opposed to each other. As 
these two antithetical entities, chit and jada, are knotted up 
together as one, in the self, it is called the knot of chit-jada 
(chit-jada granti) – the ego self or delusion. The one, the Self 
or Atman, that remains and shines as the Transcendent Space 
in all its nature as Pure Being-Awareness deems Itself as the 
body that is truly unreal and insentient, with affection and 
non-difference, as “that indeed am I”. This superimposition 
is called the delusion of the ego. This tangled skein – knot 
of the ego – is the one famed as Hridaya granti “knot of the 



192 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

heart (muḍi aḍi kāṇā muḍi)” the commencement and end of 
which cannot be discerned. This ego-knot – the author of 
all calamities – is spoken of by the knowledgeable ones as 
the root cause of samsara in all its ramifications – the very 
form of nescience. This, fashioning mental phantasies in 
itself in the field of triputi (with the differences of the triad) 
projects them as true and real thus befuddling them and 
whirls the jivas through them unceasingly. The sundering 
of this hridaya granti or the knot getting undone restores 
one back as pure Brahman – the state of kaivalya – rid of 
birth, death and Samsara. Jivas, till they reach that state, 
have no hope of redemption. The jiva, by himself cannot 
undo that knot, the origin and end of which are beyond his 
(jiva’s) perception. Since that intricately entangled Gordian 
knot blocks his perception and baulks his efforts to undo it, 
Bhagavan beseeches the intervention of Arunachala to undo 
the knot for his sake expressing his total helplessness like 
a child pleading to his mother. Since the jiva is the very 
form of this knot,* by his very nature, it is beyond his ken 
to know the beginning and end of the knot – the origin and 
subsidence of him – and well beyond his power to release 
himself from this bond.

Hence it must be known that the ultimate and final 
supplication of the truly devout, blessed with discrimination 
for the succour and grace of Siva, is for the snapping off of 
this Hridaya granti.

*	 The characteristics of the knot with all its synonyms with 
clarity – though in brief – have been explained to the extent 
required in verse 24 with the opening lines “jaḍa uḍal nāṉ” in 
‘Forty verses on Reality’



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 193

A.I.F.A.:
This verse lends itself for a different reading too.
“Arunachala, Thou hast released the knot, the origin and 

end of which were beyond my perception and straightened 
it out. Is not this undoing of the knot Thy bounden duty? 
(Thou hast performed it more as a duty that has befallen 
Thee by virtue of Thy inherent nature and not as an act of 
grace out of compassion for me.)”

Taṉ kaḍaṉ aḍiyēṉai-yum tāṅgu-dal*
Thy bounden duty is to bear me too.

81.	 Mūk-kilaṉ muṉ-kāṭṭu mukuram āhādeṉait 
		  Tūkki aṇain-daruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Be not, pray, in my case, like a looking glass thrust 

before a man sans nose; but graciously lift me up and 
envelop me within Thy fold.
Commentary: 

“mukuram” stands for a mirror. Such a mirror shown 
before a noseless man mocks him emphasizing his 
disfigurement as a noseless man. “Pray be not like that 
mirror by citing the many faults, like the ego, that my 
heart harbours as a barrier to the governing of me by Thee. 
Pray abandon me not to pine away with a pained heart 
overcome with fatigue and weariness; but lift me up who 
have surrendered at Thy holy feet and lock me up in Thy 

*	 Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai 019-Tirukkaḍambūr, v. 9.



194 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

embrace”, is the worshipful prayer. One can compare this 
verse with verse 53:

Nahaik-kiḍa milai-niṉ ṉāḍiya veṉaiy-aruḷ
	 Nahai-yiṭṭup pār-nī Aruṇāchalā
Mock me not, Arunachala, who seek Thy protection! 
Bejewel me with Thy smile of benign grace. 
“Except for the might of grace of Thee, one of infinite 

compassion towards the weak and downtrodden, I am not fit 
to be lorded over by Thee based on the worth of my deserts 
alone.” Thus Bhagavan makes a poignant plea giving vent 
to his indigent nature. Even Maṇivāchakar, the great, in a 
plaintive plea, petitions the Lord in utter humility to bestow 
grace forbearing all his faults and short comings.

Kuṉḏṟē-yaṉaiya kuṯ-ṯṟaṅgaḷ 
Guṇa-mām eṉḏṟē nī koṇḏāl 
Eṉ-tāṉ keṭṭadu? iraṅgi-ḏāy 
Eṇtōḷ mukkaṇ emmāṉē!*
Even faults that like a Mountain rise to virtue turn
If Thou but say the word! If Thou didst take me once
For Thine, why dost Thou not – though ruined – pity 

take
On me? Our Lord –Thou of eight arms and triple 	
		  eyed.”
“Lifting me up (eṉait tūkki)” also indicates “elevating me 

from my lowliness.” Bhagavan in tune with Maṇivāchakar 
prays to Arunachala to deign to weed out his defects and 
transform his shortcomings into virtues and to lift him up and 

*	 Tiruvāchakam, 8th Tirumuṟai, 33-Kuzhaitta Pattu, v. 3.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 195

lock him in His embrace. Hence he says “mukuramāhādu 
eṉait tūkki aṇaindaruḷ”. The supplication he makes in verse 
19 of this litany is worthy of consideration here “Arunachala! 
Destroy all my faults, Make me a virtuous and worthy servant 
of Thy Feet. Shine on as my guru and bestow grace and do 
guide me. (kuṯṯṟamuṯṯṟu aṟutteṉaik guṇamāyp paṇittāḷ).” In 
the next verse he spells out the Brahmatmaikya bhava (the 
union of Atman and Brahman) – the fruit of lifting him up 
and enfolding him in an embrace.

82.	 Mey-yahat tiṉ-maṉa meṉ-malar aṇaiyil-ṉām
		  Mey-kalan diḍa-varuḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Pray bestow Thy grace that we may truly mingle on 

the tender and soft flower-bed of the heart – the private 
chamber of intimacy – in the temple of the body.
Commentary: 

This verse belongs to the genre of ‘esoteric poetry’ in 
Tamil literature and couched in amatory content falls under 
the subject of “longing for union” – a fit topic to employ the 
medium of bridal mysticism. The need for a place of seclusion 
and togetherness for the pair of the bride and bridegroom is 
answered ideally in the heart and it is called “the sanctum 
sanctorum of the temple of the body (uvaḷakam)”. The heart 
that has blossomed by the Grace of Arunachala is but the 
Unbroken, flawless Transcendent space. That Heart-flower 
is indeed the wide bed of blossoms, ideal and adequate for 
the act of union with the Grace of Arunachala, the form of 
Brahman. With the ego-mind – circumscribed by adjuncts 



196 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

projecting illusory differences and causing delusion – made 
extinct, let me unite with Thee as one with non-difference by 
the spectacle of real non-dual adjunctless Self that remains 
and “shines as Self in the Self” is the content. Verse 24 of 
‘Upadēsa Undiyār’ announces the quintessential Truth of 
jiva and Para as follows:

Iruk-kum iyaṟ-kaiyāl Īsa jīvar-kaḷ
Oru-poruḷē yāvar undī-paṟa
Upādhi uṇarvē-vēṟ undī-paṟa.
“The creature and Creator are just one in their natural 

state of their ‘Being’ and ‘Awareness’. They differ only in 
their adjuncts.” “To commingle (kalattal):” By virtue of the 
eternally attained non-dual truth everyone enjoys inherent 
unity with Arunachala by one’s very nature. But the hurdle 
to the experience of this innate identity with non-dual Truth 
that is Arunachala is the finite mind – that, blind to the Self, 
deems the flesh its home – with its fragmented outlook which, 
in turn, is the product of nescience. When this darkness of 
nescience with all its shadowy concomitants is driven out of 
existence, by the expansive grace that blossoms forth, that 
Absolute Non-dual Truth becomes the experience. The jiva 
is but the reflected awareness. Yet out of mental delusion 
it identifies itself with an insentient body and functions as 
an independent entity as ‘I’. When, by the knowledge of 
Divine Grace, it is made extinct like the light of the eye 
merging with sunlight or like a doll of sugar dissolving in 
the ocean of milk becoming one with it, the jiva, loses its 
form, dissolves as the form of Self in Siva Swarupa, the 
form of Non-dual Awareness and abides in the plenitude 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 197

of Self-Experience as serene quiescence. This is the verity 
vouched by the following verse of Tirumandhiram:

Uḷḷam peruṅ-kōyil ūṉ-uḍambu ālayam
Vaḷḷaṟ pirāṉārkku vāy gōpura-vāsal
Teḷḷat teḷindārkku jīvaṉ Sivaliṅgam
Kaḷḷap pulaṉ-aindum kāḷā maṇi-viḷakkē. *
For the bounteous Lord
This heart is the sanctum holy,
This fleshy body is temple vast
The mouth is the tower gate.
To them who discern
Jiva is Sivalinga
The deceptive senses five are
But the lights that illumine.
The fruit of such integral intermingling is the eternal 

occurrence of Bliss beyond name and form in the Heart of 
the Supreme expanse of Grace called Sayujya. Bhagavan 
pleads to Arunachala, the Lord of the jivas, the Lord of his 
Self, to graciously confer on him that blessed union. From 
the exoteric perception physical union may be deemed to be 
the content of the verse.
A.I.F.A.:

In verses 82 and 91, like the bride inviting the bridegroom, 
Bhagavan beseeches Arunachala for conjoining with him in 
Grace laden Bliss.

*	 Tirumandhiram, 10th Tirumuṟai, 7th Tantra - 11-Siva Pūjai, v. 1.



198 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

83.	 Mēṉ-mēl tāzhn-diḍu mel-liyar sērndu-nī
		  Mēṉmai-yuṯ ṯṟaṉai-yeṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Consorting ever with the humble who, with humility 

as the badge of their very nature, become more and 
more humble, Thou hath gained exaltation and greatness 
(instead of becoming lowly and insignificant like them). 
What a wonder is this!
Commentary: 

Those soft and meek devotees who humble themselves 
are “tāzhndiḍum melliyar”. Devotees are simple folks who 
have the innate nature of being soft, modest and reverential. 
“To humble oneself (tāzhdal)” betokens true greatness 
rather than exiguity and that in truth is strength and power, 
rather than betraying weakness. But how? Because this is in 
perfect harmony with the saying of the elders endowed with 
wisdom:

Paṇiyu-mām eṉḏṟum perumai siṟumai
Aṇiyu-mām taṉ-ṉai viyandu.*
Āṯṯṟuvār āṯṯṟal paṇidal adu-sāṉḏṟōr 
Māṯ-ṯṟārai māṯ-ṯṟum paḍai.†
Greatness is ever unpretending and modest;
but littleness vaunteth its merits before all the world.
It is humility that is the strength of the strong;
and that is also the armour of the man of worth, 
against his foes.

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 978.
†	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 985. 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 199

Bhagavan by the use of the phrase “those humble and 
soft natured ones (tāzhndiḍum melliyar)” brackets them all 
in general in lieu of specifying them for they perceive the 
Lord mirrored in all beings, are forever humble and modest 
towards them all exuding uniformly the same reverence 
and modesty due to the Lord. This truth is corroborated by 
Pattinatthar also as follows:

Tamarpōl aṉaivark-kum tāzhmai solli
Cēy pōl irup-par kaṇḍīr!
Uṇmai jñāṉam teḷinda-varē!
Know that those enlightened ones being humble 
towards all like one’s own, will be like small 
children.
With constant reverential subservience to Lord Siva, 

the ego, by the grace of the Lord, gets ground gradually 
and goes out of existence. The twin virtues of modesty and 
humility towards all creatures of the world ever grow and 
become the ornaments that bedeck a devotee of Siva. Hence 
“humbling themselves more and more” (mēṉmēl tāzhndiḍu 
melliyar) is a phrase that hails it as the inherent nature of the 
devotee of Siva.

The Lord, as if vying with the devotee who sets the 
farthest boundary for the well-disciplined conduct of 
humility, He, besides being of easy access to His devotees, 
becomes a servitor to them and humbling Himself more 
and more pushes the frontier of humility further beyond 
and becomes Himself the ultimate in this divine virtue. 
Bhagavan remarks that it is this quality that invests the Lord 
with the exaltation of Lordship, and has earned the highest 
adoration offered daily by the world.



200 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

The ego-less state is indeed, the true grandeur of 
“pūrṇatvā” or perfection (the wholeness that is all-
inclusive lacking nothing). That the duality of lowliness 
and greatness does not appear in the absolute sense in that 
state of Quiescence that is Pure Knowledge bereft of mental 
phantasies is the idea. Is not the greatness of ether derived 
from its nature of utter subtlety among all the five elements? 
The Lord, being even subtler than the subtlest atom remains 
the atom of an atom. Even in the tiniest insects and minute 
worms of the insignificant species of sentient beings, He, 
subtler and minuter than them, remains as the Indweller 
in each one of them. He, as greater than the greatest, God 
Supreme of all Gods, remains in the hearts of even exalted 
Divinities like Brahma and Vishnu with greater Divinity 
than themselves. On account of these, Arunachala, in the 
absolute sense, enjoys the infinite grandeur of remaining 
as the One Being, the indweller of all. Tiruvāchakam, the 
sacred literature, hailed as the Veda of the Tamil language, 
highlights this very idea when it hymns as follows:

Ceṉḏṟu-ceṉḏṟu aṇuvāy tēyndu-tēyndu oṉḏṟām
Tirup-perun-tuṟai yuṟai Civaṉē*
O Sivan! Who dwellest in Tirupperunturai!
Whose None-but-Thouness goes forth
Goes forth into all things
Till they are reduced and reduced
To atomic minuteness and Thou alone art!
Further while referring to the devotees, instead of 

labelling them as “those devotees who subside or worship 

*	 Tiruvāchakam, 8th Tirumuṟai, 22-Kōyil Tiruppadikam, v. 7



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 201

Thee forever” to harmonise with the wonder he evinces 
while hailing Arunachala “What a wonder hast Thou gained 
such greatness”, stamping them as “Soft and gentle natured 
devotees who lower themselves forever (tāzhndiḍum 
melliyar)” is a dexterous play of words that is quite delightful.

“To humble or lower oneself (tāzhdal)” also means “the 
defilement of egoity becoming less and less” and also “to 
desire”. “The singularly soft natured (melliyar)” refers to 
devotees matured and ripened in Supreme Love (prema 
bhakti) expressing the devotion of the ruled towards the 
Lord and Ruler, in the bridal imagery of the love of the lady-
love towards her beloved.*

In this term “Soft-natured devotees (melliyar)”, the soft 
nature – the natural embellishment of the devotees – that 
is alluded to stands for the sterling quality of regarding 
all beings as one’s own, with compassion and tenderness 
for all, solicitous concern, timely succour, brightness of 
countenance inspired by genuine love and soft speech. As 
the company one consorts with defines and decides one’s 
greatness or lowliness, good ones must ever seek, approach 
and consort with the tribe of great personages, is the explicit 
exhortation to be inferred.

The tone of the verse also hints at the meaning that 
“associating Thyself with women (melliyar) Thou by that 
very discipline, instead of turning wayward or becoming 
lowly, hath gained exaltation! What a wonder is this!”

*	 This is also known as ‘mathura bhāvā’ and as the Sanmārga 
(The Pathway of Truth).



202 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

84.	 Mai-maya ṉīt-taruḷ mai-yiṉā luṉa-duṇ 
		  Mai-vasa māk-kiṉai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou, transforming the dense darkness of my mental 

delusion by the magic unguent of Thy Divine Grace, 
brought my mind under the sway of Thy Supreme Form 
(The Reality).
Commentary: 

The mental delusion is born out of nescience as a 
spurious or illusory entity in the shape of the insentient and 
fleshy frame as the ego “I’. It veils completely the Self – 
the form of Arunachala Brahman – and is unknowable and 
undivinable by any as ‘this’. Its nature, like darkness, is 
unknown to any and it rises like the dream born of dreamless 
slumber. This ego, further ramifies itself into the expansive 
world, entices and cheats in addition the mental awareness 
into deeming the mere appearances as the real and the truth. 
Furthermore it allures the jiva into misconstruing the objects 
of the world as true vehicles of pleasure and goads him into 
pursuing them intensely without let or hindrance.

This mahamaya (the great maya) is called the dense 
darkness of mental delusion (‘mai mayal’). Once this veil is 
lifted the stark and absolute truth of oneself – the Self – the 
form of Arunachala Siva – flashes and shines with a sparkle. 
This Form – the Self – absorbs the mind, the form of ego 
that is merely conceptual in substance and transforms it into 
Itself. Hence Bhagavan remarks “made it into Supreme Form 
of Thee, the content of Truth (uṉadu uṇmai vasamākkiṉai).” 
His Divine Grace is the instrument that rids the ego mind, 
which, fallen from True Awareness is bewildered, enticed 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 203

and enslaved by the mere shadowy pictures of the names 
and forms of the world – an offspring of the expansive glitter 
of imagination – of its delusion.

That self-same Grace, Arunachala, releasing the jiva from 
that bewildering delusion further enslaves him irretrievably 
to His Form – the inherent Bliss of Pure Awareness. The 
Divine Grace of such power is the “unguent or magic paste 
(mai)” that is capable of retrieving a mind enticed by and 
subjected to one and handing it over enslaved to another. 
This strange mystery of that wondrous Power that is awe-
inspiring compels him to refer to it as “Thy enchanting 
Power of Holy Grace (aruḷmai)”. This Grace, termed “magic 
powder (poḍi)” in verse 73, is called an ‘unguent’ here. 
“Unguent (mai)” is a paste compounded of several basic 
and primary materials and is used to cast and create a magic 
spell, binding those on whom it is applied.
A.I.F.A.:

“Arunachala, releasing me, by the collyrium of Thy 
Grace from the dense darkness of my mind’s illusion 
transformed me who was under the sway of the falsehood 
of the world into the Thine own Supreme Truth.” “Dense 
darkness” is denoted by the term “mai”. As the nature of 
mental delusion is indefinable by any it is termed so. “The 
collyrium of Grace (arulmai)” also stands for the prowess 
and grandeur of Grace. The Lord transforming one into the 
Truth of His Form is to make the jiva that perceives itself 
and the world cognized by it as two independent entities 
shine bereft of names and forms as the luminous Truth that 
shines exclusively as Pure Awareness – the substratum of 
them all. The following verse by Thāyumāṉavar is but a 
reflection of the content of this verse.



204 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Kār-iṭṭa āṇavak karu-vaṟaiyil (Chiṉmayā – 6)
“In the womb of egoity marked by dense darkness.”

85.	 Moṭ-ṭai yaḍit-teṉai veṭṭa veḷiyil-ṉī
		  Naṭ-ṭam āḍiṉai-yeṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Purging me of all predilections (vasanas) (and the two-

fold attachment of ‘I’ and ‘mine’ rooted out, made extinct) 
in the Transcendent Space of my heart thus rendered pure 
and immaculate, Thou (all by Thyself as Thy Sovereign 
right) dancest in joyous bliss. Oh! What a wonder is this!
Commentary: 

“Stripping off of all my attachments (moṭṭai aḍittu)” of 
this verse, “stripping me of my nescience (sīrai azhittu)” 
of verse 30 and “who is it but Thou that ruined my life by 
throwing mud on my livelihood (eṉ vāyil maṇṇiṉai yaṭṭi eṉ 
pizhaippu ozhittu)” of verse 88, all encapsulate the same 
idea. But “stripping me off of all my attachments (moṭṭai 
aḍittu)”, literally means shaving one’s head clean and 
“sundering all the familial attachments” as well. 

Kayal-māṇḍa kaṇṇi-taṉ paṅgaṉ-eṉaik kalandu-
āṇḍalumē

Ayal-māṇḍu aru-viṉaic cuṯṯṟamum-māṇḍu 
avaṉiyiṉ-mēl

Mayal-māṇḍu maṯṯṟu-uḷḷa vāchakam-māṇḍu 
eṉṉuḍaiya

Seyal-māṇḍa ā-pāḍit teḷḷēṇam koṭṭāmō.*

*	 Tiruvāchakam, 11-Tirutteḷḷēṇam, v. 11.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 205

When He, Her spouse whose eyes shine bright and mixt 
with my soul

And made me His, deeds and kinsfolk of karma died out
Infatuation with the earth died all other memories ceased
Doings, and speech died, sing we and Beat Tellenam.
The heart that has expanded as space, subtler and 

finer than mind, is termed “the transcendent expanse 
of Heart-Space (veṭṭa veḷi)”. The word “naṭṭam” is the 
Tamilised version of the Sanskrit term “nashtam”. “To 
dance (naṭṭamāḍal)” is an indication of the ecstasy of the 
heart. Arunachala, who was all bent to strip off his ego-
self that has cropped up in between barring the way of His 
Totality after having made a ‘fait accompli’ of the job He 
had preplanned to execute, in sheer exultation performs His 
ecstatic dance of Transcendental Bliss in the Expanse of the 
Heart – His sovereign right. Here Bhagavan, the Poet par 
excellence, using a figure of speech, superimposes his joy 
on Arunachala to heighten the effect.

A different import emerges with the reading “destroying 
the bud (moṭṭai azhittu)” instead of “stripping me off 
(moṭṭai aḍittu)” which is “ensuring the absence of the loss 
of my Real Form by effecting the self-loss, the extinction 
of the ego”. This means the prosperous flourish of the Self 
as Self by the union with Arunachala, the very form of 
Non-Dual, Impartite Self, Being-Awareness, consequent on 
the annihilation of the ego. The same version can also be 
interpreted to mean as “making the bud of my Heart-lotus 
bloom forth in full.” It implies the sundering of the ‘knot’ 
that binds matter with spirit (chit-jada granti).



206 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

This verse reveals the blessing bestowed on Bhagavan 
as the fruit of the prayer he made in verse 27 “make my 
heart lotus unfold and bloom by Thy Grace (maṉa jalaja 
malarttiyiḍu Arunachala).”

86.	 Mōham tavirt-tuṉ mōhamā vait-tumeṉ
		  Mōham tīrā-yeṉ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou eradicated, to start with, my infatuation for the 

world by creating an insatiable yearning for Thee. Why 
then, dost Thou not remove now this latter attachment too 
as Thou didst the former by granting me the experience of 
Thy Form which is a gift and blessing of Thee?
Commentary: 

Here Bhagavan prays for quick elimination of the latter 
too. The delusion that the word “mōham” stands for, denotes 
the delusion of the mind related to the world that draws a 
curtain over truth and renders it imperceptible. “Love of 
Thee (uṉ mōham)” bespeaks of the raging longing that is 
highly intense, that springs up from the heart with the firm 
and solid resolution of attaining Arunachala by all means. 

Paṯ-ṯṟuha paṯ-ṯṟaṯ-ṯṟāṉ paṯ-ṯṟiṉai appaṯ-ṯṟaip 
Paṯ-ṯṟuha paṯ-ṯṟu viḍaṟku.*
Attach and tie Thyself to Him who hath conquered 
all attachments; bind Thyself firmly to Him in order 
that all thy bonds may be broken.

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 350.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 207

The love for Arunachala is but a way and means to do 
away with the love of the world – the sole cause of the travails 
of transmigration. But that is not the summum-bonum for 
the devout men of wisdom. But even that subsiding, that 
Natural State of Quiescience of the Experience of Siva as 
Self, is the conclusively secure and ultimate goal of human 
birth. Bhagavan prays in a similar vein in verse 66 “Freeing 
me of the passion for the enjoyment of sense objects of the 
world of maya, Thou made me yearn madly for union with 
Thee, my own Atman. Grant me a cure for this madness 
too”.

pittuviṭṭu uṉainēr pittaṉ ākkiṉai aruḷ pittam 
teḷimarundu
“Shining egoless in blissful perfect tranquillity of 

Sivahood is true and Supreme Bhakti (parabhakti)” is the 
idea.

87.	 Mauṉi-yāyk kaṟpōl malarā dirun-dāl
		  Mauṉa-mi dāmō Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Can, just that stiff silence, limited to silence of speech 

alone, unaccompanied by the blossoming of the heart in 
full – and remaining mute and still, like a statue of stone – 
be the state of real Quiescence?
Commentary: 

No! It can never be. It is highly probable that Bhagavan, 
observing someone in his presence who remained still like 
a rock with eyes closed and without even uttering a single 



208 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

syllable – mere verbal silence – as if he was absorbed in 
‘Samadhi’, like a jnana-nishta, bestowed this verse.

“To blossom forth (malardal)” means that, with the 
severing of the ‘hridya granti’, ego-knot, the finite ego-self – 
the individuality dies, only to expand and spread as the form 
of Transcendent space – the Form of Siva. That Impartite 
state accompanied by the pellucid clarity of knowledge and 
tranquillity is the state of real silence – and not the others 
– is the idea. Mental activity in the form of resolutions 
(sankalpas), positive and negative, by the very nature of 
that state, does not arise except when willed by the Divine 
for some specific reason. Even if it were to arise all on a 
sudden for some reason, without any pre-meditated wishing 
or willing, after the completion of the deed, in trance-like 
stillness free from the sense of ‘I’, the vasanas or impressions 
of those actions go unregistered in the mind. The state that 
remains distilled in the form of pure and supreme quiescence 
is devoid of the sense of doership – the form of nescience. 
Then even if the instruments of action and perception were to 
act in many a way, the imagined connection with them in the 
form of the ego-knot that comes in the way of the Self – with 
the entire gamut of the vasanas that were lying for long in the 
darkness of nescience enveloping the heart – has been totally 
annihilated and rendered naught. 

The one who is anchored in the true awareness of that 
Natural State that emerges after the sundering of the ego-
knot – the stita prajna – the Sahaja nishta – the jivan mukta, 
is ever the non-doer. One would do well to reflect on the 
content of verse 30 of Supplementary Verses to Forty Verses 
on Reality



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 209

Sēymai-yuḷam cheṉḍṟu-kadai kēṭpār-pōl vādaṉaihaḷ
Tēymaṉam seydum-seyyādē avaihaḷ
Even as one whose mind has wandered away 
while listening to a story told here, a mind whose 
impressions have been totally destroyed, does not 
act, though it seems to act.

Chitta mauṉam seyal-vākku elām-mauṉam
Suddha mavuṉam-eṉpāl tōṉḏṟil parā-paramē.*
Oh! to be blessed
With Silence of mind,
Action, speech all,
Pure Silence Paraparame”
Here he prays for the blessed state of Pure and Supreme 

silence for pure ‘mouna’ shining in and through a thought-
free mind is the sole gateway to the land of mukti.

Nāṉ-eṉḏṟu oru-mudal uṇḍu-eṉḏṟa nāṉ-talai nāṇa-		
eṉṉuḷ

Tāṉ-eṉḏṟu oru-mudal pūraṇa māhat talaippaṭṭu-		
oppil

Āṉandam tandu-eṉ aṟivai ellāmuṇ ḍavasa-nalgi
Mōṉam taṉai-viḷait-tāl iṉi yādu mozhigu-vadē.†
The ‘I’ that vaunted itself as the ‘One’, with head 
bowed to make its exit, within my heart, One 
Content rising as ‘I’-‘I’ as Whole and Infinite, 
filling everywhere, granting Bliss limitless, 
consuming away my mind, making it all Itself, 

*	 Thāyumāṉavar, Parāparakkaṇṇi, v. 276.
†	 Thāyumāṉavar, 27-Pāyappuli, v. 14.



210 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Wert Thou to grant me the siddhi of quiescence, 
what Oh! can I say!
Thus does Thāyumāṉavar express beautifully the nature 

of that mōṉā!

88.	 Yava-ṉeṉ vāyil maṇ-ṇiṉai yaṭṭi
		  Eṉ-pizhaip pozhit-ta daruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Who else, other than Thee, could have ruined me (who 

like everyone else, as a jiva lived happily in the midst of 
others feeding on the sense objects) by throwing mud on 
my life of comfort – the selfhood or jivatva?
Commentary: 

“Throwing mud on my livelihood (eṉ vāyil maṇṇiṉai 
aṭṭi)” refers to the gracious intervention of Arunachala in 
removing from him the desire for enjoyment of the pleasures 
of the sense-objects. That Arunachala snuffed out the sense of 
enjoyership that cannot function in isolation from the sense 
of doership is meant by “throwing mud on my livelihood (eṉ 
vāyil maṇṇiṉai aṭṭi)”. “My life or livelihood (pizhaippu)” 
refers to the existence of life in a body. Clutching on to some 
‘body’ and deeming it as ‘I’ with non-difference, owing 
to vasanas from countless eons of time, due to nescience 
devoid of the Supreme Knowledge, is termed ‘life’.

This is the ‘knot of chit and jada’ or in common parlance 
‘samsara’. “To destroy or root it out (ozhittal)” stands for 
severing the chit jada granti with the sharp sword of jnana-
vichara – the enquiry of ‘who am I?’. When the spurious I, 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 211

the feeling of the ego, subsides in the Heart by Atma Vichara, 
the experience of one’s real form springs up in one’s heart 
solely as pure and true Awareness. By the wisdom that has 
so dawned, the erstwhile conviction “This insentient body is 
the true ‘I’” is banished out of existence.

Thus even while living in a body, separating the 
insentient from the sentient or the body from the One within 
the body or destroying the jivabodha even while in a body is 
“ozhittal”. In other words it is the sundering of the ego-knot 
or release from samsara.

This is what Sundaramurty Nayanar hails when he 
hymns “ūṉuyir vēṟuceydāṉ noḍittāṉmalai uttamaṉē”* “Oh 
the foremost One of Nodittan malai, Who didst separate my 
body of flesh and I”

What provides immense delight to the heart is to know 
how a rare hymn bestowed by Appar Swami distilled in the 
crucible of personal experience befits most beautifully this 
verse:

Uzhait-tum tuḷ-ḷiyum uḷḷat-tuḷē uru
Izhait-tum endai pirāṉ-eṉḏṟu irāp-pahal
Azhaik-kum aṉbiṉa rāya aḍiyavar
Pizhaippu nīk-kuvar Pēreyi lāḷarē.†
He, the most skilled, makes those devotees who 
constantly labour with love at heart, ecstatic with joy, 
lose the form of mind gradually in God, calling on 

*	 Sundaramūrthy Nāyaṉār Thēvāram, 7th Tirumuṟai, Padikam 
100, v. 1.
†	 Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai, 16-Tiruppēreyil - 
Tirukkuṟuntohai, v. 160.



212 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Him day and night, their very existence (pizhaippu) 
lost, become one with Him.
Thus Arunachala, by endowing on him the true 

knowledge of the Self, destroyed the imagined jivahood, 
the work of nescience and through the experience of Self 
beyond name and form created distaste for things devoid 
of the essence of reality. That deed of Arunachala, great 
beyond compare, is depicted for a cursory perception as if 
an act of disservice and harm had been meted out to him is 
in a lighter vein with a refreshing sense of humour.

89.	 Yārum-aṟi yādeṉ madiyi-ṉai maruṭṭi
		  Yevar-koḷai koṇḍa daruṇāchalā 

Substance: 
Who else except Thou, unknown even to those in the 

immediate neighbourhood, hurling my mind into a state 
of stupefaction, plundering me took me away as Thine 
Own (Form)?
Commentary: 

The wonderful deeds of grace enacted by Arunachala in 
His nature of the indwelling Atman remaining in the hearts 
of devotees as the Awareness of awareness, Pure witness of 
sentience, are beyond the ken of anyone outside in the light 
of the dual attitude of a jiva; hence “unknown to any (yārum 
aṟiyādu)” is used.

For the men with a mundane outlook deluded into taking 
as real the denotative awareness and differentiated knowledge 
of the ego, it is initially hard to comprehend the true jnana 
when it flashes. The true state that dawns all on a sudden 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 213

appears to be one hitherto unknown and staggers one at first. 
It is as if the ego-centered-knowledge (objectified with a 
sense of difference) has been dealt to death accompanied by 
the ability to distinguish as ‘I’ or ‘this’ blinded forever. Then 
the Infinite Knowledge of Transcendent Space expanding as 
the form of Self – the True state, new but unbelievably real – 
at first appears to shock and bewilder one. Hence Bhagavan 
says “deluding and stupefying my mind (eṉ madiyiṉai 
maruṭṭi)”. As there is none else other than Arunachala, the 
thief, who verily plunders the hearts of those who think of 
Him in their hearts, he asks “who robbed it away (evar koḷai 
koṇḍadu)?”

Even as the child-saint Jnana Samabanda, rich in 
divine grace, who was claimed and governed by the Lord 
of Brahmapuram sang, “the robber who steals my heart 
(eṉ uḷḷam kavar kaḷvaṉ)”, later on our own Bhagavan, the 
preceptor Sri Ramana, who was claimed and ruled over by 
the Paraman (the Supreme) the very Form of compassion 
residing at Arunachala, announced Him as “the robber who 
stole my mind”.

Yet another meaning may be gleaned by dropping the 
word “my (eṉ)” from the verse. The content that emerges 
then, would read as follows: “Exhibiting limitless adroitness 
which Arunachala displays in stealing the minds of the 
devotees, He, unknown to any stole it away.”

Bhagavan uses the very same phrase in Arunachala 
Ashtakam too as “entrancing and stupefying my mind 
(aṟiviṉai maruḷaṟuttu)”.

This verse brings out explicitly how the jivabodha (the 
ego or selfhood) becomes food proper in the form of Self to 
Sivabodha leading to the state of “self-loss” (taṉṉizhappu). 



214 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

The earlier verses, 73 and 84, namely “Arunachala, Thou 
like a deft magician stupefying with a magic powder the 
minds of the people, stupefying me with the wonder powder 
of Thy holy Grace, took away my jivabodham and revealed 
Sivabodham, the experience of Siva.”

Poḍiyāṉ mayak-kiyeṉ bhōdat-taip paṟit-tuṉ
	 Bhōdat-taik kāṭ-ṭiṉai Aruṇāchalā

and verse 84, “Arunachala! Removing the dense darkness 
of mind’s delusion by the magic power of Thy Grace, Thou 
hast made it into the Supreme Form of Thee, the Content of 
Truth.”

Mai-maya ṉīt-taruḷ mai-yiṉā luṉa-duṇ 
	 Mai-vasa māk-kiṉai Aruṇāchalā

are in unison with the content of this verse.
The contents of this and the previous one can be 

compared with the meaning of verse 8 of Padikam that goes 
“vaittaṉai vāḷā vaiyahat tuyyum vazhiyaṟi madiyazhittu” 
(Thou hast destroyed my ability to prosper in the world and 
made a wastrel of me; this condition is miserable and no one 
is happy; to die is better than to live thus, O Transcendental 
Self in the form of a mountain and shining as the cure for the 
madness (of worldliness)! Grant me the sovereign remedy 
of clinging to Thy Feet, the state of Union with Thee” and 
the one by Tāyumāṉavar quoted below:

Veḷḷak karuṇaimada vēzhamām niṉ-ṉaruṭkeṉ
Kaḷḷak karuttē kavaḷam parā-paramē.*
For Thee, the wild elephant rut with compassion
My thieving mind is verily Thy mouthful”.

*	 Thāyumāṉavar, 43-Parāparakkaṇṇi, v. 109.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 215

A.I.F.A.:
Arunachala, tell me, who is it who, unknown to any, 

stupefied my mind and by stealth robbed me away? 
That he himself was unaware of the fact of his having 

been robbed away by Arunachala is explicitly stated in 
“Wrapped in great secrecy and unknown to any (yārum 
aṟiyādu).” “Stunning or stupefying the mind (madiyiṉai 
maruṭṭal)” is to prevent the mind from darting outward but 
drive it inward. Once the mind becomes interiorized, the 
ego subsides and merges in the Heart, it’s source, thereby 
making itself untraceable and extinct. This is borne out by 
the expression “who robbed it away?”

90.	 Ramaṇa-ṉeṉ ḏṟurait-tēṉ rōsam koḷā-deṉai
		  Ramit-tiḍac cheya-vā Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Taking the rightful liberty of Thee being the beloved 

Lord of my heart who makes me blissful as a reveller in 
the Self, I have laid bare my feeling. Pray be not offended 
or be cross but deign to come over and bless me with bliss.
Commentary: 

‘Ramanan’ means the beloved one or the one nearest and 
dearest to the heart or the one who delights. “To bless me to 
delight with “Bliss” is brought out by the term ‘ramittiḍa’.” 
What has been made public with the liberty due to a beloved 
are explicitly stated in Verses 88, 89 and others as follows: 
“Who destroyed the life I was eking out by throwing mud 
on my life (yavaṉ eṉ vāyil maṇṇiṉaiyaṭṭi eṉ pizhaippu 
ozhittadu)” and “who did stupefy me and by stealth rob 



216 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

my heart unknown to my kith and kin (yārum aṟiyādu eṉ 
madiyiṉai maruṭṭi evarkoḷai koṇḍadu)” and others likewise 
in a similar vein, that were poured out earlier. The sentence 
“Come over and bless me (vārāy)” implies the following 
meaning: “Thou, the ever-attained Preceptor Supreme, 
the Divine Effulgence of Awareness, who ever abides and 
shines in the mind without the mind being aware of Thee, 
shine resplendently in my heart, the origin of the mind, so 
that by Thy gift of Grace – the Bliss of the Self – I may be 
immersed in Infinite Peace and Quietude with serene silence 
of the mind.” In the unified consciousness that prevails in 
that true love of Supreme Devotion (para bhakti), the lovers 
remain as one life though in dual bodies. Whatever transpires 
between this truly united pair, either by way of utterances 
or actions, passes off without engendering indignation or 
censure for remissness of conduct.
A.I.F.A.:

Lest Arunachala, after lending His ears to the harsh and 
grating words uttered out of liberty enjoyed as a right should 
alter His gracious bent of mind towards him, Bhagavan hastens 
to reveal the intent of his heart clearly thus reassuring His Lord.

91.	 Rāp-pahal illā veṟu-veḷi vīṭ-ṭil 
		  Ramit-tiḍu vōmvā Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
In the house of the Heart, the Transcendental Space of 

Grace that shines bereft of either day or night, come, let 
us both be united as one with non-difference and revel in 
the Bliss of Union.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 217

Commentary: 
“The void of space (veṟu veḷi)” stands for the Pure 

Expanse of Heart Space or Grace where besides that nothing 
else exists. What does it mean when it is declared “it is void 
of anything else?” Of the three spaces, gross or physical 
space (bhūtākāsam), the space of the mind (cittākāsam) 
and the absolute pure space of awareness (chidākāsa), in 
the perception of the Absolute, chittam or mind is totally 
non-existent in chit (pure Awareness). Hence in that 
Transcendent Expanse, the very form of Arunachala, by 
virtue of the absence of mind, the gross space that exists in 
the space of the mind, the other elements that exist in the 
gross space and the entire universe born of these elements 
are in their entirety naught. “Home (vīḍu)” denotes the 
state where the mind is purged of all its attachments – both 
internal and external – like ‘I’ and ‘mine’ – the state where 
the mind bereft of its dual attitude is anchored in Infinite 
Peace and Tranquillity attained by the experience of non-
duality. The segment of the verse, “In the house of pure 
heart-space where neither day nor night prevails (rāppahal 
illā veṟuveḷi vīḍu)” clearly bears out the truth of the heart 
being the limitless Space that shines as Pure Awareness, 
unsullied by the touch of maya wherein Arunachala, as the 
ever resplendent sun of jnana abides bestowing grace and 
not simply the fleshy organ of the body. It can be perceived 
that the real Heart so announced is bereft of and transcends 
the fragmented differences of time and states (avastas) 
like day and night, memory and forgetfulness, sleep and 
wakefulness and attributes and their absence.

When, by the grace of God, the mental dross in the shape 
of the defilement of the egoity is rendered naught along with 



218 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

the assertiveness of the ego that manifests as activity indulged 
in by the ego-self also goes banished out of existence. And 
in the Heart where the mind (chittam) has resolved itself 
in the form of ‘chit’ (awareness), there naturally springs up 
and flourishes the unbroken state of being the Self. In that 
state, the Self, by virtue of being the very nature of the Self, 
recognizes and realizes its very form, Arunachala Siva, who 
in His nature of Being-Awareness, unceasingly shines in all 
splendour without let or hindrance as the very Self. This 
experience of Siva, the singular and sovereign enjoyment 
of Bliss, is what is prayed for by Bhagavan. It will be of 
benefit to read and compare this in conjunction with verse 
82 which goes “let us unite as one in the reality of non-
dual bliss on the very tender and soft flower bed of my heart 
in the temple of my body (mey ahattiṉ maṉam meṉmalar 
aṇaiyilnām meykalandu iḍa aruḷ).”
A.I.F.A.:

Arunachala, let us both revel in the Home of the Expanse 
of Transcendent Awareness bereft of day or night.

Since that is bereft of Kevala and Sakala which comprise 
of all the three known states namely jagrat (wakefulness) 
swapna (dream) and sushupti (dreamless slumber) 
Bhagavan calls it “bereft of day and night (rāppahalillā)”. 
When the state of Turiya is realized, as the one that alone 
is forever, the prior three states are proved to have never 
existed. Hence it, losing its name ‘turiya’ transcends even 
that and becomes ‘turiyatita’. It is the space of Awareness 
that is totally void of ‘triputi’; hence the usage “void 
(veṟuveḷi)”. The state freed of the delusion of ‘I’ is one’s 
eternal Home. It is the state marked by the renunciation 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 219

of dharma, wealth, and pleasures. Renouncing everything 
thinking of the Lord is the Supreme Bliss of Heaven indeed. 
This Bhagavan reveals by saying “Come, let us revel in the 
Heart (vīṭṭil ramittiḍuvōm)”. This is otherwise known as 
“Purged of the impurities of the mind, experiencing Siva, 
attaining Sivahood.”

92.	 Lak-shiyam vait-taruḷ astiram viṭ-ṭeṉai
		  Bhakshit-tāy prā-ṇaṉō ḍaruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou, marking me as Thy target, let go the exceedingly 

sharp arrow of Grace in Thy possession, devoured me 
alive and made me naught (the parasite that Thou art).
Commentary: 

“Target (lakshiyam): I was Thy marked target.” In 
verse 25 the forerunner to this verse itself Bhagavan lays 
bare this truth “(what tapas have I performed that among 
many devotees Thou hast singled me out as the target of Thy 
grace? (kuṟiyāy eṉaik koḷak kuṟaiyeṉ ceydēṉ?)”.

The pure Awareness, the form of Arunachala Brahman, 
is itself the content of the term ‘Grace’ (aruḷ). That, in itself, 
is indeed the Brahmastra, the mightiest of all weapons 
and with unfailing and exact precision strikes the jiva (the 
jivahood in the form of delusion) that has been predetermined 
as the chosen target, and without granting the least chance 
of escape from being struck and consumed by itself devours 
it all-alive. Incidentally Bhagavan strikes a reminiscent note 
in verse 102 wherein he breaks forth: “The very instant I 
thought of Thee as Arunachala in my heart, I was entrapped 



220 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

irretrievably in Thy net of grace (Aruṇai eṉḏṟu eṇṇa yāṉ 
aruṭkaṇṇi paṭṭēṉuṉ aruḷvalai tappumō? Arunachala)”. The 
affirmation that “Arunachala is the most potent Brahmastra 
inescapable by any” asserted here is further underscored by 
that verse. For Arunachala, Grace is the weapon on hand 
and hence the usage “releasing it (viṭṭu)”. “Swallowed away 
with life (bhakshittāy prāṇaṉōḍu)” stands for consuming 
the jiva or the jivahood. Arunachala has consumed away 
the jiva or the jivatva (ego-self) but has left the body intact 
like one who gulping away the pulp of the fruit leaves 
the skin and shape intact. Bhagavan, by this reveals the 
blessing that Arunachala conferred on him, namely, the 
state of deliverance or jivan mukti by destroying his jivatva. 
Further, reading this line along with the one in ‘Padikam’ 
wherein Bhagavan declares “It, attracting the ripened soul 
to itself, feeds on them. The destroyer of lives (jivabodha) 
is this mighty Hill of Arunachala (iṉṉuyir balikoḷum ihdu 
eṉ…uyirkkoli Aruṇamā giriyē)” invests the minds of the 
readers with better grasp of the wonderful play of the Grace 
of Arunachala.
A.I.F.A.:

Arunachala, marking me as Thy target, Thou devoured 
me alive by slaying me with the sword of Thy Grace.

Those targeted by the Lord appear alive only in the 
perception of the onlookers but in their own experience that 
appearance is totally non-existent as he says “Thou devoured 
me alive and destroyed me (bhakshittāy prāṇaṉōḍu)”.

“Missile of Grace (aruḷ astiram)”: That those who become 
a prey to His Grace may, attaining deathless immortality, 
live as ever-liberated, He devours their ego along with their 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 221

lives. In other words He makes them jivanmuktas. Hence 
Grace is personified as a ‘missile’.

93.	 Lā-bhanī iha-para lā-bhamil eṉai-yuṯ-ṯṟu 
		  Lā-bhameṉ uṯ-ṯṟaṉai Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Thou, who art the supreme and infinite gain of those 

who attain Thee, by condescending to accept me who 
am devoid of any worth – here or hereafter – as of some 
consequence, what hast Thou gained?
Commentary: 

Arunachala, being the Life of life, both in this world and 
ever after, pervading allover, the form of infinite bliss of the 
Self Supreme, Himself the sum total of all auspiciousness 
from start to finish, is the ultimate goal of human life, worthy 
of being aspired for and attained. Hence He is “the best of 
all bargains (lābha nī)”.

“Unconcerned of life either here or hereafter, living on 
what fate made available, inactive and indolent – remained 
I.” Hence he calls himself “without gain either now or 
hence (iha para lābhamil)”. (The adoption of Tūṣṇīm bhāva 
characterized by the absence of the assertiveness of the 
thought ‘I’ – the driving force to activity – excepting when 
activated by the command of Divine Grace dwelling within, 
for reasons best known to that In-dweller, totally bereft by 
nature of the functioning of the jivabodha that is alien to the 
contemplation of the Self – the real form – this state of total 
and interior silence and taciturnity, is the best sadhana to 
be engaged in by the competent seekers as the sole gateway 



222 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

to the land of mukti is to be absorbed here.) Further when 
Fullness or Infinity is joined by even Fullness or Infinity, 
the infinitude of the Infinite ever remains unchanged and 
increases not. Hence “what hath Thou, the Whole Primal 
enriched Content, gained by uniting with me? (eṉai yuṯṟu 
lābhameṉ uṯṟaṉai?)”.

Further from the standpoint of the Absolute, except the 
Self – the Atman, the form of Pure, non-dual Awareness – 
nothing else exists. Hence the Self – “I am”, in truth is not 
‘anything other’ except Itself. The strange phenomenon of 
jiva, jagat and para (the individual, the world and God) 
due to the play of the expansive imagination of the mind 
due to the association of maya with an I, ‘Pure’ or impure, 
appears to be true. And even when they appear espousing 
the differences of ‘triputi’ (triad) in the temporal plane, all 
of them are not other than their substratum – the pure non-
dual, Immutable, Sole, Being-Awareness – the One Self* 
since they all remain as the form of Self. So, the Self is the 
all. (Hence both ways Nothing is He; yet All is He. Nothing 
is He: for the entire phenomenon of manifestation being a 
projection of a fictitious mind lacking reality, none of these 
is He. Yet All is He: even when the unreal phenomenon 
appears superimposed on the Self, they appear and shine 
as real only by partaking the reality of the Substratum, the 
Self. Therefore All is He). Arunachala remains the whole 
in His entirety, with the nature of Being, the Effulgence of 
Being, of the form of natural Transcendental space. Hence 
Arunachala alone is the whole, primal, rich Content. What 
the Tamil Veda, Tiruvāchakam sums up may be cited here:

*	 Refer Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu, v. 1.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 223

Iṉḏṟu-eṉakku aruḷi iruḷ-kaḍindu uḷḷattu
	 Ezhu-kiṉdṟa jñāyiṟē pōṉḏṟu
Niṉḏṟa-niṉ taṉmai niṉaippu-aṟa niṉaindēṉ
	 Nī-yalāl piṟidu-maṯ ṯṟiṉmai
Ceṉḏṟu-ceṉḏṟu aṇuvāy tēyndu-tēyndu oṉḏṟu-ām
	 Tirup-perun tuṟai-uṟai Sivaṉē
Oṉḏṟum-nī yallai aṉḏṟi-oṉḏṟu illai
	 Yār-uṉṉai aṟiyakiṟ pārē.*
Oh Civan who dwelleth in Tiruppendurai Shrine! 
This day on me in grace Thou risest bright as a sun
Bidding from out my mind the darkness flee!
That thought may cease upon Thy nature manifest,
I think besides Thee all that is, is naught –
Moving ever – as atoms ever wasting Thou art one!
Thou art not anything; without Thee nothing is;
Who are they that can know Thee as Thou art?
The phrase “Thou art the best of gains (lābha nī)” also 

means “The form of Self transcending name and form, Thou 
art my Supreme gain.” Even as Maṇivāchakar sings

Tantadu uṉ-taṉṉaik koṇ-ḍadu eṉ taṉ-ṉai
Saṅkarā ārkolō caturar
Andam-oṉḏṟu illā āṉandam peṯ-ṯṟēṉ
Yādu-nī peṯ-ṯṟadu-oṉḏṟu eṉ-pāl†
What Thou hast given is Thee; what Thou hast 

gained is me;
O Saṅkarā, who is the knowing one?
I have obtained the rapturous bliss that knows no end;
Yet now, what one thing hast Thou gained from me?

* Māṇikkavāchakar, Tiruvāchakam, 22-Kōyil Tiruppadikam, v. 7
† Māṇikkavāchakar, Tiruvāchakam, 22-Kōyil Tiruppadikam, v. 10.



224 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Bhagavan also on the strength of the special (privileged) 
liberties he enjoyed with Arunachala, indulges in such 
pleasantries that are outside the ordinary run.

Like the shadow pictures that are projected on the 
screen in a motion picture, in Arunachala – the vast expanse 
of space, the Impartite Sat-Chit form of Self – called the 
Abode Supreme, all the affairs and differences of jagat, jiva 
and para are mere appearances imagined. Therefore, either 
an addition or a subtraction or even their very appearance 
or disappearance can cause neither a gain nor a loss to the 
screen, Arunachala, the substratum of all.
A.I.F.A.:

“Arunachala, since I have attained Thee Thou hast 
become my profit and gain. But what is Thy gain by 
accepting me, devoid of any worth here or hereafter? Pray, 
enlighten me!”

The Lord, the Form of Transcendental Bliss attained 
by him with all the maladies of illusive birth wiped out is 
termed by Bhagavan as his ‘gain’. The achievements either 
here or hereafter remain to be accomplished, only so long 
as the assertiveness of the ego as ‘I’ prevails. When that 
ego that pretends to be the first principle out of delusion, 
has ended by the wisdom of the Divine Grace of the Lord 
and has become extinct in the Lord without there being 
a second entity, all the achievements that trail the ego ‘I’ 
get exterminated along with it. Hence Self Realisation, the 
condensed mass of Supernal Bliss is one in which all the 
achievements of here and hereafter, however great are but 
just a drop. The truth of his having gained this supreme gain 
is dexterously announced as “me, devoid of any gain here or 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 225

hereafter (iha para lābamil eṉai)”. Param – the hereafter or 
the Eternal Home. How?

Bhadda-ṉāṉ eṉṉu-maṭṭē banda-mukti chin-taṉai-kaḷ
Bhaddaṉ āreṉ-ḏṟu taṉ-ṉaip pārk-kuṅgāl - cidda-māy
Nitta-muktaṉ tāṉ-niṟka niṟkādēṟ banda-chindai
Mukti chindai muṉ-ṉiṟkumō.*
“Thoughts of bondage and freedom last only so long as 

one imagines ‘I am bound’.” But when we question, “Who 
am I, the doer of this deed?” and realise the Self, the sense 
of agency is lost and the three karmas slip away and one 
shines as the eternally free Atman. If thought of bondage 
cannot arise, how then thoughts of mukti gained stand their 
ground?” No, they cannot. Hence, they are also included 
here. Here, the term ‘hereafter’, is also included by the term 
‘here’. Since the Lord stands to gain nothing new by way of 
experience in the same way he won the experience of being 
the Self by the Grace of the Lord, he poses the query “What 
is Thy gain in joining me?” Maṇivāchakar too speaks of the 
same idea, “Devoid of any gain here and hereafter (iha para 
lābamil)” while saying “iraṇḍumili taṉiyaṉēṟkē – Thou hast 
bestowed sweet Grace on me bereft of the two.”

94.	 Varum-paḍi solilai vandeṉ paḍi-yaḷa 
		  Varun-diḍuṉ talai-vidi Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Didst Thou not bid me to come to Thee? Hence (even 

if Thou wert to bear on Thy head) come, bear Thyself 

* Sri Bhagavan, Uḷḷadu Nāṟpadu, v. 39.



226 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

the burden of my yoga (union with Thee) and kshema 
(welfare) in their entirety. (If Thou wert to say, ‘oh! It is a 
difficult task’, so be it!) Sufferance in the process is Thy 
fate ordained.
Commentary: 

Those who pin and peg their faith in the Lord will never 
be let down. Saint Appar confirms this verity in a verse in 
Thēvāram.

Nam Kaḍam-baṉaip peṯ-ṯṟavaḷ paṅgiṉaṉ
Teṉ Kaḍam-bait tiruk-karak kōyilāṉ
Taṉ kaḍaṉ aḍiyēṉai-yum tāṅgu-dal
Eṉ kaḍaṉ paṇi ceydu kiḍappatē.*
The Lord of Kadambai Temple, He
As one half of Him, She
The Mother of Kadamban our own
His duty to support even me
Mine to serve and be still
The devotees who, ever in total servitude of the Lord, 

the Ruler of all, tread the path of Grace, have all their needs 
provided for by Him who is pleased to do so. Here one is 
reminded of the assurance of Lord Sri Krishna in the Gita

Ananyāścintayanto māṁ ye janāḥ paryupāsate |	
Teṣāṁ nityābhiyuktānāṁ yogakṣemaṁ 			 
	 vahāmyaham || IX-22||

* Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai, 019-Tirukkaḍambūr, v. 9



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 227

But those who worship Me, meditating on Me alone, 
to them who ever persevere, I bring attainment of 
what they have not and security in what they have.
The solid dispassion and the strong resolution which 

Pattinaththar voices in this song, is worthy of being quoted 
here.

Iruk-kum iḍam-tēḍi eṉ-pasikkē aṉṉam
Uruk-kamuḍaṉ koṇḍu-vandāl uṇbēṉ – perukka
Azhait-tālum pōhēṉ aṉavaradam dēham
Iḷait-tālum pōhēṉ iṉi.
“I shall, to appease my hunger, eat only if food be brought 

seeking the place I stay. Even if profusely invited over and 
over, I shall not go. O Hara! Even if the body wastes away, 
I shall not eat otherwise” is clearly visible as the natural 
trait of Bhagavan when he says “come measure out my 
sustenance (vandu eṉ paḍi aḷa)”. Providing sustenance day 
in and day out unfailingly and on time is a task daunting to 
the ordinary. Yet since Thou Thyself bade me come, bearing 
my burden is Thy bounden duty and in the discharge of this 
chosen responsibility the pain that may be engendered is but 
Thy fate to endure. So he says “suffering in the process is 
Thy Fate (varundiḍuṉ talai vidi)”. One may look askance at 
this for no devotee will ever dream of saying to the Lord “the 
suffering on that score is Thy fate”. But, if Bhagavan, most 
ripe in supreme devotion, the foremost among the devotees 
of Lord Arunachala, like Appar, says so to Arunachala, the 
“Life of his dear life, dearer to him than life itself,” with his 
heart assenting actually uttered these words, the implication 
is totally different. How is it?



228 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

Eṉṉil ārum-eṉakku iṉiyār illai
Eṉṉilum iṉiyāṉ oruvaṉ uḷaṉ
Eṉ-ṉuḷē uyirp-pāyp puṟam-pōndu-pukku
Eṉṉuḷē niṟkum iṉṉambar Īcaṉē.*
There is none better who does good to me than myself.
But there is one who is more lovable than myself 
Civaṉ in Iṉṉambar stays within me as my breath 
Doing the twin acts of exhaling and inhaling.
It is only for the crores of jivas who, out of mental 

delusion, assuming the sense of doership, a mere thought 
–as if in a dream and appearing to be invested with reality 
due to the power of maya –and whirl around to no avail, 
that such sufferings may occur. But to the omnipotent 
Arunachala in whose mere power of the unbroken presence, 
by virtue of His form of Awareness as in the presence of the 
Sun, all activities in their natural rhythm and spontaneity are 
set in motion, nothing is difficult except in the differentiated 
perception of a perverse mind.

How many ever activities may happen, in how many 
ever places, with however much precision, Arunachala in 
truth is an akarta – a non-doer. All activities including the 
five great functions (Evolution, Preservation, Involution, 
Concealment and Bestowal of Grace), flowing from His 
mere potent presence “all are merely simple Awareness” is 
the intent. Those who have known and realized this truth 
through the loss of the ego-self (selfloss) alone are those 
who have gained the ultimate state of the Bliss of Peace and 
Quiescence.

* Appar Thēvāram, 5th Tirumuṟai, 21-Tiru Iṉṉambar, v. 1.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 229

A.I.F.A.:
“Did (Thou) not bid (me) to come (varumpaḍi solilai)” 

may also be said to contain the following import:
“No word beseeching, poignantly inviting Thee to 

graciously appear before me ever escaped from my lips.”

95.	 Vāveṉ ḏṟaham-pukkuṉ vāzh-varuḷ aṉḏṟē-yeṉ
		  Vāzh-vizhan dēṉ-aruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
That very day when Thou, bidding me ‘come’ and 

taking me along with Thee entered into the Heart and 
bestowed on me the life of union with Thy Form beyond 
name and form, I lost my self-hood (jiva-bodha), the 
fragmented individuality – the cause of infatuation and 
grief. That, indeed, is the greatness and grandeur of Thy 
grace.
Commentary: 

This verse bears testimony to the fact that unless the 
Lord out of His profound grace, invites a soul bidding 
‘come’, leads and lands and inducts him into the Heart, it is 
impossible for a jiva to accomplish it depending solely on 
his negligible strength. The ordination of the Omnipotent 
Divine is such that the awareness of the jiva by its very nature 
has the tendency to rush forth outside at top speed towards 
objects of senses and matters external with the high velocity 
and vehemence of surging floods when the flood-gates are 
thrown open. Swimming against this current and reaching 
‘Home’ is truly beyond the power of the jivas however 
mighty they may be. Saint Appar’s remarks in Thēvāram:



230 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

numbiṉemmai nuzhaiyap paṇiyē yeṉbārum*
“those who say, order us to creep through a narrow 
passage behind Thee”, are to be seen.
The truth and ‘significance of the Lord entering the 

Heart’ will be explained in detail in verse 97. The state or 
life of Sivabodha is the truth and reality of the Substratum 
while the life of a jiva is merely a super-imposed appearance 
on it. They cannot co-exist simultaneously in the heart of 
one. The line “the moment Thou bestowed on me the life 
of union with Thee (Sivabodha) I lost my jivabodha (uṉ 
vāzhvu aruḷ aṉḏṟē eṉ vāzhvu izhandēṉ Arunachala)” clearly 
indicates it. (This explains why Bhagavan was merely a by-
stander, contrary to the nature of the world, simply being a 
witness to the nature and ways of the world).

“Robbing me of my jivabodha, Thou revealed 
Sivabodha, the experience of Siva (eṉ bhōdattaip paṟittu uṉ 
bhōdattaik kāṭṭiṉai Arunachala)” of verse 73 and “throwing 
mud on my livelihood deprived me of my life (eṉ vāyil 
maṇṇiṉai aṭṭi eṉ pizhaippu ozhittadu Arunachala)” of verse 
88 can be viewed in conjunction with the line of this verse, 
“the moment Thou gifted me the life of union with Thee, I 
lost my life of jivabodha” for a better comprehension and 
appreciation. 

Right from the most exalted grade of earthly bliss in 
all its entirety up to the bliss of varying degrees and kinds 
derived from objects and matters, all are just droplets of 
that ocean of the Bliss of Brahman, the eternal, ever-present 
object-less, Self, beyond name and form, the Supreme Peace 
that springs up in the Transcendent Expanse of Awareness 

* Appar Thēvāram, 4th Tirumuṟai, 021-Tiruvārūr, v. 9.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 231

(chit) of the Heart, and shines bereft of the defilement of 
the egoity in the form of chit jada granti. For Bhagavan 
who has gained that, can the loss of the jivabodha matter at 
all? As there is no loss whatsoever he beseeches His Grace 
simply praying ‘aruḷ’ (bestow Grace).

96.	 Viṭ-ṭiḍil kaṭ-ṭamām viṭ-ṭiḍā duṉai-yuyir
		  Viṭ-ṭiḍa aruḷ-puri Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
(If I hold Thee not and at the precise moment of 

giving up my life, and) Were I to give Thee up, the Form 
of pure Knowledge, the Self in my heart the dreadful 
misery of rebirth will be my lot. Bestow Thy grace that (in 
my last moments, out of inadvertence) I may not cease to 
remember Thee, but relinquish my life with contemplation 
centered on the Self.
Commentary: 

“Wert Thou to give me up without bestowing Thy 
Grace”, can also be inferred as the meaning of the phrase 
“viṭṭiḍil kaṭṭamām (If I give Thee up letting go of the hold on 
Thee, misery will be my lot)”. In tune with what he entreats 
Arunachala for in Padikam (Verse I) as “If Thou who hath 
graciously claimed and governed me by Thy Causeless 
Compassion, dost not bestow on me now the Vision of Thy 
Real Form and were I to give up my body caught up and 
harassed by dense darkness of the world, yearning wistfully 
for Thee, what would become of me? (karuṇaiyāl eṉṉai 
āṇḍanī eṉakku uṉ kāṭchi tandu aruḷilai eṉḏṟāl iruṇali ulahil 
ēṅgiyē padaittu ivvuḍal viḍil eṉgati eṉṉām)” he reinforces 



232 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

the same request as “If I were to give up without holding 
on to Thee the dreadful misery of rebirth would be my lot 
(viṭṭiḍil kaṭṭamām)”.

As the last thoughts of the jiva at the time of death 
determine the next birth, the paramount exhortation to be 
imbibed and absorbed by the learned ones desiring the state 
of Brahmanhood is the indispensability of the meditation 
of the Self – Arunachala – the state of Pure Awareness 
and the Abode of ‘no return’ to earth – to escape from the 
Persian wheel of birth, the cause of the miserable samsara 
occasioned by the delusion of maya. This is called the 
‘luminous Path’ or the Path of Light.

If this ‘dhyana’ were to spring forth in the heart without 
the mind becoming agitated even during the painful throes 
of death, that dhyana in the heart of such a one, should have 
already been established as a spontaneous, continuous and 
effortless flow – as that of oil – which is triggered in the 
heart purely by the grace of Arunachala Himself who deems 
the effort on the part of the devotee as an excuse enough 
to let His Grace of Causeless Compassion descend on him. 
Hence he entreats Arunachala “before the venom of maya 
serpent mounting to my head causes me to perish, bestow 
Thy Grace (iṟumuṉam aruḷ paṯṟiḍa aruḷ puri)” in Verse 64. 
Unless the total attrition of predilections (vasanas) has 
already been effected, myriad of thoughts with the misery of 
transmigration in its wake, due to the sway of the strength of 
earlier impressions remaining uppermost in the mind spring 
forth at the time of departure. In order to counteract and 
transform them, sustaining the current of meditation over a 
prolonged period of time fortified with intensity becomes a 
sine-qua-non for the mumukshus:



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 233

antakāle ca māmeva smaranmuktvā kalevaram |
yaḥ prayāti sa madbhāvaṁ yāti nāstyatra saṁśayaḥ|| 	
	 Gita, VIII-5 ||
And whoever at the time of death gives up his body 
and departs thinking of ‘Me’ alone he comes to My 
state (of Being); of that there is no doubt.

97.	 Vīḍu-viṭ ṭīrt-tuḷa vīḍu-pukkup paiya-uṉ 
		  Vīḍu-kāṭ ṭiṉai-yaruḷ Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Dragging me (by Thy cord of Grace) forcefully out 

of my house, entering the house of my Heart, there, by 
degrees Thou revealed to me that That was Thy real, 
eternal Abode. That revelation is but Thy overflowing 
Grace Divine towards me.
Commentary: 

That Arunachala spirited away Bhagavan out of his 
house is probably centered on the fact that being in a house 
that breeds bondages and simultaneously to plunge oneself 
with intense dispassion in the sadhana of devotion and tapas 
(austerities) is a tall order well-nigh impossible even for 
hearts highly matured, refined and stoic, due to the presence 
of many a hurdle inherent in such a life is to be inferred.

His life of restraint and withdrawal (nivritti) and his 
being pulled out of the house was a pre-ordained fact. 
Yet Bhagavan, in the light of the total identity between 
what is preordained and the ordainer, superimposes the 
act of dragging him out, on Arunachala, thus placing the 



234 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

onus on Him following the traditional courtesy. The act of 
Arunachala entering the house of the heart of Bhagavan is to 
sweep off clean all the predilections, effecting a total attrition 
of vasanas (vasanakshayam) and thereby render the home 
of the Heart immaculate enough to serve as an exclusive 
temple for Him. It may also be to scrutinize, evaluate and 
plumb the depth of his devotion and dispassion to ascertain 
the deservedness of the abode before He could make it a 
temple fit for Him.

But then Arunachala, in truth, never enters a place 
anew. He cannot. How? If He does so, it runs contrary to 
His nature of being the all-pervading Infinitude that is ever-
attained. But Texts on such subjects often speak of ‘the 
Divine entering the hearts of the devout.’ Arunachala, the 
form of the Infinite Knowledge, as the ever shining Sun of 
the Atman abides and shines in the hearts of all beings as 
‘I-I’ unceasingly whether they perceive Him there as such 
or not. But He does reveal Himself as a new entrant in the 
hearts of those whom He himself chooses as fit recipients of 
His grace. This is precisely to transform their exteriorized, 
object-oriented perception that forever remains a barrier 
obstructing their perception of Him who shines unceasingly 
with the effulgence of Being, into an inward one and Self-
oriented. It is then that He reveals Himself to them as if He 
has come afresh and anew.

Such being the truth, why does Bhagavan, ingrained in 
the truth, instead of openly declaring it as it really is, say 
differently and contrary to the Truth sounding as if true? 

Many mahatmas and jnanis like Nammāzhwar and 
Maṇivāchakar do so despite their inherence in the experience 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 235

of their natural State – a gift of the Grace Divine – and 
express many of the exceedingly subtle, metaphysical and 
mystical secrets here and there in their effortless outpourings 
of hymns.

Likewise this Text, like the Texts of those saints and 
knowers of Truth in its entirety, is taken by most as a classic 
Text of hymns full of devotion (bhakti) excepting for a few 
who are enquiry-oriented. Hence falling in line with the 
established tradition (sishtachara)* and without running 
counter to it, Bhagavan also speaks so. Heart (uḷḷam) does 
not at all refer to ordinary mind as understood in ordinary 
parlance. But it stands for the state of awareness of the Pure 
Being (the sadbhava) the origin of mind that forever keeps 
oscillating. That it has no relationship with the body has 
been spelt out earlier. When the mind enters the Heart, on 
entering, not only is its form of thinking done away with, but 
remaining there, if it even stirs so much as to make a whit 
of an effort, even before it could think, it is expelled out. 
Hence, mind in the form of thoughts, is a non-entity there. 
(Heart is the mind sans thoughts. The pair – memory and 
its loss, a duality – is inseparable ever functioning together. 
A mind bereft of both forgetting and remembering is, in 
truth, the Heart). As there is no mind, the entire universe of 
dualities – mere thoughts – are but a form of mind which 
ends there, rendered extinct.

The abode of Lord Arunachala termed as mukti, 
“Home” (vīḍu) is nothing but the Heart of Quiescence, the 
state of Pure Awareness, free of thought or thinking and the 
world, the measure and product of thoughts. Therefore it is 

*	 The rules prescribed traditionally followed by the disciples.



236 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

described as the “House of the Heart (uḷa vīḍu)”, “House 
Thine (uṉ vīḍu)”. In the phrase, “dragging me out of the 
house (vīḍu viṭṭu)” the term “house (vīḍu)” even though 
in common parlance may appear to refer to the house in 
Madurai where Bhagavan used to reside till he abandoned it 
to come over to Arunachala, from what has been described 
so far, specially in adhyatmic terms, refers, in fact, to the 
mind, the abode of jiva. In the phrase, “the house of the 
Heart (uḷa vīḍu)” House or Home stands for ‘daharakasa’ 
the Transcendental Expanse of the Heart or chittrambalam 
(the temple of Awareness). “Ushering me into the House 
of the Heart” is implied by “uḷa vīḍu pukku”. “Gradually 
revealing Thy mansion (uṉ vīḍu kāṭṭiṉai)” denotes the 
mansion of total Emancipation and Beatitude – the most 
subtle state of kaivalya.

“Gradually Thou revealed Thy House – (paiya uṉ vīḍu 
kāṭṭiṉai)”: Thy state of kaivalya Thou revealed by degrees in 
total secrecy so that even those near could neither perceive 
nor have an inkling of it. But Thou revealed it all in the most 
subtle and secret manner in my inner Awareness known only 
to me. A couplet in Tirukkuṟaḷ goes as follows:

Asai iyaṟku uṇḍu āṇḍōrē eryāṉ nōkkap
Pasai-yiṉaḷ paiya nahum.*
The slender shaped maid melteth to see my imploring 

look and softly smileth; and the gentle smile giveth her an 
added grace.

Kaivalya is called “Home (vīḍu)”. A mind freed of the 
differentiated appearances with dualities and triads – a mere 

*	 Tirukkuṟaḷ, v. 1098.



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 237

phenomenon of the phantasy of the ego-mind – along with 
its entire gamut of offspring of bonds born of desires and 
attachments in full – and merged and sunk in the depth of 
the Heart and shining simply as the most subtle and mere 
Being is termed kaivalyam. The non-dual Being-Awareness 
that is the nature of Transcendent Form of Arunachala is 
called kaivalyam or “vīḍu (Home)”.

“Drawing me by Grace and revealed to me gently Thy 
Home” can be another reading.
A.I.F.A.:

When the words are rearranged in the order “uḷa vīḍu 
paiyapukku”, it would mean “entering the House of Heart 
by degrees.”

“uṉ vīḍu (Thy House)” is the luminous state of mukti 
marked by the welling up of Supreme Bliss.

A line in Tiruvāchakam can be read together with this 
verse for the identity of the idea conveyed.

kāyattuḷ amudu ūṟa ūṟa nī kaṇḍukoḷ eṉḏṟu kāṭṭiya cēya 
māmalar cēvaḍi

As the nectarine Bliss springs and wells up, Thou 
revealeth Thy immaculate Feet of Flowers saying “Behold, 
now.”



238 Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai

98.	 Veḷi-viṭ-ṭēṉ uṉ-seyal veṟut-tiḍā duṉ-ṉaruḷ
		  Veḷi-viṭ ṭeṉaik-kā Aruṇāchalā

Substance: 
Pray spurn me not that I have laid bare in the public 

domain the deeds of stealth Thou enacted (in the secrecy 
of my heart) through this Text of Hymns; but do reveal 
Thy Grace and shelter me by uniting with me in that 
Transcendent Expanse of Grace thus conferring mukti or 
Kaivalya on me.
Commentary: 

The verses catalogued below reveal the numerous deeds 
of His Grace that He enacted in his Heart.

“When I surrendered unto Thee as my whole and sole 
God, Thou hast totally decimated me with my bondage 
and vasanas (deyvam eṉḍṟu uṉṉaic cāravē eṉṉaic cēra 
ozhittāy).”	 v. 48

“With the madness for Thee, Thou hast freed me of 
the madness for the world of maya; grant me now the cure 
for all madness (pittuviṭṭu uṉainēr pittaṉ ākkiṉai pittam 
teḷimarundaruḷ Aruṇāchalā).” 	 v. 66

“The mere thought of Thee has drawn me unto Thee and 
who can gauge Thy glory! O! Arunachala (peyar niṉaittiḍavē 
piḍittu izhuttaṉai uṉ perumai yār aṟivār).” 	 v. 70

“Thou hast possessed me, an unexorcisable ghost and 
made me mad (for Thee) that I may cease to be a (wandering) 
ghost Arunachala (pēyttaṉam viḍa viḍāp pēyāp piḍittu eṉaip 
pēyaṉ ākkiṉai).”	 v. 71

“Thou, like a deft magician stupefying with a magic 
powder the minds of people, stupefying me with the wonder 



Arunachala Akshara Mana Malai 239

powder of Thy holy Grace took away my jivabodham and 
revealed Sivabodham, (poḍiyāl mayakki eṉ bhōdattaip 
paṟittu uṉ bhōdattaik kāṭṭiṉai).” 	 v. 73

“Removing the dense darkness of mind’s delusion by 
the magic power of Thy grace, Thou hast made it into the 
Supreme Form of Thee; the content of Truth (maimayal 
nīttu aruḷ maiyiṉāl uṉadu uṇmai vacam ākkiṉai).” 	 v. 84

“Who else other than Thee, stupefying me and by stealth, 
unknown even to those who were closest to me, stole away 
my mind? (yārum aṟiyādu eṉ matiyiṉai maruṭṭi evar koḷai 
koṇḍatu).” 	 v. 89

“Thou, marking me as the target of Thy unfailing 
but extremely sharp arrow of Grace, hath released Thy 
Brahmastra on me, devouring me alive (laṭciyam vaittu aruḷ 
astiram viṭṭu eṉaip paṭcittāy pirāṇaṉōḍu).” 	 v. 92

“Deigning to trap me that I may receive Thy Grace, 
Thou like a spider, didst weave wide Thy web of Grace 
and entrapping me, devoured me alive. (cindittu aruḷpaḍac 
cilandipōl kaṭṭic ciṟaiyiṭṭu uṇḍaṉai).” 	 v. 103

The transcendent Expanse of Grace (aruḷ veḷi) is the same 
as the space of Awareness (Chidambaram or chidakasam). 
The formless form of Arunachala, namely, the non-dual 
Bliss of Pure Awareness, ever remains the summumbonum, 
the ultimate and the Supreme. Gathering him in Him the 
form of Transcendent Expanse of Divine Grace in Union (as 
one with Him) is the content of the phrase “aruḷ veḷi viḍal” 
(manifestation of Thy grace). This means “ridding me of my 
delusion of the ego, the form of ‘This body am I’, attitude 
which is nothing but the awareness of the fancied adjuncts
